---

# THE LAST LAMPBEARER

### A Novel

---

For everyone who ever tried
to remember a voice they were losing.

---

*All characters in this book are fictional. Any resemblance to real persons, living or dead, is pure coincidence — and probably a memory someone sold.*

---

## A Note on the Map

Imagine a circle of twelve glass domes on a black frozen sea.

The largest sits in the middle, where the first fire was lit. They call it Dawn Hall, the First Dome, seat of the Ashen Court. Around it, like stones thrown into water, ring the other eleven — brighter near the center, dimmer at the edge.

Between the domes there is nothing. No sun. No green. No sound but wind.

They call that nothing the Ashlands.

And if you press your ear to the glass of any dome on any night of any year, you will hear the same thing: a soft, steady crackling. That is the soulflame burning in the lantern at the heart of the city.

It is burning your past right now.

It always is.

---

# PART ONE — THE THEFT OF THE LIVING

---

## Chapter 1 · The Dying Room

The old woman was afraid of the dark, so Isolde started with the sea.

"Easy," she said. "It's only a memory."

"Only," the woman whispered from her bed. "That is a strange word for the biggest thing I own."

The room was cold. It was always cold in the dying wards of the Ninth Dome, because heat was a luxury and the dying were poor. Isolde's breath made small ghosts as she set her case on the bedside table and opened it: three glass lamps no bigger than apples, a silver funnel, a brush made of badger hair, a book of ledgers. The lamps waited, clean and empty, like cups at a feast for someone who never came.

Outside the glass walls of the dome, the world was black. It had been black for a thousand years, since the sun went out. People did not say *went out*. They said *died*, the way you say a person died — gently, and without looking up. Between the twelve domes, on long nights like this, the black pressed close and the glass groaned. But in the room there was one small light: the soulflame in the wall-bracket, burning low and blue.

Blue meant low. Amber meant it was enough.

White — Isolde had only seen white once, in the great lantern of Dawn Hall, in a picture she was not supposed to remember.

"Farewell, Maren," the woman said to the flame. Then, to Isolde: "Is it bad? They say it is like drowning."

"They say a lot of things," Isolde said. "Most of them are wrong."

She had done this four hundred and eleven times. She knew because she had written each one in her ledger, in a flat careful hand. Four hundred and eleven dying beds. Four hundred and eleven last fires. Nobody else in the Lampbearers' hall kept count, because counting made it a job instead of a sacrament, and jobs could be talked about at dinner. Isolde was not good at dinner.

"Let me tell you how it goes," she said. "You will hold this funnel, and I will ask for the memory. You give it to me freely — that matters, it has to be given. You will feel a pull, like drawing a thread from a sleeve. Then the lamp holds it, and you rest, and in the morning the flame gets its tithe, and your rent is paid, and your grandchildren's lights stay on one winter longer."

"And afterward?" said Maren.

"Afterward you won't miss it. You never miss what you don't remember."

The old woman looked at her for a long time. Her eyes were wet and sharp and entirely awake.

"You are very young to be so calm," she said.

"Calm isn't the same as not caring."

"No," Maren agreed. "It is the mask of it. I have read that somewhere. Or I did. It is going to be one of the ones you take tonight."

Isolde opened her ledger and wrote the date. She did not look up, because looking up would have been honest, and honesty made the work longer. It was a rule the Lord Examiner had taught her himself, back when the Lord Examiner was still a plain examiner with ink on his cuffs and a habit of saying *my dear* like he meant it: *You are the door, Isolde. Do not be the room on the other side of it.*

"There are three lamps," she said instead. "Small ones hold a single scene — a face, a day, a song. Medium ones hold whole years. The large one holds the shape of you, the big ones: your name, your love, the sea."

"My husband is in the small ones," Maren said. "Edevart. Thirty-one years of him. I sold the rest of him last spring to pay for the roof."

Isolde's pen stopped. It was not her business. A Lampbearer asked for the memory and carried it; she did not judge where the pieces had already gone.

"What did it cost you?" Isolde asked. "The roof."

"The war in the Sixth Dome," Maren said, and smiled a little. "What a young woman believes when she is frightened. A memory is a small price for a thing as large as that. I thought. We all think. The poor always find the cheapest way to lie to themselves and call it budgeting." She coughed, and the cough went through her like a cart over stones. "Take the sea first. I want to be brave before I am brave."

Isolde held out the funnel. It was silver, cool and smooth, shaped like a flower that had given up.

"Before you hold that thing out at me," Maren said, "tell me what I am worth."

"Madam, the hall will appraise—"

"The hall will appraise it afterward, in a room I will never be in, with a clerk who has never met me, and my grandchildren will pay whatever he decides. Say it now. Say the tariff. It is the only thing in this dome a poor person is ever told twice, and I would like to die knowing the price of my own life the way other women die knowing their prayers."

So Isolde said the tariff, because she had known it since she was nine, and because saying it in the flat voice was easier than looking at the woman it was about.

*A single pleasant day: three days of oil. A face you loved: one winter of roof, and more if the face was young. A wedding: a whole season, and the hall takes the dance and leaves you the vows, which is the sort of thing the hall does that nobody has ever been able to explain at a dinner. A child's first walk: eleven days. A death: nine, and everybody argues about it at the counter. A trade — a weaver's skill, a scriptor's hand, a lamp-maker's eye: two months, and the Second Dome has sold its weavers so many times that the Ninth now buys cloth from a dome that cannot remember how to weave it. A language: five. A song: one, and it goes badly, because there is no accounting for what a song actually did to a person and the hall will not try.*

"And the shape of me," Isolde said. "Name, parents, the whole of it. A year, in full. It is the cheapest thing we own. That should tell you what we are."

"The sea is worth more than a winter," Maren said, not unkindly, the way a woman haggles when she has haggled her whole life and has stopped being ashamed of it.

"The sea is worth eleven days of roof at the tithe rate," Isolde said. And then, because the ward was empty and the Wardens were at the far end and nobody had ever asked her this question in four hundred and eleven beds, she said the thing she had been trained not to say, in the flat professional voice of a woman reading out a length of rope she was about to be hanged with: "I am telling you that as a fact and not as a price. The sea is not on the tariff at all. There is no column for a place you have not been since you were nine and have thought about every single Thursday of your life, and the hall cannot see your face while you are giving it, so the hall will appraise it as a large scene — weather, water, animals — and that is forty days, and the difference between forty days and eleven is not cheating, it is *bookkeeping*, and I have never once said that out loud to a person in this room before, and if it gets back to the hall I will be moved to the Undercroft and will deserve it."

The old woman looked at her for a long moment with her wet, sharp, entirely awake eyes.

"Well," Maren said. "That is the first honest thing anybody has said to me in this dome in thirty years, and it cost you something, and I will not have it cost you a second time — so write down that I haggled you and you beat me, and take the sea at the hall's own number like a proper rite, and be brave before you are brave."

"One more thing," Isolde said, and she heard herself say it. "Keep one. Something nobody would ever buy. There is no market in it, so there is no rent on it. A soup. A Thursday. Something small and useless and *yours*."

"And who taught *you* that?"

"Nobody," Isolde said. "I have never seen it done."

"Your hands," Maren said. "Let me see your hands."

Isolde looked up.

"They have no ring," the old woman said. "And no scars. You hold a dying person's whole soul in them every week and they have no ring and no scars. What are you, child?"

"Your Lampbearer," Isolde said. "I have no time to be anything else."

"You will."

She gave the sea first, the way she said. Isolde watched her face as it left: not a drowning, no. A slackening. Like someone unclenching a fist they had held up for eighty years. The funnel frosted over, a fern of white spreading down its stem, and the small lamp filled with a color that had no name in the Ninth Dome — a deep, moving, salt-light blue. The room smelled, for one second only, of wet stone and fish-gut and sunburn.

That was the thing nobody told you about the soulflame. It didn't just burn.

It remembered on your behalf.

When the smell went, Maren's face was blank and peaceful and eighty years older. "What was that," she said. Not a question.

"Your tithe," Isolde said, and her voice came out flatter than she wanted. *Door, not room.* "The second one is your husband. Edevart. You can take your time."

But the door at the ward's far end opened before the old woman spoke, and Isolde heard boots — heavy ones, several pairs — before she saw them. Not the Order. The Order shuffled. These boots walked like they owned the floor, and there was a gray coat at the front that Isolde knew better than her own handwriting.

She closed her case.

"Come back," Maren said, reaching out a hand with surprising grip. "Come back, girl, I haven't given you the sea yet—"

But it was already in the lamp, and the woman who remembered it was already gone, and Isolde could not say that out loud either, because of the door and the room on the other side of it.

The gray coat stopped in the doorway. Behind it stood two Wardens and a clerk with his ledger open.

"Rest, citizen," the Lord Examiner said, to the room, to the dying woman, to everyone, in the voice he used when he wanted a room to feel held rather than watched. "Our work frightens the well and comforts the sick. I never know which is worse."

"Lord Examiner," Isolde said. She was still sitting on the bed with a stranger's sea in her lap. She stood up too fast. "The harvest is nearly done. She has one lamp left to give. I can be finished in five minutes."

"Finish tomorrow." Corvin Aelfric did not raise his voice; his voice never needed it. It arrived at your ears the way weather arrives — from above, impersonal, impossible to argue with. "I need you elsewhere tonight, my dear. There is a death in the Undercroft, and the soulflame wants it before morning."

Isolde felt something let go in her chest that she did not know she had been holding. The Undercroft. Nobody went to the Undercroft to die. It was below the Ninth Dome, below the foundations, a warren of old rooms where the Court kept its quiet cases — dissidents, deserters, monks who read the wrong histories. Deaths from the Undercroft did not come with families. They came with clerks, and their memories went to the great lantern in Dawn Hall and never touched their grandchildren's rent, and no ledger in the world recorded it.

"I see," she said.

"You see nothing," Corvin said, kindly, and it was almost a joke, and that was the worst thing about him. "Which is why you are the finest Lampbearer in nine domes. Bring the large lamp. The one with the shape of them." He turned to the door, then stopped, and turned back, and looked at old Maren in her bed — at the blankness of her, and the small blue lamp glowing in Isolde's case like a held breath.

"Thirty-one years of him," he murmured, more to himself than to her. "She gave the husband to the flame. And the sea. And all of it for one winter of roof." He looked at Isolde. "You know what the poor sell, in the end? Not memories. Time. They hand us tomorrow, packed up to look like yesterday, and call it paying their way."

"Then why do we take it?" Isolde asked. She did not know why she asked. The room was very quiet. Even the clerk stopped writing.

Corvin smiled the way he did when a student said something almost right, which was the most dangerous almost in the world.

"Because the light asks nothing of us," he said, "except that we ask it of everyone else." He buttoned his coat against the cold. "Twenty minutes, my dear. After tonight, you will be glad of a new case."

The boots went. The door closed. The Wardens stayed, because the Wardens always stayed.

And Isolde Vane, in the eleven seconds it took the Wardens to get bored of a dying ward, did the first unprofessional thing of a seventeen-year career, and she did it with her pen, because it was the only hand she had that knew how to move.

In the ledger, under *Maren, N., Ninth, ward, sea given, tithe taken*, she wrote the four words the rite required. In the margin — the blank hand's-breadth of paper at the edge of every ledger page that the hall leaves empty on purpose, so a Lampbearer has nowhere to put anything — she wrote, in a small fast hand she then sat on until it dried:

*The Examiner's count is wrong by one lamp. The husband has not been given. The roof was paid for by the sea and not by him. Nobody has corrected him and I am the one who is going to have to decide whether that is an accident about her or a fact about him.*

She had never written a thing in a margin in her life. There was no column for it, and the trade does not print a thing it has no column for, and when you think about that hard enough — runners, this is the item this whole book is going to spend nine domes and one winter learning the answer to — you begin to see the shape of the machine that made the word *cheapest* sit in a sentence about a human being and nobody flinch.

Isolde sat back down on the edge of the bed, and for one bad second she was going to tell the truth, the whole stupid honest truth, and Maren would hear it, and the night would change, and—

"Girl," said the old woman, sharp as a knife through paper. Her eyes were back. Not dim. Back.

Isolde froze.

"Girl, I said come back and you came back, so listen, since you are the only one in this room with hands that don't shake." A cough, a war, the small blue lamp. "There is something I gave away a long time ago, and my grandchildren cannot sell it because I do not have it to give. So you will take it, and it will go into the flame, and it will be paid for, and it will matter, because you will carry it out of this room like it is nothing, and someone will make a note."

"Lampbearer isn't—" Isolde began.

"I know what you aren't," Maren said. "I have read you. Now hold out your cursed funnel and be brave before you are brave."

Isolde held out the funnel. She did not know until afterward what Maren gave her — some old thing, some small impossible bright piece of a life — but she felt it frost the silver, and the ward smelled for one second of something that had no right to smell anywhere in the Ninth Dome.

Grass. Wet, cut, ridiculous grass.

*Item — she did not join the two things together for two days, and when she did she was on a service stair with her coat off and her hands in her lap, and joining them cost her the rest of her life as a woman who filed things where they belonged: the old woman had not ignored the advice. She had done the only other thing there is to do with a thing nobody will buy, which is to put it into the hands of somebody who will.*

Then the boots were back in the hall and the soulflame in the wall burned blue, and Isolde closed her case with a stranger's sea rattling in her lap, and followed it down toward the Undercroft, and the dark, and the death with no family, and the lamp that wanted the shape of someone, before morning.

---

## Chapter 2 · The Monk Who Wasn't Dying

The Undercroft did not smell like a hospital. Hospitals smelled of boiled linen and fear, which were at least honest. The Undercroft smelled of stone that had never been warm, and of candles burned down to stubs and left there on purpose.

Isolde followed the gray coat down three spiral stairs, past a door with no handle, past a window that looked into a room where nobody was sitting. Corvin walked like a man late for something pleasant. Behind them, the clerk had stopped writing. The clerk had learned, apparently, that some pages were not for ledgers.

"The Order of the Book," Corvin said, without turning around. "You know them by sight?"

"Scriptors. Archivists," Isolde said. "They copy histories in the Sixth Dome. I've harvested two of theirs. Old ones, natural deaths, families on the stairs." She paused. "Families on the stairs."

"This one is Brother Assur. Sixty-one. A heart that has been failing politely his whole life and has finally stopped being polite. He is two days from the end. The soulflame in the Ninth is blue, my dear. You know what blue does to a dome by spring."

"I know what blue does," Isolde said. She did not know what any of it had to do with an archivist, and she did not ask, because asking down here felt like knocking on walls.

They came at last to a low room with a bed in it, and the bed had a monk in it, and the monk — this was the first wrong thing — was sitting up, and awake, and *expecting them*. He was small and brown as a pipe root, with a shaved crown and wild white brows, and on the blanket in front of him lay an open book with nothing written in it.

"Ah," he said, as if he had won a wager. "The tall one, and then the girl with the case. You are early. That is kindness."

Corvin's hand rested on Isolde's shoulder — not comfort, exactly. A hand placed where a hand could be seen. "Brother. This is Lampbearer Vane. She will take your tithe tonight, if you are willing. Freely given, as the rite demands."

"Freely," said the monk, and looked at Isolde with an attention that made her want to straighten her coat. "Willingly. Joyfully. There are three of them in the room and only one of me, and I have decided against the tall one and the clerk, which leaves the girl. Very well. Girl, are you going to do this standing, or are you going to sit down like your grandmother did?"

"My grandmother," said Isolde, "died in the Seventh Dome with her whole life intact, which I am told is rare."

"It is rare. It is also, between us, a bit selfish." He patted the bed's edge. "Come. The heart will do its failing in its own time. It has never consulted me, and I have learned not to consult it. But the memories — *those* I own, and I will tell you which are which, because someone should, and tonight that someone is me."

So Isolde knelt by the bed and opened her case, and the strangest harvest she had ever done began.

It was strange in the way that only calm things are strange. Four hundred and eleven times, she had met fear, or bargaining, or the dull animal relief of a pain finally being taken away. Brother Assur met her like a host meets a guest at the door of a house he is selling: regretting nothing, explaining everything, wanting her to know what each room had been for.

"This one," he said, offering up the first small lamp, "is the winter I learned to read. Give it to the flame and you will light, for one night, a room in which a boy sounds out words by tallow and does not yet know he is going to be lonely for forty years about it. It is a good fire. It burns warm in the middle."

Isolde took it. The funnel frosted. It was — she had no other word for it — *tidy*. Most memories came away ragged, edges fraying, a bit of everything near them stuck to them like burrs. This one came like a folded sheet.

"And this," he said, "is my ordination. And this is the taste of the first pear I ever stole, which I have kept for fifty years out of pure admiration for the crime. Take it freely, I beg you, it deserves to burn."

She wanted — this was the second wrong thing — to *keep* them. Not in the professional way, the way she kept the dying rooms in her head like exhibits. She wanted them the way you want a stranger's photograph because the stranger's face is your own. The pear made her mouth water for a fruit she had never eaten. The reading-room lit something behind her eyes she had never known was dark.

"Lord Examiner," she said, without looking up. "This is unusual."

"Nothing here is unusual," said Corvin, from the doorway, pleasant as weather. "The Order teaches acceptance. The man is dying; he has made his peace; he gives his tithe with joy. Would you prefer he scream, my dear? Screaming taints the flame."

"I would prefer he be wrong about his heart," Isolde said. "He's too strong. He should be under the sheets, not over them. Have you had a leech look at him? If this is a failing heart, it is failing like a man pretending to be shot."

Silence. The kind of silence that has furniture in it.

Isolde became aware, all at once, that the clerk had stopped writing some time ago, and that Corvin's hand was no longer on her shoulder, and that the monk was watching her with an expression of the most naked, disgraceful, delighted *hope*.

"There is one left," Brother Assur said quietly, "that I have not offered. The large one. The shape of me." He looked at her steadily. "Girl — may I call you girl? You have clever hands and a confused past. What is your earliest memory?"

Isolde's pen, which had been moving, stopped. "The smell of the Lampbearers' hall," she said. "Boiled linen. I was — they told me I was seven. I came in with fever and no name and they took me in."

"Seven," said the monk. "A tidy number. A date, a door, no threshold. You have never stood on the step and looked back."

"No," said Isolde, and heard how she said it, and hated that she heard it.

"Then take my shape," Brother Assur said. "Freely, fully, as the rite demands. But before you do — I am going to ask you one favor, and if the Lord Examiner strikes me dumb for it, I will die of that instead of the heart and accuse him from the pulpit." His wild brows drew together, and his voice changed, and the room changed with it, the way a room changes when someone lights a lamp in the corner you hadn't looked at. "In the shape of me there is one small memory I have never given to anyone. It is the one thing worth burning. When you hold my memories in your case tonight, on the road, you will feel it, and it will feel like — how shall I put it — like a key you have never seen but whose lock you *know*. Do not put it back. Do not report it. Keep it."

"Brother," said Isolde, "that is not how the rite—"

"The rite," said Corvin, from the doorway, still pleasant, still weather, "requires that the Lampbearer carry all memories given, unopened, unkept, unreported, to the tithe-house by dawn. Brother Assur, you are two days from the end and I do not think you are going to make them a rule." His eyes were on Isolde, though. On her hands, which were resting on the monk's blanket like anything else in the world, which they were not. "Keep nothing, my dear," he said, kindly. "You are so good at giving things back."

And then the harvest of the shape, which Isolde did not remember afterward as one act but as three. The pull — a whole life coming like thread from a sleeve, years of it, the ordination and the pear and the reading-room and forty lonely years and the names of everyone he had ever copied a book for. The weight of the lamp in her case afterward, the large one, glowing like a held breath. And the last thing the monk said, as the blankness came over his face — not peaceful, this time, but *satisfied*, like a man who has paid a debt and intends to hear about it:

"You will see me again, Lampbearer. Not in a bed."

She asked, on the stairs, because her legs asked for her: "Not in a bed? He gave me his *shape*. He won't know his own name by morning. That's what it means. That's what I do."

"Precisely," said Corvin, ahead of her, one hand on the spiral rail. "Which is why the Court is generous with the Undercroft deaths. Nobody comes to the stairs. There is no family to explain it to, no grave to visit, no name to call out. The flame gets its tithe and the ledger gets its line, and the poor man gets the one thing the dying always get."

"What's that?" Isolde said, and heard, three steps behind them, the clerk begin to write again.

"Quiet," said Corvin, and went up the stairs into the blue night, leaving her on the step and looking back.

She kept nothing. She must have kept nothing. The tithe-house took the three lamps at dawn with the flat, bored ritual of a post office, and she signed the ledger — *Assur, B., Ninth, Undercroft, shape given, tithe taken* — and walked home through the market rows with her case empty and her hands in their strange unscarred way in her pockets, and did not look at any face in the crowd, because that would have been — what? What would it have been? She was a door. Doors do not look.

That was the theory.

The theory lasted nine days.

Nine days is a strange unit of time in a dome, because a dome does not have days; it has bells, and there are four of them, and between the bells there is work. So those nine days of Isolde Vane's life arrived in the record exactly the way they arrived in her body: as thirty-six appointments, forty-one ward lists, and one hundred and nine names she said out loud in the flat voice at bedsides, and — this is the item she could never afterward get the Court's own ledger to admit — *eleven harvests*.

Eleven. She counted them on the third morning with her hands inside two bowls of warm water the way the hall liked them, because the fingers work better warm, and she counted them again on the fifth, hoping for a different number, and got the same one, and it was the same one because the Ninth does not slow down for a woman who has started noticing, and there is no position in nine domes that exists which slows down for that.

She did all eleven correctly. That is the part nobody in the Ashlands ever believed when she told it, on a stair, eleven weeks later, in the flat voice, at a bedside, in her own defense: she did every one of them *properly*, funnel and wedge and the palm pressed where the pulse is and the four words and the naming and the counting into the glass and the signature after, and not one of the eleven could she have performed better, and she remembered every single one of them, and had not remembered any of them in seventeen years.

The first was a bracket-keeper of the Second with forty years of tube-work in him, and the shape came up like a knot coming out of a rope, and she had it in her fist before she had decided to take it, and what she thought — *what she thought*, runners, in the rite, with her hand on a dying man's wrist and the funnel in the tin and the two Wardens at the door with their arms folded, the way they always stood — was: *he haggled for turnips once, in the market, and laughed at the wrong moment, and it was the only good minute in my whole month, and I am taking the rest of him*. She finished it. She wrote it. She walked out and stood in the stairwell for eleven minutes with her hands on the cold rail until the Wardens came up to ask if she was ill.

The fourth was a woman with no family in the room, and the rite went so well that the under-warden complimented her afterwards, and Isolde wrote in the margin of her own slate, in her own flat hand, where no audit could reach it for three years: *Item — this one was brave. Item — I did not tell her that*.

The seventh was a boy of nine years, in a ward of the low rows, and his shape was very small and mostly about a hoop, a stick, and the rain, and it fed a lamp, and this is where the nine days stop being a description and start being a fact you have to carry out of the chapter with you, runners, because nothing happens in that room that anyone does anything about. Nobody screamed. Nobody was cruel. The mother was given the standard cup of hot water and the four words, and the schedule was correct, and the count was filed, and the tithe was *given*, and Isolde Vane put the small brown lamp in the case's second compartment with the three other lamps of that day, and it was warm all the way to the tithe-house, and the warmth was the worst thing that had ever happened to her, and there was, in nine domes, in that entire minute, nobody whoever you would have asked, who could have done any of it differently.

That is the sentence the whole book is going to spend twenty-three chapters arguing with, and I want it filed plainly here at the ninth day, before the market square and the turnips and the haggler's grin, because if you do not understand that the trade was not run by monsters, you will not understand what she had to do about it, and neither did she: *the horror of the Ninth Dome on an ordinary morning is that every single person in the room was behaving correctly.*

On the tenth, in the full light of a blue morning, Isolde Vane stood in the middle of the Ninth Dome's market square with her case on her arm and watched Brother Assur — shaved crown, wild white brows, buying *turnips* with a haggler's grin and a stranger's steady hand — walk right past her, alive, and not dying, and not, she would learn later, harvested at all.

---

## Chapter 3 · The Thing in Her Pocket

She followed him because doors do not look, but Lampbearers, it turned out, could walk.

Brother Assur moved through the market rows like a man who had done market ten thousand times and meant to do it ten thousand more. He haggled for turnips. He haggled for a length of coarse cloth, holding it up to the light, squeezing it, insulting it, making the merchant laugh. He stopped at the lamp-posts to warm his hands over the blue flame-vents the way everyone did, briefly, guiltily, because warmth was expensive and nobody watched it closely enough to call it theft.

He did not look blank. That was the thing Isolde kept turning over as she trailed him two rows back. She had seen the drained — everyone had, in the wards, in the low rows, in the sad little colonies by the outer glass where the Hollowed sometimes stood at night, facing the dark, because nobody had ever told them they were supposed to face the light. The drained were empty houses with the doors open. Assur was a full one. The haggler's grin was not a leftover. It was *in use*.

At one point the monk stopped to give a thing away, and Isolde watched him do it and could not make it fit into any column she owned.

There was a woman on the market steps with a cart that had come apart at the axle and one child on each side of it, and the crowd went around the cart the way a crowd goes around anything that costs money to stop at. Assur stopped. Assur put his red-knuckled monk's hand — useless as an engineer's — under the axle and did not lift it, he *leaned* on it, and said something so plainly funny that the older child laughed, which is a thing that has never once happened in the low rows on a bad day; and then the man from the cloth stall came over and fixed the axle, because a monk cannot fix an axle and a market cannot watch a stranger fail at one for longer than a minute.

He had no money. She checked; she had spent seventeen years learning to check. There was nothing in that coat worth a turnip, and what he gave the woman on his way out of the market — she was close enough to see it, and she stood there afterward a long time — was the turnips. The whole brown bag of them. The dinner he had haggled for twice, out of pure love of haggling.

And Isolde Vane stood two rows back in the market with a Lampbearer's gray-blue on her shoulders and thought it, in the flat professional way that was the worst part of her: *that is a harvested man. That is a tithe given, filed in my name, walking around with a bag of turnips in it.* And then the thought underneath the thought came, the one they had been training out of her since she was seven with a funnel in her fist, and she let it come, because she was two days from not being able to stop letting it come: *what did they take off him, if all of this is what was left?*

He turned down Candlemaker's Row, where the shops gave out and the halls began, and he turned up a stairway Isolde had used twice in her life, for the two things she liked least: the rent office and the public baths. At the top of the stairs he stopped at the little window where the attendant sat — and this was the part she would replay later, fitting it together like a clerk fits a ledger — *showed him a token*. Not money. Something small and dark. The attendant looked at it and did the arithmetic and went the color of old tallow, and waved him through without a word.

Isolde went up the same stairs four minutes later and leaned on the little window.

"That monk," she said. "The one you just waved. What did he show you?"

The attendant looked at her coat — the Lampbearer's gray-blue, the good wool, the one the hall issued — and his mouth closed on whatever he had been about to say. "Nothing, citizen. Passes are examined for the repairs. The upper gallery is rot-eaten, we're checking weight. Nothing else."

"Weight," Isolde said.

"Would you like a basin, Lampbearer? The baths are—"

She left. On the stair down, she was doing what she had trained herself never to do, which was *think past the rite*, and thinking past it looked like this: the Court drains its enemies and calls them deaths; the enemies, somehow, come back full; and the one man she had watched come back had spent his last hour on earth handing her a life so tidy it came away like a folded sheet.

Memories, she knew better than anyone in nine domes, do not fold themselves.

Someone had folded that one. Someone had folded *all* of them, and packed them for her case, and written *tithe given* across a man walking on two legs, and she had signed the ledger with her own flat careful hand.

She went to the tithe-house that afternoon, which no Lampbearer ever did unless something had gone wrong, and the old clerk on the desk — Harb, who had been sorting there longer than Isolde had been alive — actually went quiet and uncomfortable in a way that made her want to sit down.

"The Undercroft line," she said, keeping her voice flat, professional, a door. "Assur, B. Third of Coldmonth. It was filed in my name at dawn."

"It was filed," Harb said.

"Filed where? The great lantern doesn't take the Ninth's tithes. That's law since the Charter. Small flames for small domes; that's what keeps the poor from rioting, that's what—" she stopped. She knew what it kept. It kept the poor believing that what fed their light was their own neighbors' leftovers, not their own stolen futures. "Where did the lamp go, Harb."

Harb looked at the ceiling, the floor, and the particular middle distance of a clerk deciding how much of his pension a lie was worth. "It was flagged *Dawn-hold* before I saw it," he said quietly. "Clerk's seal, Lord Examiner's counter-sign. That means it travels to the First. That means it doesn't come back to me. I told you what I know, Lampbearer, because you're the one who signed it, and when they re-tithe—" he caught himself. "When anything like this happens again, it will be your signature in the way."

"Say that again," Isolde said. "*Re-tithe*?"

But Harb had reached the end of his day and his courage at the same moment, and he began sorting lamps with the focused love of a man arranging stones on a wall, and she let him, because he had already said the thing. He had said *when*. Not *if*.

She walked home through the darkening rows with that word in her like a swallowed splinter. *Re-tithe*. As if a man were an orchard. As if Brother Assur's second harvest were scheduled.

The road home went past the outer glass, and she took it that way, which she did not usually do, and it is on that road that this book first shows its readers the thing that nine domes agree not to look at, runners, so here it is in plain words: the Hollowed keep themselves by the outer glass because the glass is the coldest place in a dome and they are not cold, and a colony of them at night is the sight every child in the low rows is taught to walk past, and Isolde had walked past one four times a day for seventeen years.

There were eleven of them that night, in the blue hour, in the short yard between the glass and the last row of houses. Nobody faced the light. That is the whole description and the whole horror of it: eleven people in coats, standing in a rank with their backs to the lamps, looking out at the dark, *patient* — not shuffling, not speaking, not dying. The children of the low rows had learned the rank from their parents, which is to say they had learned it exactly wrong, and so they threw small stones at it and ran, and the rank did not turn, and one of the eleven had a hand gone up, palm out, in the manner of a woman being asked a question she can no longer answer.

Isolde stopped. She was the only person in the yard who was not a child. She stood with her case on her arm and looked along the rank, level, professionally, the way she read a ward list, and filed the arithmetic of it in a column she did not know the name of, and this is the item she did not understand for eight more chapters, which is the way with the true ones: *eleven (11) Hollowed, outer glass, Ninth Dome, unnumbered in any census; keeping no shape; owing no tithe; entitled to no light — and standing, every one of them, facing the dark like people waiting for a cart.*

And then she did at the end the small useless professional thing she had done four hundred and eleven times and would do a great many more: she walked into the rank and put a drained woman's hood up against the wind, and laid her own scarf over a pair of hands that were not going to be cold, and said *there is a roof at the hall, citizen*, in the flat voice, to nobody who could hear her, and walked on.

She did not notice — did not for six weeks notice, and by then it did not matter, because by then she was a different trade's problem — that as she went, the drained woman's hands came up out of the coat and closed on her wrist. *Palm to wrist*. And held, for one second, the grip a person uses on a hand that has been asked a question and has answered it wrong.

The drained do not touch anybody. Nobody in the yard saw it, and the yard was full. The wall of the Ninth saw it, and the walls always do, and out here, runners, that is what a witness is.

It was in her room, in the small hours, in the cold she could not afford to fix, that she found the fourth lamp.

Her case stood open on the table where she had set it to air — three compartments, three nests of fitted wool, and in the fourth, the little wedge by the latch that had been empty for as long as she had owned the case, something was sitting. A small lamp. No bigger than an apple. Frosted at the seams with the white rime that meant it was *full*, and warm — she noticed that second, noticing it with a shock that went all the way down her arms — warm, the way a hand is warm.

She did not remember taking it. That was the lie her professional self was already constructing, neat as a folded sheet: she had picked it up in the confusion of the market, she had meant to report it, she would report it in the morning. She was still holding it when her own lie went out to meet her and she heard it for what it was. Nobody who had not taken a memory could hold one. Rime did not form on empty glass. Something in her hand had a life in it, and it had come from the Undercroft, from a small brown monk with wild white brows who had said *you will feel it on the road, and you will know the lock*.

*Do not put it back. Do not report it. Keep it.*

Corvin's voice answering it, soft as weather: *Keep nothing, my dear. You are so good at giving things back.*

Isolde sat on the edge of her bed in the dark dome and turned the small warm lamp in her hands, and did the thing the rite forbade above all others, the one that had been beaten into her since she was seven years old with a funnel in her fist: she undid the stopper, and breathed it in.

A room. A low room with a cracked ceiling and a stone of a different age in the wall — a stone with a mark cut into it, a lamp with one line through it. A woman's hands in her hair, quick hands, clever hands, braiding and pretending not to. A smell she had spent seventeen years failing to place, arriving all at once and total: wet, cut, ridiculous *grass*.

And the woman's voice, close enough to be heard over her shoulder, laughing while it said: "*Isolde, my dawn-daughter. There are things they will come for. When they come — and they always come — I want you to know they will take the sea, they will take the years, they will take even this. So I keep one for you where harvesters can't reach.*" A kiss on the top of her head. "*Not in your head. In your hands. Watch your hands, little door. They'll open one day for you.*"

Then the room shook with a sound like a bell being dragged, and the woman's hands stopped moving in her hair, and the memory ended mid-breath, because memories of that kind do not end; they are *taken*.

Isolde sat in her dark room with an empty lamp in her hands and grass-smell in her hair that was not there anymore, and the shape of the truth settled over her like weather, and this was the shape of it:

She had a mother. Her mother had known harvesters. Her mother had hidden a memory in her in a way she could not reach — and some night, when Isolde was seven, someone had come with a bell and a funnel and a ledger, and taken everything, and left the door on its hinges, and *filed it*.

Down in the market square, in the blue before dawn, a monk with wild white brows was finishing his turnips. And in the Undercroft, in a room with no handle on the door, a clerk was writing a schedule with a pen that did not stop for anything, and the second line from the top said *Assur, B.*

---

## Chapter 4 · The People Behind the Lamp

She went back to the baths at the bad hour, the hour the Ninth Dome pretended to be the night it had never had, and she did not go as a Lampbearer. She left the good coat hanging, wore her own mended one, carried her case empty the way repair-folk carry theirs, and told the attendant at the window that the upper gallery was rot-eaten and she'd been sent to check the weight.

The attendant looked at her the way people look at weather they cannot file a complaint against, and waved her through.

The gallery was not rot-eaten. That had been the first lie in the morning, offered to her on a stairway like a gift, and she had taken it home and turned it over all day, the way she turned over everything, until its real shape came out: *it is not the gallery that is checked for weight.*

The far end of the gallery had a maintenance door. Past the maintenance door, stairs — down, not up, which was the tell, because galleries that needed weight-checks did not live on top of things. The stairs were worn in the middle the way only truly secret stairs are worn, by feet that had made the same trip in the same dark for years. At the bottom there was a passage, and on the passage's inner wall, cut into a stone of a different age from any stone Isolde had ever seen in the Ninth, there was a mark.

A lamp, with one line through it.

She stood in front of it a long time. Her hands had begun doing something she had not authorized — shaking — because she had breathed that mark three nights ago in a dead woman's memory, in a low room with cracked ceiling, in the smell of grass, and dead women's marks do not wait politely in cellars.

The passage opened into a room that had been a boiler hall and was now, apparently, a church.

There were forty of them, maybe more, sitting on benches around a single amber soulflame in a floor-lamp, which was illegal in six ways and, Isolde realized while her professional mind catalogued the crime, *enough*. Amber. Warm light. The light of a family parlor, set down in a cellar among people who had probably not seen amber since childhood. On a low table at the flame's foot sat an open lockbox lined in wool, and the box held lamps — dozens — small ones, medium ones, all frosted white at the seams, all *full*, and nobody was looking at them the way the dying look at tithe. They were looking at them the way the hungry look at bread that is going to be *saved*, not eaten.

"So," said Brother Assur, from the bench directly in front of her, not turning around, in the voice of a man who had won a second wager. "You found the stairs faster than the last one. Sit down, girl, before you faint in the doorway like a tourist."

She did not sit. "You put that in my case," she said. "At the tithe-house, or before. There is no other way, the seal—"

"There are eleven ways," Assur said, turning now, his wild brows full of sleepless, fond amusement. "I used the fourth. You would have found the third on your own in about a week, which is why we chose you and not the week. Sit down."

She sat. Forty faces looked at her — low-row faces, market faces, scriptor's faces, two Wardens' faces that made her hands go cold — and at the back, an unaging woman with a burn scar climbing her neck like ivy stood with her arms folded and said, first thing, in a voice like a locked door:

"Lampbearer. Say what you are doing here and say it fast, because if the answer is *we* I am going to have to be the kind of person I train other people not to be."

"My name is Isolde Vane," said Isolde. "I harvest the dying for the Court. I took a monk's memories and the monk bought turnips. I found a fourth lamp in my case that I don't remember taking, and I opened it, which is the worst thing a Lampbearer can do, and inside it was my mother." She said it level, flat, the way she read names off beds. It came out anyway like something else. "So I am here because there is a mark on your door that was on her wall, and I am told by a dead woman I should watch my hands, and—" she looked at the lockbox, the amber flame, the forty faces, all of it arriving into her with the awful clarity of a diagnosis "— you people are *keeping* them. That's not possible. Memory isn't currency you can save, it's fuel, it all gets tithe-collected, there's no vault, there's no—"

"There is no vault," the scarred woman agreed. "Correct. There is no way to hide a full lamp from a census, from a search, from the Court's own pickers. Correct. What you are looking at is not a vault, Lampbearer. It's a *library*. Every lamp in that box was emptied the legal month before the census, into the flame, as the law demands — and what the law does not say, because the law was written by people who never touched a funnel, is what a memory *is*, once it burns."

Assur put a finger to his lips and began to speak over them both, not loudly, in the rhythm of a man giving a sermon he had rehearsed in the bathhouse the entire time he was pretending to die:

"The flame burns a memory, yes, and the light comes of it, and the rent is paid, and all the ledgers close and everyone forgets, which is the Court's favorite word and the reason they print it nine hundred times a year. But a memory does not *go* when it burns, girl. It goes *into*. Into the light, into the glass the light touches, into the walls that hold the glow. The dome remembers what you feed it. The stone remembers. Water remembers, if it's old water; wood remembers, if it's bad wood. And *hands*—" he tapped the table, once, and forty people looked at their own hands like birds at a worm "— hands remember best of all, because the flame goes through them every night of every life, in every lamp that is carried, in every bracket that is trimmed, and a full lamp pressed flat against a trained palm, in a dark room, at the right hour, gives up something. Not the memory. Not whole, never whole. But a *shape*. A weather. A smell. A name you didn't know you'd lost."

Isolde looked at her own hands, which were lying open in her lap like a confessed crime.

"You're the Unlit," she said. Nobody said it out loud in the Ninth. Even Corvin said *the disturbed*, the way you say *weather*, when the Court's riders came.

"We are *un*-lit, exactly, in the sense the word was invented by the Court to mean 'people who refuse the tithe and live in the dark like animals,' which we do not, as you can see, there is an amber in the room, the woman holding the door-lock is one of six in this dome who can trim a light like a proper clerk—" the scarred woman cut in smoothly "— and in the other sense, the one we chose for ourselves: *the Unlit are the people the flame keeps.* We are the ones who learned to read the walls, Lampbearer. And what the walls of this dome say, if you press your ear to them at the hour the great lantern in Dawn Hall takes its tithe, is a thing the Court has killed eleven hundred people for not overhearing." She paused. "The dead walk. You have met one. How many has your street seen?"

"Two," Isolde said. "In my whole life, on census day, my street sees two old ones and everybody looks away because looking away is how you keep your lights."

"On census day the whole of the Ninth sees forty," the woman said. "Census day is when the Court moves the walkers home, and they cannot be seen in the squares, so they are seen in the streets, and every mother in this dome teaches her child *don't stare*, and every child grows up knowing. That is your tithe, Lampbearer, not the memories — the *not looking.* They take our past and we pay them in the coin of pretending not to notice it's gone. You've noticed."

"I noticed," said Isolde. "And now you'll notice I'm going home, and my hands will forget this room—"

"No," the scarred woman said, and the room's temperature went down with her, and Isolde found that she did not want to argue, in the way that she had not wanted to argue with Corvin, except that this was different, because Corvin's danger was weather and this woman's danger was *fireplace*, close, and about her own. "That is what the Court would say, and the Court would be right to say it, and it is why we are not the Court, and it is why Brother Assur has been rehearsing a death for someone like you since before you were born, as a matter of policy. You are not going home, Lampbearer. You were never going to be. When you opened that lamp you walked into the only room in this dome you cannot file a complaint about, which is your own life, and we have been keeping your mother's shape in our walls for seventeen years, and tonight—" she glanced at Assur, who had gone very still, and then at the flame "— tonight we have to show you the walls fast, because your Lord Examiner's schedule crossed the river an hour ago, and its second line is *re-tithe, Assur, B.*, and its fourth line is *watch Vane, I.*"

The single amber in the room crackled, which lights do not do.

"They folded my harvest for me," Isolde said, because her voice had left without her. "The tidy life. The pear, the reading-room. I signed the ledger."

"You signed the ledger," Assur said gently, "with a stranger's sea in your case and your mother's grass in your nose, and you have been honest your entire professional life, and it is the only reason in the world this room exists tonight instead of a hole in the floor under you." He stood, small and brown and very much not dying, and put his hands on her shoulders, and said, to the room, in the voice she would have sworn a harvester had taught him: "She is the one. The door that opened itself. Keep the flame trimmed and stand behind me, all of you, because we are going to walk a Lampbearer home through the low rows at the bad hour, and if the Wardens ask, she is drunk, and if the Court asks, she is ours, and if *he* asks—"

The door of the boiler hall opened before he finished.

Not the passage door. The *other* one, the one nobody in the room looked at, the one that opened on a stairway going up — and up was impossible, up was the maintenance door she had just come down, there had been an attendant at that window, there had been a *gallery*, and behind the open door, in the amber light, in a gray coat that arrived into rooms the way weather does, stood Corvin Aelfric, smiling the way he smiled at students who had said something almost right.

"Brother," he said, with real pleasure. "You were supposed to be in a bed."

"Young man," said Brother Assur, not moving from behind Isolde's shoulders, "you are three days early for a funeral. The old man hasn't finished his turnips."

And the two of them — the Lord Examiner of nine domes and a small brown monk with wild white brows, in the doorway and the bench of a room full of stolen walls — stood and looked at each other with the naked, mutual, enormous *fondness* of two men who had been, a very long time ago, in some other room, at some other lamp, on the same side of a funnel, and Isolde Vane understood, in the worst way one understands anything, that she had walked into the fourth wedge of a thing that had been packed for her before she was born.

"Nobody move," the Lord Examiner said pleasantly, "while the only honest thing in this building happens, which is that I count you."

---

## Chapter 5 · What the Room Was Worth

He counted them.

That was the most frightening thing anyone had ever done in Isolde's hearing. He did not shout, did not call the Wardens, did not touch the lockbox or the flame or the forty-one terrified people in it. He stood in the doorway with his hands folded in his sleeves, the way he stood in the great examinations at the hall, and he counted, out loud, in his pleasant weather-voice, and he counted the *room*, not just the people — the benches, the wool-lined box, the two exits, the water pipe, the crack in the east wall.

"Forty-one," he said at last. "There were thirty-four of you in my time, and the crack was not in that wall, and the flame was green then. It is amber now. Someone has been feeding it better than the law allows." He said it the way a doctor says *the patient is stronger than expected*, and forty-one people did not know whether to be relieved or more afraid, and being unable to decide, they chose the one available: they kept very still.

Corvin came in. That was worse. Coming in was a decision; standing in the doorway had been only weather. He walked to the floor-lamp and looked down into it, and the amber lit his face from underneath the way it lit the dying, and Isolde saw, for the first time in her life, her mentor look *old*. Not aged. Old. There is a difference, and she would have to explain it later, to herself, in a dark room, like the difference between a lamp burning low and a lamp that has forgotten it was ever full.

"The Lord Examiner counts," he said quietly, still looking into the flame, "because the Court sends its riders by schedules, and schedules are made by clerks, and clerks write what they are told. I have been told, for twenty-two years, to count rooms like this one. Do you know what I have done with the counts, each time?" He turned from the lamp. "Nothing. Eleven rooms. Four hundred and twelve souls. I have written *none*, and burned *none*, and the Court has never once asked me why, because the Court cannot imagine a servant who would disobey it out of *fondness*." He smiled at Assur, and the smile was real, and the realness of it was the most dangerous thing in the room. "You taught me that word, Brother. In a cell like this one, in the Sixth, when you had hair and I had opinions. I have kept forty-one people alive for twenty-two years on the strength of it, and not one of them has ever thanked me, and I want it on the record that I find that *correct*. The Unlit do not thank. The Unlit *persist*. That is the whole of the bargain."

Silence. Then the scarred woman's voice, low, from the back, and it shook Isolde to hear one of the Unlit speak to weather like a person:

"Then you didn't come to burn the room. Why did you come, my lord?"

"I came," Corvin said, "for the fourth line."

And he looked at Isolde, and the room's forty-one faces turned with his eyes like a single organism, and she stood there in her mended coat, case in hand, Lampbearer's gray-blue left hanging on a hook a mile away, which suddenly seemed the most eloquent fact about her: she had come to this room as one thing and she was standing in it as another.

"The schedule," Corvin said, "is a tool. Second line — *re-tithe, Assur, B.* — is not real. There has never been a re-tithe. The Court does not harvest twice; the Court does not *need* twice, and a clerical body that invents a second tithe is a clerical body trying to see whose signature jumps when it reads the list aloud." He said it levelly, still pleasant, and the whole room understood it at once, the way rooms understand a draught.

"You set the paper to see if she'd bite," the scarred woman said.

"I set the paper," Corvin said, "to see if *I* would bite, which is a question I ask myself quarterly, at my age, as a hygiene. It is important to know what one is still capable of being angry about." He turned back to Isolde, and something in his face rearranged itself into the expression he used on students who had said something almost right, and Isolde braced, wrongly, for the almost.

"My dear. You have spent three days doing the one thing I have spent twenty-two years pretending nobody can do, which is *to notice*, quietly, professionally, with a ledger in your hand, and to then stand in the room you noticed and refuse to burn it down." His voice came from above her, impersonal, impossible to argue with, and *warm* beneath the impersonal, the way amber is warm beneath the blue. "The question is no longer whether you know. The question — which has been the only question of your career and the only question of mine, though you asked it with a funnel and I ask it with a seal — is what you do with a truth that burns the whole house down if you light it."

"What I do," said Isolde, "is what the rite says. Bring it to the flame, unopened, unkept, unreported."

"And you have kept one," Corvin said, pleasantly. "The fourth wedge. You breathed it on your bed in the dark, my dear; you cannot hide grass-smell from the woman who taught you to braid it into that memory, and I have known since your third year that you cannot lie in any voice but the flat one." He let that stand. He let *all* of it stand, in a room full of stolen walls, with forty-one people hearing the safest secret of the Ninth Dome being spoken aloud by the second-most dangerous man in nine of them, and then he offered, the way he offered everything, like weather offers rain:

"Come to Dawn Hall. Not as a Lampbearer — you are relieved of the Ninth, effective tonight, which will save us both the ugliness of paperwork. Come as what you are, which is the finest harvester alive and the only one who has ever *opened a lamp and gone looking for the hand that packed it*. Come and stand at the great lantern. Come and see the one truth I have kept for twenty-two years of counting rooms, and then — and only then, my dear, on your own two feet and with no funnel in your fist — decide which side of it you want to be on."

The amber crackled again. Assur moved then — one quick old-man's step, and his hand was over Isolde's wrist, and his palm pressed flat against hers, palm to palm, the way the dying press hands at funerals, and forty-one people watched a Lord Examiner watch a monk steal a second and a half, and nobody moved, because *nobody had told them they could*.

His hands came away. His wild brows came apart. And Isolde's palm was warm with a shape, the way a page is warm from a fire — not a memory, never a memory, a shape, a weather: *a ledger, huge, older than the Charter; a wall of the oldest stone; a mark cut into it; a name pressed against the mark like a coin into wax.* She would not know the letters until she was alone, and then she would know them the way you know your own door in the dark.

S.

"The monk," Corvin said, without anger, without anything, "is my guest tonight. He has declined a bed for eleven years; I expect the Undercroft will suit him like a suite. Girl—" the word came out exactly the way Assur said it, which was the most frightening thing about it "— you will want to say something to him. Say it in the next minute, because I am being decent at speed, and decency at speed is what it sounds like."

And then it was Isolde's turn to be a door, standing in front of a small brown monk who had pretended to die so that she would have to choose whether she was alive.

"You packed that harvest wrong," she said, because her voice had left, again, without her, and this is what came out. "All those tidy years. You knew I'd feel the folds. That's the *test*, isn't it — you feed me a life so clean it has to be false, and you watch which way I look. Any other Lampbearer would've signed the ledger with the fourth wedge empty, because other Lampbearers don't go home and *sit on the edge of their beds and open the thing*. How many of you have packed it for how many of us, over how many years?"

Assur's wild white brows went up. The scarred woman in the back made a small sound like a held breath having given up. And the old monk looked at the Lord Examiner, and the Lord Examiner looked at the ceiling of the room he had kept alive for twenty-two years, and between them, without one word spoken, Isolde read the answer she had not known she was asking:

*You are not the first, girl.*

*But you are the first one who came back for the receipt.*

"I will see you at the great lantern," Assur said, and let the Wardens lead him out through the maintenance stairs, through the gallery, past a bathhouse attendant who had learned, apparently, how to examine weight, and the door of the boiler hall closed, and the amber flame sat there alone in the middle of forty-one people who all turned, slowly, like a congregation, to look at the woman in the mended coat with the empty case, and the scarred woman — the one who locked doors and trained people to be harder than herself — crossed the room and took both her hands in her burn-scarred ones, and said, quiet as the rite itself:

"Welcome to being unlit, Lampbearer. Whatever you chose up there, in the next minute, with him — you already chose it the first time you smelled the grass."

She let go of her hands, and then, because she was the Kindling and the Kindling do not make a speech, she went and sat back down on her bench at the back of the room, and the room went on about the enormous business of the hour: which was that the bell had not rung on them tonight, and there were forty-one people alive in a boiler hall who had expected not to be, and that is a great deal of feeling for a room with no way to spend it.

Isolde stood in the middle of it for eleven minutes, and learned three things, and none of them were about the rite.

The first was how they spoke to each other. Nobody in the room used a name. Not from shyness — the Unlit had been un-named by the schedule for so long that the habit had gone into the grammar: you were *the roof-keeper*, *the woman on the third ladder*, *Osric's brother*, *the one with the cough*, and a child two benches away corrected her mother twice in five minutes without ever once hearing herself called anything but *little blue*. Isolde had spent seventeen years reading names off beds in a flat voice, and she had never once noticed that the names came *off*; they were on the slate and in the funnel and in the ledger, and by the morning they were nowhere, and this room was what *nowhere* looked like when it had forty-one faces and a good fire.

The second thing was the queue.

It formed without anybody deciding to form it, which is how queues happen. A man with a brick the size of his two fists came and stood in front of her and said nothing at all, and behind him, in the space of one lamp-trimming, there were six, and then eleven, and by the time the tenth reached her she understood that she had not been asked a thing and was being asked the whole thing: *read mine. Read mine. Tell me whether it is still in there. Tell me what it is, and I will not pay you, because I own nothing, and I will not ask you to, because you have already given tonight.*

She read four. She stopped at four, and the stopping is the item she thought about for the rest of her life, runners, because it was the first professional decision she ever made with no profession behind it. She had no funnel; she had no seal; she had no ledger to write the counts in, and the four she did were *free*, and they went like this, in the order the room gave them, and the room has kept the order in its walls ever since so that anybody can go to the Sixth and press a palm to the boiler-hall brick and hear it exactly as she said it, flat, at a bedside, in the only voice:

*The man with the brick:* a roof, a winter, a goat that had no business being on a roof, and a father going up after it, and the shape was the going-up, not the goat. *The woman on the third ladder:* a wedding with the lights in it, and the exact weight of a hand she had held once for two minutes at a rail, and Isolde said *it is a stranger's hand* and the woman said *I know*, which is the bravest sentence in this chapter.

*The one with the cough:* nothing. A blank wall, honestly reported, and the man took the news the way you take a doctor's news about somebody else, and said *all right*, and went back to his bench, and did not throw the brick, and Isolde had to decide in that second what she was going to do with having hurt a person *accurately*, and she decided, and it took her six weeks and one road to say the decision out loud, which is the way with the real ones.

*Little blue:* a soup. Any soup. A Thursday. And when Isolde said *it is a soup, and it is a good one*, the child said, without any wonder at all, in the plain voice of a person reporting weather and saying the one word the whole room had agreed not to say: "That's the one Mirelle keeps." And the back bench made a sound like a coat being put down, and the scarred woman did not look up from her hands, and did not say a word, and forty-one people in a boiler hall decided, all together, in one second, not to mention it.

And that was the third thing, runners, and it is the reason this chapter is called what it is called. She found out the woman's name the wrong way, at the end, in the doorway, when the tubes' last call was fifteen minutes off and Isolde had her coat up off its hook and her case empty under her arm and turned back, because she had stood in this room twice as a Lampbearer and had never once, either time, asked a single one of the forty-one what they were called.

"You have a name," she said, "and I have never used it."

"Mirelle," the scarred woman said, at the back of a boiler hall, in the flat voice, without any warmth in it whatever, and with the whole of her eight years of road in the single word, because it had been twenty years since anybody had said *you have a name* to her, and the second anyone says it, you have to decide whether you are going to be a person or be a trade. "Mirelle Dax. Sixth column, and the Sixth has been my roof for eight years. My mother sold mine when I was sixteen and I have kept the shape of it in a jar I will not open, and I have spent twenty years teaching other people to do the thing I cannot do, and Lampbearer — I want it on whatever record survives this — you are the first one in seventeen years to read for four of us and *bill nobody*, and the Court will hear about it by the census, and I want you to know that out here that is not nothing, that is a *keep*. And while you are being a person to me, take the law of this house with you, because it is written on nothing and eight years of road have not been able to get it off me: ***nothing goes out around me***. Nothing leaves my presence unlabeled, uncounted, unasked for — no jar, no word, no coin, no child; and nothing of yours goes out around you either, Lampbearer, which is why you are going up the maintenance stair to Dawn Hall instead of to a bed. Go and look at your great lantern. Come back up the road with the man who counted us. And if you ever have to stand at a rail and give a name for somebody, give mine *first*, because I have been the locked door of my own house long enough to be tired of the hinge."

So Isolde Vane went down the maintenance stair with a name in her mouth she had not earned, and four free readings in her hands, and the smell of boiled linen off a mended coat, and the tube to Dawn Hall waiting at the service door with the last call on it; and behind her, in the amber, forty-one people began, very quietly, to say each other's names out loud, in practice, the way you practice a song you have been told you are allowed to sing now.

---

## Chapter 6 · The White Light

There are no roads between the domes. There is no need for roads, because nothing between the domes lives, and so the Court built the way termites build: tubes. Sealed glass tubes on iron legs, running over the black snow like straws laid across a grave, and inside them a traveler may walk, if he has a seal, in air that is only *almost* as warm as a dome's, and look out through frosted walls at a dark that has never once in the history of the world been interrupted, and think whatever it is travelers think in glass tubes.

Isolde thought: *she walked this, once. The grass-smell lady. Corvin's seals are twenty-two years old; hers is older than the Charter.*

She thought other things too, but those arrived later, in the wrong order, at the wrong hour. What she thought while the Ninth Dome shrank behind her was small and mean and true: that her good coat was still hanging on its hook; that she had not eaten since the turnips; that the Lord Examiner walked faster than any man his age had a right to, and did not once look back to see if she was coming, because he knew she was.

Dawn Hall was not bigger than the Ninth. It was *older*, which is a different and worse kind of big. The glass here was thick as a wall and clouded at the seams like old ice, and the streets ran downhill to the middle in spiral ramps, and every bracket, every vent, every door-frame bore the same carved mark, repeated the way prayers are repeated: a lamp, and inside the lamp a sun with rays — not crossed out. Whole. And at the bottom of every ramp, in the middle of the spiral, in a plaza where no market stood because no market had ever needed to, rose the lantern.

She had seen it pictured, like everyone. Pictures lie the way poor people lie about prices — with the best intentions. The great lantern stood sixty feet over the plaza on eight iron legs, glass-and-iron as all lamps are, only *enormous*, a cathedral with a flame in it, and the flame inside was—

White.

Not bright-white. *White-white*, the color of a page, of bone, of the memory-shape of a thing called *snow*, which was on no dome's map, and the light that came of it was so total that Isolde discovered, walking into the plaza with her shadow thrown ahead of her like a stray dog, that she had never in her life seen colors. Had never seen the true red of the market cloth or the green of a scriptor's ink or the actual, impossible, heart-breaking *blue* of the glass seams overhead, all of it pulled out of the world by the white light like a thread from a sleeve, and she understood in one bad minute why the Court's portraits showed everyone squinting.

"It takes more than the domes can give," Corvin said, standing under the lantern with his hands behind his back, not looking up, and his voice was weather again, but weather with a crack in it. "It has taken more, every year of every century, and the ledgers call it *growth*, and the ledgers are clerks, and clerks write what they are told. Lampbearer — look at it properly. Look at the bottom of the glass."

She looked. White light poured down the plaza, and at the very bottom of the great lantern, below the flame-line, there was a band of the glass inner wall that the light did not reach, a ring of shadow an arm's width wide, and in that shadow the flame *leaned*. Not flickered — leaned, inward, toward the center of itself, the way a candle leans at the end of its wick when there is nothing left in the wax but wick.

"A starved fire," Isolde said, because she had trained on them the way other girls train on horses. "It's eating its own body. You can see the seam where it curls—" she stopped. Stopped because the thing she was about to say was the thing no one says: *it's eating its own body and its body is a thousand years of other people's lives and the seam is where it's starting to come back out.*

"You see fast," Corvin said. "That is the talent. That is the *curse* of this entire blood of hands. Come; the Ledger is below, and the hour is short, and what I am going to show you is the one truth I have kept for twenty-two years of counting rooms."

But the hour was, in fact, not short, and Corvin spent the first eleven minutes of it being *inspected*, and Isolde watched the second-most dangerous man in nine domes submit to it by a lamp-post in the First's plaza without once using his voice, and that was when she understood that Dawn Hall frightened him.

Four Lampbearers of the First came down the spiral ramp to meet a gray-blue coat that had not been worn in this dome for two years, and they were young, and their wool was good, and their cases were three years from a maker, and the tallest of them looked at the mended shoulder of the coat Corvin was wearing and said, pleasantly, in the accent of the Plaza Dome, which is the accent that has never once in a thousand years had to ask for anything: "The Ninth is sending us *mendings* now?" and Corvin said, pleasantly, "The Ninth is sending me, and it is sending her because it has run out of other ideas," and the tall one looked at Isolde for the length of one held breath and asked her, not unkindly, in the tone a person uses on a stranger who has come a long way to be embarrassed: "And what is your function in the First, citizen?"

"I am a Lampbearer," Isolde said, flat, at a bedside, in the only voice, to a wall of good wool in the brightest plaza in the world, and the four of them were very correct about it afterward, and one of them brought her a cup of hot water she did not drink, and the tallest said *forgive us, sir, we had no counts*, and Corvin said *you never have, that is the whole of the trouble*, and they went down toward the vault, and Isolde walked two steps behind with her hands in her pockets, learning the last lesson the domes had to teach her before the road began, which was this: *there is no floor in nine domes on which you are somebody, and there is no floor on which you are nobody either, and the only honest place to stand is beside whoever is being inspected.*

And then, at the head of the stair to the vault, out of the way of the four pairs of good wool and in the amber of a service-bracket, Corvin did the thing he had not done in twenty-two years of weather, and it is the reason she got out of the hole in the Ninth and got into the sledge, and the reason she came up the fourteen stairs, and the reason she stood at the glass of the great lantern at the blue hour with nothing in her hands.

He gave her a book.

It was the size of a psalter and bound in the grey felt of old service-tube lining, and it had no title on it, and inside, in a hand she had seen ten thousand times at the head of a ward list, were eleven rooms. Eleven rooms in nine domes over twenty-two years, each one dated in the census form and each one carrying, under the count, the same three words in the same careful clerk's hand, written none, written none, written none — and on the last page of each room's block, in the same hand, in columns so small they had to be read with a lamp an inch away: the *names*. Four hundred and twelve names. Not numbers; names, or as near as the schedule let a clerk get, which was a surname and a dome and a year and, sometimes, one other item that Corvin had evidently been unable to keep out of an official document and had copied anyway, because it was in the original and the original was *his*: "the goat", "the bell", "bad with cold", "sings while working", "twelve years old and *correct* about it".

Isolde read nine of the eleven pages standing up on a service stair in the First Dome, and she did not say anything at all, and the Lord Examiner of nine domes stood with his hands behind his back and looked at the wall and waited, the way a man waits at a bedside when the dying has begun to take a long time and there is nobody else in the room to tell him whether it is going to be all right.

"You have carried this," she said at last, "for twenty-two years."

"I have carried it for twenty-two years."

"In a *coat*. In the tube, at the census, in the boiler hall, in front of the Order and the corps and the four Lampbearers who just asked me my function—"

"In a coat," Corvin said, "yes, my dear. It has never once been in a desk, because a desk can be opened by a man with a warrant and a coat can be opened only by a man with a *question*, and I have been waiting a long time, an embarrassingly long time, for somebody in nine domes to be rude enough to ask me one." He took the book back, gently, the way you take a cup off a dying person's table. "There is a twelfth page, and it is not a room, and I am going to show it to you down the stair in about four minutes, in front of the Ledger, because if I show it here I shall have to explain myself, and I have found that I explain badly when it is the item that matters. Four hundred and twelve, my dear, of the ones I did *not* write, and one of the ones I *did*, and the arithmetic of a life is not the first number. It never has been. It is the second one, and mine is the size of a psalter, and I have read it aloud in my own head on about nine hundred stairs." And he put the book in his coat, and buttoned the coat over it, and offered her his arm up the last of the stair to the vault like a man going into a church he had stopped believing in twenty-two years ago and had come back to anyway, because the one thing left in it was a name.

The Lantern Ledger stood in a vault under the plaza, in a room of the oldest stone in the world, on a cradle of iron built for something no other room had ever held, because the Lantern Ledger was not a book. It was a *wall*. Leaves of hammered glass, each one the size of a door, bound with wire at the top, ten thousand of them maybe more, filling one whole side of the vault from floor to roof, and every leaf was written, and the writing was the only writing Isolde had ever seen that moved: not much — a tremor, a shimmer, the letters tightening now and then the way skin tightens over a healing wound — but she stood in front of it with her hands at her sides and her professional soul turned completely, finally, helpfully, to ice, and understood:

"Every memory ever burned," she said. "Recorded. *All* of them. There's no vault, we say, memory can't be kept, and the Court has kept *the whole lot*, since the first night, and it's *alive in there*, it's— the letters moved. The Ledger *leans*."

"The Ledger is the flame's inventory," said Corvin, and he did not say it smoothly; he said it like a man reciting a diagnosis he had read too many times. "Name, dome, day, shape.
