The tithe-houses feed it by seal; the scribes of the Order copied it by hand for nine centuries before the Charter; the First Leaves were written by the harvesters themselves, the original twelve, the women and men who lit the domes on the night the sun died, and who were — I will tell you this plainly, my dear, plainly, because it is the hinge of everything I am about to ask of you — *who were the first liars.* They said the sun was gone forever. They said nothing could be done forever. They built the rite, and the rent, and the census, and the coin, and their own names at the bottom of the oldest page, and then they grew old in bright rooms, and the flame — which they had built to be small, to be *enough*, the way all fires are built — began, year by year, to require what you see in that glass, and no one in nine domes has ever asked the question the Ledger will answer for you in the next two minutes."

"What question," said Isolde, but her hands were already moving, because Assur's shape was warm in her palm like a coin, and the oldest stone of the vault was in front of her, and on the oldest stone there was a mark cut into it — a lamp with one line through it — and the shape her palm knew was not a question at all.

The shape was: *press, and read what the wall was told to forget.*

She pressed. The stone took her hand the way old stone takes warmth — wholly, and without hurry — and down through the wall, through the leaves, through ten thousand doors of hammered glass, the tremor came to meet her, letters tightening everywhere at once, a shimmer running from the First Leaves upward like a draught through a field of grass, and Isolde Vane read, in a voice that was not hers and not the wall's and belonged to neither the Court nor the Unlit nor any rite ever written, the name pressed against the mark like a coin into wax:

*Seren Vane, First Keeper of the Lantern, hand of the twelve, who proved the flame could be returned.*

And directly under it, in a later hand, a smaller hand, an examiner's hand, flat and careful and terribly, terribly familiar:

*Harvested by order of the Ashen Court. Executor: C.A.*

The vault was very quiet. The white light overhead made the whole room glow like the inside of a held breath. Across from her, the Lord Examiner stood with his hands open at his sides, palms out, the posture of a man who has been carrying something up a long stair for twenty-two years and has reached, at last, the landing, and he said, without weather, without pleasantness, in a voice she had never once heard him use:

"Now you have it all, my dear. Your mother was not a victim of the rite. She *invented* half of it. And I was twenty-six years old, and junior, and obedient, and I held the funnel, and she braided your hair in the next room and told me — she told me, Isolde, and I have never in my life been able to say it in the front of a room, so you will forgive me a stairway — she told me: *Take the little one last, so she remembers the shape of you, and raise her with it, because someone has to remember what it cost.*" His hands did not move. "I did not take you last. I took you *first*, and I gave you the smell of boiled linen at seven years old and no name and no threshold, because the Court's own rite says the child of the accused is the Court's own apprentice, and an apprentice with no past *cannot* avenge, and I was so proud of that cleverness, for twenty-two years, my dear. Look at me now. I have run out of cleverness, and there is a Grand Tithe in six weeks, and the flame is in the glass, and the only hands alive that can work it are the ones she braided."

*Item — the two numbers of Corvin Aelfric's silence, which every clerk afterward tried to make into one and could not: twenty-two years is the length of the counting of the rooms, and seventeen is the length of the keeping of a name. For five of those years he was a man who had nothing particular to be quiet about. He said so once, on a stair, in the weather voice, and it is the saddest line in the whole of his paperwork: "the first five years were* practice, *my dear, and I was extremely good at practice, and that is the entire trouble with being good at anything."*

---

## Chapter 7 · The Thing She Kept Outside

She did not scream, which surprised them both.

Seventeen years of being a door, and the last of it came in useful now: Isolde stood under the moving wall of every name ever burned and felt her whole life — the hall, the rite, the funnel, the flat careful ledger-hand, the smell of boiled linen that was not a childhood but a *cover-up* — go quietly around an axis she had never been allowed to know, and she said, in a voice that came out almost level:

"Say the word *Grand Tithe* again. Say it the way you'd say it to the Court, so I can hear what you'd leave out."

And Corvin, who had carried it up the stair for twenty-two years, said it.

Census-night, nine domes, all at once. Every tithe-house open until the blue hour; every hearth-lamp read and recorded; and beneath the record, in the seams of the pipes that carried warmth from the great lantern outward — new valves, installed over four winters, called *the return lines*, because the Court's clerks write what they are told and are told only beautiful words. On the night the valves close, the outer domes will give their year to the center. Not a tithe of the dying. A tithe of *everyone*, trimmed low: every sold memory in every lamp in every house, drawn back through the lines to feed a flame that has been eating its own body for two centuries. The old, the Hollowed, the low-row sick will feel it first — a bad night, a worse winter, *blue in March*, the clerks will call it, because they are told nothing uglier than that. The rest will lose the year's market. A season of forgetting, sold by weight, to keep Dawn Hall white until next spring.

"And you installed the valves," Isolde said. "Over four winters. With your counts."

"I installed the valves over four winters, with my counts, in eleven rooms I did not burn, and I have told you the size of the truth and not the shape of it, my dear, so let us be exact: I did not design the Grand Tithe. I *made it possible*, which is the whole of the Court's service, and now I am telling you it cannot hold, that the flame will need a second one by next Coldmonth and a third by the year after, that nine domes cannot be sheared twice in a winter and remain domes, and that there is — there *is*, look at me, look at the wall, it is shaking for you, it has been waiting twenty-two years to be asked — there is one other page in the Ledger that no examiner has ever read aloud, because it is not written on glass. It is written on the outside of the lantern, in the black, where nobody goes, and your mother wrote it, and I sealed over it with my own hands, and I have thought about it every day at every hour, and I am too old and too cowardly and too *deeply complicit* to go out and scrape it off, and the flame leans, and the valves are cut, and the only hands — *the only hands*, Seren's daughter, I am not asking you as the Court, I am asking you as the twenty-six-year-old who held the funnel — the only hands that can work the great lantern without lighting it higher are yours."

*Proved the flame could be returned*, the oldest leaf had said. Returned. Not relit — *returned*, which was a word for a borrowed thing, and there was no one alive to have lent it, unless—

"What's in the Ashlands, Corvin."

His weather cracked, all the way down. "The first harvest," he said quietly. "The twelve lit the domes on the night the sun died by burning *everything they had of it* — a memory of light so old and so huge it was not anybody's, it was *everybody's*; that is what fed the first flame, and that is why the flame has never stopped wanting; it is looking for the rest of what it ate — and your mother, in the year before we took her, proved that the burning was not total. That some of what the first lantern swallowed did not burn. It went *out*. Out through the glass, into the dark, and the dark kept it, the way the walls keep what the flame keeps, only *wholly* — because the dark has had a thousand years and nothing to forget." He looked up, for the first time in their whole acquaintance, like a student. "The Court has sent forty expeditions. The glass-tubes run to the Ninth because nobody beyond the Ninth will walk. Whatever your mother put out there is *not dead*, Isolde, it is *not alone*, and every census-year the Ledger's First Leaves lean outward toward it, and I have counted that leaning for twenty-two years, and I have never once reported the direction."

"The direction," Isolde said.

"The thing out there knows the domes," he said. "It leans when we burn. It is — I cannot say *made of* the memories, my dear, I can only say the Ledger says the first flame *ate a day of the world* and something out in the dark has been *digesting it* for a thousand years, and your mother's page is on the outside of the lantern, and the valves are cut, and there are six weeks until I stand in the plaza with a seal in my hand and close nine domes' year into one fire, and there is no one — *no one* — else alive who can hold a lamp and go out into the black after it."

She signed nothing. That was her answer, the next hour, and the hour after, when they walked up out of the vault with the white light leaning through the floor-grate and the Court's second-most dangerous man did not once say *my dear*, because he had run out of cleverness, apparently, and was testing whether he had run out of everything. She signed nothing, and ate nothing — a bowl of warm broth in a hall with a hundred doors on it, none of which were hers now — and at the bad hour she went up through Dawn Hall's oldest stair, following a shape a monk had pressed into her palm, to the outermost ring of the First Dome, where the service doors stand, and the great lantern leans overhead, and the outside of the lantern is black iron and black glass and the dark on the other side of it is the whole history of the world.

The stone there was the oldest in the First Dome, and the mark was cut under the grime, and her hand found it the way her hand had found everything since the fourth wedge: *authorized*. But she was not alone in a way she had not been authorized for, because at the foot of the service stair, two Wardens stood with their hands behind their backs, and behind them, on the landing, a gray coat on a man who had clearly walked down quickly, for once, in twenty-two years, and stood there, and said, without anger, with a kind of exhausted *relief* that was worse than anger:

"The direction, my dear. I said *the direction*, and you went the wrong way on the stair at the second landing, which tells me you know exactly where you are going and I have been wrong, since the bathhouse, about the most dangerous fact about you, which is not that you noticed — it is that you were *pointed*. Assur's little palm-shapes. How many has he taught you, and how much of my dome did he walk out with under one thumb?" He held out his hand, palm up, empty, an offer and a sentence at once. "The seal or the tube. Either takes you to the same place eventually; the seal takes you *warm*. I am being decent, Isolde. I have been decent at speed for twenty-two years and I am tired, and the Grand Tithe is in six weeks, and you are going to be the one standing at the lantern, or you are going to be the one standing in the dark, and I cannot — I find that I *cannot*, which is my whole disease, my dear, you should study it, it is the only free thing left in me — I cannot make you mine the way I made you a Lampbearer, because the second time I would have to do it to your face, and your face is *hers*."

"You'll drain me," Isolde said. "The rite, fully, publicly, with the Court's own examiner holding the funnel. My name on the Ledger by morning. That's the seal."

"The seal is a post," Corvin said. "Lantern-warden. I invented it tonight; there will be no paperwork, which should tell you something about my state of mind. You will stand at the flame with your hands, where I can see them, and you will learn what it leans toward, and on census-night we will *open the valves halfway*, which is a number no clerk has ever been told, and if the thing out there is what your mother thought it was — then in one winter we will know whether nine domes can be saved, and if it is what I fear it is, then in one winter we will have spent half a year's memory to find out *nothing survives*, which is cheaper than what I am being asked to do instead and I have the ledger to prove it. The tube is exile. Unharvested — hear me, *unharvested*, I will not have your name in the Ledger on my hand a second time — but out, with what you carry, and no seal, and no return, and the truth I cannot say to your face on a landing, which is this: *she made you a Lampbearer's hands and a thief's patience and she braided into both, in the next room, a thing she did not tell me.* I held the funnel, my dear. I know exactly what we took that night, and there was a day — one day, a whole day, and I did not see it, and it was not on the leaves, and the only person alive it could belong to is standing on my service stair in a dead woman's coat." He let that stand, in the black, under the leaning flame, and held out his hand. "Seal or tube. And go *the right way on the second landing*."

She took the tube.

It cost her his hand to do it — she had to put her palm down on the service seal and let it read the shape of her, which meant that for four seconds the most powerful man in nine domes was holding her bare hand in the dark under a starving flame with his eyes shut, and she understood, dimly, in the way one understands a draught, that this, too, was part of it: that he had wanted, since she was seven, to be *hated* cleanly, and that she had denied him that the way she had denied the whole rite every honest second of her career, by refusing to be the room on the other side of the door — by being, instead, the door that walked away with it open.

The tube spat her at the Ninth — no; *past* the Ninth; the service doors beyond the Ninth, the ones with no attendant and no gallery, opened under her hands because her hands were the ones she braided, and then they closed, and the glass sealed, and the white light of Dawn Hall behind her shrank to a star, and then to nothing, and then there was front and back and no up, and the cold was a wall with no door, and somewhere in the black ahead, past the frost of her own breath, something that had been digesting the sun for a thousand years turned, the way a sleeper turns, and — leaning toward her, the way the Ledger leaned — *listened to her boots*.

---

# PART TWO — THE ASHLANDS

---

## Chapter 8 · The Boy With Eleven Knives

The first rule of the Ashlands, which nobody taught her because nobody alive in a dome knew it, is that the cold is not a temperature. The cold is an *animal*, and it is patient, and it has read you before you have read it, and it knows — the way a dog knows a hand going to a bowl — the exact moment your body starts spending itself.

Isolde knew she was dying by the hour, professionally, the way she had known four hundred and eleven deaths from the bedside, because a Lampbearer's whole career is the study of the last warm minute. Her fingers had stopped being fingers twenty minutes after the service door sealed. After forty, the strange kind courage came — the walking-fast courage, the *I can do this all night* courage that Corvin's weather would have called *the blue hour of the body* — and she understood, with the last tidy room in her head, that she was being brave for the reason old Maren was brave, and old Maren was in the ground with the sea in a wall.

The glass of the Ninth Dome rose on her left like a cliff of frozen light, and the colonies were at its foot.

She had to pass them. That was the second rule she found out on her own: the Hollowed stand where the warmth leaks, the way crickets stand where the porch-light bleeds, and there were *hundreds* of them out there in the black, in coats the color of nothing, standing in stillness so complete that she walked into the middle of one and it was a man, gently, before she knew he was not a post. They faced outward. That was the thing no picture had ever shown, the thing that made her stop dead in her own freezing and say her own name aloud, to be sure of it, to keep one small lamp of herself lit: they all faced *away from the light*. Forty expeditions of the Court, and the first finding was that the drained do not stand in the dark like prisoners.

They stand in the dark like *sentinels*.

"You're doing the thing," said a voice under her left boot, and she nearly died of surprise, which is a bad way to die when you are already dying of cold. "The thing where you look at them proper. Nobody does the thing. Sit down, lamp-girl, or the frost'll finish the sentence your boots started."

Under the hem of her coat, in a hollow behind a drift that had no business being a doorway, something *was*. It was a boy. Thirteen, maybe, or a bad-kept twelve, brown as pipe-root the way Assur was brown, with a face too old for its own grin, and he was sitting — no, he was *lying in a hole with a hole in it*, which was the third rule of the Ashlands, and the hole was warm, which was a lie so big that Isolde stood at its lip in the blue hour of the body and said, in a voice that was mostly frost:

"That's not possible. Nothing burns out here. There's no tithe, no tax, no *fuel*—"

"Wrong, wrong, *grumpy*," said the boy, and held up what the hole was burning on, and the hole was burning on a lamp no bigger than a plum, blue and low and entirely *unauthorised*, and the lamp was burning on a memory the way all lamps do, but the memory was — she could tell by the shape of the burn, the thin yellow edge of it, a burn she had seen exactly once in her life, in the Order's own manuals, in a chapter on *petty tithes* — not somebody's.

"Somebody's *cat*," the boy said proudly. "I found the lamp out in the blow-spirals, it's had the same think since it was lit, twenty year maybe — animals burn cheap, lamp-girl, a cat's whole life is a saucer and a window and *one* opinion about the garden. I named it. I named the lamp. The cat's already named, they come with it." He patted the frost at his side like a stepmother. "Down you come. You're the second person I've ever seen walk proper past the standing-folk. First one was a lady, years back, she did the thing too, she said *the walls lean outward, boy, remember that*, and then she went down the black, so. You're the *second*. I'm Ovre. Tam Ovre. Get in the hole before you cost me a good cat-memory, I'm not *made* of saucers."

She got in the hole.

Warmth in the Ashlands is a rumor you agree to believe, but this one had a source: the plum-lamp sat in a nest of packed snow — *snow*, absurd — and the snow did not melt, because the boy had lined the nest with the grey felt of old service-tubes, scavenged, and the fire burned in a tin no bigger than a fist, and the heat came up through the floor of the hole the way heat comes up through a person's whole life, which is to say: not evenly, and never from where you expect. There were pots. There was a sledge outside under the drift, loaded with what a scavenger's sledge loads: glass, wire, seals with nobody left to answer them, a Court lantern-bracket polished like a wedding ring, and forty jars of *nothing*, which the boy said, with enormous scorn for anyone who would ask, "are for keeping *weathers* in, obviously, dome-kin don't get that, you lot live in a *leaky wall*, we live in the *draft*—"

Isolde reached for one of the jars, because a jar of nothing was not a thing she owned a category for, and got her hand flatly slapped, hard, twice, by a boy who did not even look at her while he did it.

"*Don't*," Tam Ovre said, in the ordinary voice of a person correcting a child about a stove. "That's a *keep*. You don't read a keep. You *visit* it. The lady said, and she was *right*, and it's the only rule out here that isn't about dying — you put your palm on the glass and you *ask* and if it tells you, it tells you, and if you *pull*, then you're just another dome-kin coming to take the weather off a poor man. Sit down and *look*, lamp-girl, the labels are the whole *show*—"

And the labels, in a scrawled hand that had learned its letters off jar tags, were the best and saddest inventory Isolde Vane had ever audited, and she went down the shelf on her knees in the warm dark of a hole in the nothing and she read them out loud, one by one, in the flat voice, the way she had read four hundred and eleven ward lists, and it came out like a liturgy and neither of them noticed:

*FROST, GOOD DAY, NINTH ROAD.* *WIND OFF THE RIBS, ONE (1) HOUR, LOUD.* *A DOG, NOT MINE, THIRD DOME, FREELY.* *SMELL OF A BOILER, ASH.* *TIN SHOP BELL, SIXTH, WRONG SIDE OF YEAR.* *RAIN.*

"Rain," Isolde said.

"Rain," Tam said, with the enormous smugness of the absolutely unverifiable. "Off the outer glass, the *big* one, the year the tubes froze and the dome sweated. That's a *weather* keep, lamp-girl, that's the only kind worth having, because you can't buy rain. Nobody ever harvested rain. The Court tried, in the *old* leaves, and it's why the domes leak, and that jar is worth more than your whole *case*."

"That one," Isolde said, at the end of the shelf, where the forty-first jar stood with no label at all, sealed with wax that had been applied twice, and had been kept, from the wear of the shelf around it, in exactly that place, for years. "Whose weather is that?"

"That's not a weather."

"Then it is not a keep either, because every keep has a label, and that jar has been on that shelf long enough to wear a groove, and you have not moved it, and a boy who has lived alone in the draft as long as you have done has moved *everything* out here by now except one thing—"

"*That's a person*," Tam said.

And the frost stood up in its pillars, and out in the black the standing-folk went on facing the wrong way, and a Lampbearer of the Ninth Dome who had in seventeen years never once been refused by a child with a scraped hand sat back on her heels in a hole under a drift and understood that she had just been handed the first rule of the Ashlands that no rite had taught her, and that it had been given to her by a boy with bad handwriting: *a keep is not a thing you open, it is a thing you are invited to.*

That is the item I want in front of you before the column comes, runners, because it is going to be the shape of her entire winter. Her first thought about the forty-first jar was the professional one, and she was ashamed of it for eleven weeks. Her second thought was that she would like to know what was in that jar more than she had ever wanted anything at a bedside in her life. Her third thought was the one that got her out of the hole and into the sledge: not to take it, not to ask again, just to be *near* the person who could say *no* to a Lampbearer and live.

"All right," she said, and put her hands flat on her knees, and did not touch the jar again. "Teach me the knives first."

And there were the knives.

Eleven of them, laid along the hole's wall in rolls of bad leather, sized from *thimble* to *door*, each with a tag of twine and a mark burned into the handle, and the boy followed her looking and said, defensively, "They're *job* knives. The little one's for wire, the big one's for *ice*, I'm not a *murderer*, lamp-girl, I live alone out in the nothing with the standing-folk and the *wind that moves things*, eleven is what eleven *is*—"

"Twelve," Isolde said, because she had seen the twelfth, tucked under his pillow where he clearly thought nobody's eyes could ever reach, the longest one, the one with no twine and no tag and no mark, and something about the way it lay — worn at one spot on the handle, worn *by a hand* — made the frost in her voice go polite, the way her voice went at beds. "Who taught you the knives?"

"Nobody!" The grin went up like a drawn bucket, too fast, too bright. "The Ashlands teaches! I'm *self-*—"

"*Twelve*," Isolde said.

"Fine. There was a woman. Years back. She did the thing, she stood proper, she said *the walls lean outward, remember that boy*, she had hands like — " and the boy, who had been talking for probably months, years, alone under a drift with a cat-memory for a guest, stopped, and looked at her, and finished it quietly, in a voice that belonged to a much older man in a much smaller body: "— like *yours*, lamp-girl. Same *not-shaking*."

The plum-lamp leaned. Not flickered — *leaned*, in its tin, toward her, the way a candle leans at the end of its wick, and Isolde Vane looked at the boy with the eleventh knives and the twelfth secret and the cat-memory named like a guest, and she did the arithmetic of the blue hour, which is a Lampbearer's arithmetic, the only kind that had ever been true for her, and it came out like this: *the woman who said the walls lean outward, and did not come back down the black, and left a scavenger's education in a hole under a drift.* She did not say *when*. Out here, *when* was for clerks.

"Teach me the hole," she said instead, and felt something in her chest let go that she did not know she had been holding, exactly the way she had felt it let go on the stair to the Undercroft nine days ago, which was a thing she would have sworn she could not feel at all. "The *rules*, Tam Ovre. All of them. And the names of the drifts, and where the blow-spirals run, and which jars keep which weathers, and where the lady's roads went, because the thing you did not say is a road and I am going to walk it, and—" she heard herself, in the warm hole, in the blue hour, and did not stop, because out here nobody had ever been listening and so nobody had ever had to *not* be a door "— and if you ever call me *lamp-girl* again I will put you in one of the forty jars."

"Fourteen," Tam said, awed, and began to grin at her the way you begin to trust a weather. "The lady counted *fourteen*. She said *you'll meet one, the counting kind, the one who can't stop*—"

The drift outside the hole moved, quietly, the way a sleeper shifts; and then, much less quietly, from the black ahead — down the road of the lady, out of the dark on the far side of nothing — came a *light*. Not blue, not white, not any tithe-color the domes knew: a low, amber, *processional* line of lamps, a dozen, twenty, moving over the ice toward the Ninth Dome's foot, single-file, and at their head one that swung, the way a hand swings when it is carrying something it intends to *put down somewhere, soon*.

Tam's hand went to the door-knife. "The standing-folk aren't the only thing out here that *leans*," he said, and for once the grin was gone, and his face was thirteen years old again, and he said it like the Ashlands teaches everything, in the smallest possible sentence:

"Lamps that walk *away* from a dome, lamp-girl, mean a dome that don't want them anymore. Those are *carrying* somebody. Get in the sledge."

---

## Chapter 9 · The Kindling

The sledge was not so much driven as *negotiated with*, and Tam negotiated well, and the two of them went down the back of the drift and along the bed of an old blow-spiral while the column of lamps came on, and by the time the first bearer crested the drift where the hole had been, Isolde had learned three more rules of the Ashlands: that the wind has roads and the scavengers know them the way the domes know canals; that a man can run on a sledge behind a wind if he holds the wire right, which costs one knife out of eleven; and that the amber column was not raiding, not hunting, and not, as she had first thought, expelling.

It was a *hearse-train with a habit*. There were sheaves of them, the bearers moved in pairs, they spoke low over what they carried, and when they came past the blow-spiral the lamplight found their faces — twenty of them, ash-grey from a decade of the draft, with the wrapped shapes laid out on stretchers along the train's center, and one of the bearers saw Tam crouched at the spiral's lip with his wire and his eleven-roll and his passenger, and the whole column stopped, the way a flock stops, and the bearer said, across the ice, in the flat exhausted voice of a man who has walked too far to be surprised:

"Ovre. You've got a *dome-kin*."

"She's got *me*," Tam shouted back, with the last dignity of the absolutely correct. "She counted the *twelfth*, Hask."

And then they took her to the Kindling.

It was under the ice, at the place where the blow-spirals of nine domes cross and the frost stands up in pillars like the ribs of something enormous that had died standing, and down through a throat of old service-glass, and down, and then — Isolde had to sit, on a step cut in the ice, because her eyes had been lied to her entire life and she could feel the lie leaving — *amber*. Whole streets of it. A settlement of a thousand people under the black snow, in rooms hollowed from the ice and lit by hoarded hearth-lamps trimmed by hand, and the light came *down* the stairway in layers, the way water does, and where it pooled there were *people*: children with the ash-grey of the draft on them, old women arguing over a pot, a scriptor with a real ink-stain copying a real leaf of a real book by a real flame — and every wall of the stair, Isolde saw as she went down it, every wall in the town, was *written on by nobody*, which is to say it had been read: hand-marks in ten thousand places, palms pressed flat to the ice in the bad hour, the whole Kindling was one long listening-room, a people who had spent two generations with their ears to the walls of the world.

Hask, who could not stop looking at her coat, said it, on the stair, in the flat voice, the way men say the weather:

"We move the walkers. Every census the domes expel — the ones whose rent fell due and the ones who looked too long and the ones whose names jumped in the schedule — the glass spits them out the service doors and they stand, and the standing-folk face outward, and some of them *walk*, for years, in the long spiral, and we meet them where the drifts change, and we bring them in, and we keep them, and they learn their names again from the walls, because the walls kept them, miss — everything the fire takes, the walls keep. That's the whole of us. That's the whole idea. Your Lord Examiner's own rite, done *backward*." He swallowed. "We've done it twenty years. We lost the woman who taught it. She went down the black after the *leaning thing* and the ice gave her back to nobody." And, not meeting her eyes, because the Kindling did not thank and did not beg, and this was neither: "We keep a lamp lit at the crossroads for her every year. There's a man came up the spiral three days ago with a seal on his collar that said the great lantern was hungry and the census was *cut to six weeks*, and tonight the train found one more on the Ninth road, and I think, miss — I have thought it since the sledge — I think you should be the one who sees who it is."

The dead-ward was at the edge of the amber, where the lamps were stacked like guests asleep, and the one they had found on the Ninth road lay on the last stretcher, small and brown, with a shaved crown and wild white brows, and the seal of the First Dome clipped to his collar like a *calling-card*, and both his hands were closed.

The bearers had tried nothing. That was the dignity of the thing; you can see it when it is done to you. Isolde knelt in the amber dark beside Brother Assur and understood, the way she had understood four hundred and eleven times from the bedside, exactly what had happened to him and exactly how long ago and exactly how far he had been carried: drained, fully, for real, by the Court's own hand this time, *not* by Corvin's — there was a countersign on the seal she did not know and did not want to know — and then thrown out the service door of the First Dome like a used bandage, and he had *walked*. Hollowed, across the whole black between the domes, on the long spiral, facing outward, because the drained do not lie and the drained do not rest, and whatever the Court called him on the way out, on the way *back* he had been one of the Kindling's own: a walker, coming home on his own two legs for a fire he could no longer remember the name of, and had got — she touched the frost on his boots, and did the arithmetic, and her voice went flat, at a bed, for the last time — had got to the crossroads. Twenty years of the listening-walls, and he had died at the last mile of a road a woman had taught him to walk.

"His hands," said Hask. "They're closed like — they've been closed since the road. The bearers can't—"

"I know," Isolde said. "Nobody pries a harvest." She looked at the wild white brows, at the satisfied face, at the seal on the collar with the countersign she did not know. "He won the wager twice, Hask. He sat in my case's fourth wedge. He packed his own death for me. He walked twenty miles of black with a *clenched fist* because he knew, the way the walls know, that there is exactly one pair of hands in nine domes that can open a monk's hand without breaking it." She took the closed fists in her own, gently, palm to palm, the way the dying press hands at funerals, and she said to him, level, flat, the way she read names off beds, and the way her mother had laughed it into a memory: "I'm back for the receipt, Brother. You can put it down."

The left hand opened on a sigh of frost, and inside it, wrapped in the lining of a dead man's sleeve, sewn flat against the palm where no harvester in the history of the rite had ever looked, because harvesters look in the head, was a scroll of hammered glass, a leaf from the outside of the great lantern, scraped off black iron by a hand that had had *a thief's patience* and a lamp with one line through it scratched small in its corner, and the writing on it was the writing from the oldest page of the Ledger, and it said:

*My dawn-daughter. If a monk's hand reached you this, then I have been burned and your hands have come open for somebody else, and the rite is finished in you, and that is the whole of my work, and I am glad. Forgive the rest of it: I was not only a keeper of the flame, I was one of the twelve who ate the sun. We were told it was a loan. It was a feeding, and it never stopped, and what we fed out of the world did not die in the fire — it went into the dark whole, because dark keeps what light spends, and it is down there still, dreaming the day we burned, a thousand years of it, alone, digesting. We named it the way you name a guest you will have to face someday: the Vigil. Go to it before the Grand Tithe closes the valves and nine domes shear their year into one hungry fire, and go *not as a harvester* — it has eaten harvesters; they arrive with funnels and it eats the funnels — go as the hands that were braided by a woman who taught it, at the end, that something can be returned. I could not carry what I found back. It leans, my dawn-daughter, and it has been leaning longer than the domes, and it leans toward the glass, and the glass is *thin* at the crossroads, under the ribs, where the Kindling keeps their amber. You will know the place by the sound of a thousand years of morning turning over in its sleep. Bring the boy with the knives and the rebel with the seal and — yes — the tall coward who held the funnel, because he will want to die correctly and that is a thing I always respected in him. Burn this leaf after. The Ledger can no longer read what it cannot see, and there is one line I have kept out of ten thousand glass doors, and it is the line that says: her name is Isolde, she is my dawn-daughter, she counted everything, and she is coming, and it is* ***almost*** *time.*

The Kindling burned the leaf in a floor-lamp, which was the closest thing to a church the Ashlands had, and the amber leapt to read it and then settled, and Isolde stood in the thinning smoke with a thousand listening walls around her and one more than she could count — *fourteen*, she said quietly, and Tam, beside her, grinned into the flame with enormous relief, because the counting kind cannot stop — and from the door of the dead-ward a voice came, low, with a burn-scar on it, a voice Isolde had once heard in a cellar under the Ninth telling forty-one people what they were worth:

"You came to the wrong crossroads, Lampbearer." Mirelle Dax stood in the amber dark in a runner's furs, and the scar climbed her neck like ivy, and her arms were folded, and the whole room of the Kindling watched her the way the Unlit watch everything they love, which is to say *sideways, and ready*. "The Vigil is three weeks' down-spiral from here, in the deep black, where the glass is thin as a promise and *something older than the domes* keeps its eye on it, and we have lost eleven runners in two years learning that the walls lean for a reason. You have walked a hundred miles since the turnips, you are her hand in her coat, and my people have kept a fire lit at your crossroads for a woman I never met who taught them all how to listen." She crossed the room and took both her hands — *palm to palm*, of course; everything she touches is palm to palm, Isolde thought, and then wondered, later, why she had thought it — and said, in the voice of a locked door deciding:

"The Grand Tithe is in six weeks. The dark is three weeks' walk, and the way in is through a wall the whole Court thinks is *solid*, and I do not lose runners for ideas. But I have been keeping this one since I was sixteen, and you are standing in it, and my hands have remembered your name for eight years." The scarred woman's eyes came up and met hers and did not let go. "So. Dawn Hall wants your mother's flame. The Court wants nine domes' year. The thing in the black wants what it ate back. And you, Lampbearer — you, who walked out of the rite with it open — *what do you want?* Before I burn my runners' road for you, tell me what you want, and say it like you mean the ledger, because I have heard the flat voice your whole life, across every room in this dome, and it is the only honest one I have ever—"

The floor-lamp leaned.

Not flickered. Not the draft. *Leaned*, out of its tin, out of its bracket, amber and enormous and *processional*, toward the stair that went down under the ice, and every wall of the Kindling answered it — ten thousand hand-marks blooming white with rime at once, a sigh of frost through two generations of listening — and from somewhere very far beneath, the way a sleeper stirs when the morning is coming, came an answer to the question Mirelle had not finished asking: a *sound*. Not a bell being dragged. Something worse. Something better. The sound a hand makes on a lamp-glass, the size of the world, on the *inside*.

"See, *lamp-girl*," Tam said, in the enormous quiet, grinning like weather at her, holding out the door-knife like a bouquet. "It *leans for you*. Answer her. Say it in the flat voice. *What do you want?*"

---

## Chapter 10 · What the Flat Voice Wanted

"I want to learn how to give something back."

Isolde said it in the flat voice, the way Mirelle had asked her to, the way she read names off beds, and the whole Kindling heard it the way the Kindling heard everything — sideways, and ready. "I have carried things my whole life. Sea-water for a dead woman, years for a rent, a whole monk folded up for a Lord Examiner who couldn't tell me to my face. I have been a *door*, and a door is only a hole that other people's things walk through. I want, for the first time since I was seven — I want to be one of the things that *stays*."

Mirelle held her hands a second longer, and let go, and turned to the room with the brisk brutality of a woman choosing not to be soft in front of her people, which — Isolde was beginning to learn — was the only language the Unlit had for love.

"Then you'll want to move," she said. "The down-spiral leaves on the turning-lamp, which is an hour ago, and I do not wait for griefs, mine or other people's. Hask — four runners, the long road, no fires we cannot put out with a boot. Boy — you and your *twelve* can come, since you are the only thing out there that the dark has never managed to eat, which I *will* ask you about, later, in a jar-less room."

"I come free," Tam said, with the last dignity of the absolutely correct, "and you'll get the story free, and it comes in pieces, dome-woman, like everything good and like most furniture."

They went down.

That is the whole geography of the deep Ashlands, once you get past the ribs: *down*. The blow-spirals run between the domes like the seams of an enormous shell, and where they cross, the frost stands up tall, and under the crossings the ice goes *old*, and old ice lets runners cut stairs into it, and the stairs go down at the angle of a hill you could walk but would not choose to, and every four hours there is a waystation hollowed from the ice with a dead lamp in it — an honest, legal, *retired* hearth-lamp, donated by some dome family whose ancestor walked this road and came back — and at each station Mirelle lit hers from it, and moved on, and never lingered in the warm room like the others, and Isolde understood, in the arithmetic of the blue hour, that the woman was afraid of the amber. That a people who kept everything in their hands might have to keep this particular road, and its count of the lost, in hers.

Mirelle taught her the third motion on the second day, in a waystation with a retired lamp in it and nobody else awake, because — she said — a thing learned in front of people who are counting never gets learned. It only gets performed.

She set a jar on the ice-table between them. Small, sealed, labelled in a handwriting older than any dome: *Sable. First Dome. A winter morning, one dog, freely kept.* Isolde read the tag out loud, because out here you read everything out loud or it does not exist, and then she did what she was told. Thumb at the stopper. Palm to glass. And the shape came up into her hands off the little bell-sound of the seal: a cold yard, a dog the size of a loaf, and a *welcome* in a kitchen where nobody had ever owed anybody anything.

"Good," Mirelle said. "Now give it back."

Isolde opened her fingers, and it did not go.

It sat in her palm with nowhere to go, the way a harvested year sits in a Lampbearer's coat the whole way home. She tried the push, and the push is a *taking* motion. She tried the ask, and the ask is a *giving-away* motion that needs somebody standing on the other side of your hands. There was nobody on the other side. There was a jar, and a wall, and a retired lamp, and not one of them could reach into a person and take a thing *out*. The jar stayed empty. The shape stayed in her, warm and homeless, the size of a dog.

"*Ah*," Mirelle said, with no surprise in it whatever, and an hour later Isolde understood that the woman had known exactly how this was going to end and had made her feel it in her own hand instead of being told, which is the only way the Unlit teach anything. "There it is. That is the disease, and you carry it professionally and do not know it yet." She took the jar and put the shape back in with three patient movements — palm, wall, stopper — the way a lamp is laid down between two people at a bedside. "Your domes train a thousand hands to *harvest*. The Charter trains them to *take*. The rite trains them to *hold*, and hold, and hold, and *not one motion in the whole trade goes the other way*. You have never in seventeen years put a shape back where it came from. Show me."

And Isolde could not. Not one. Not the sea, not the years, not the monk: she had four hundred and eleven harvests riding in her coat like passengers, and the training of the Ninth, and the hands of the best harvester alive, and the third motion was simply not in her — and the strange part, the part she filed at the time and did not understand until the end of the road, was that she had never once noticed it was missing. Nobody in the domes ever had. You do not miss a verb.

"Every year I ask a Lampbearer on that stair," Mirelle said, sealing the little winter morning back into its glass, "and every year one comes down it with full hands and says *I don't know how*. In six weeks we walk to the Vigil to find out whether it can be *taught* at all. So know it tonight, before the dark gets a vote: you are not slow. You are *untrained*. There is one verb missing out of the whole trade, and it is the smallest one, and it is the only one that has ever mattered."

That is what the road took off Isolde Vane on the second day, and she did not know it had been taken: she had come down nine domes of ice looking for a way to give her mother back, and the dark had answered her, kindly, in a cold room, with the plain news that she did not yet have hands for it.

The dark, Tam told her on the fourth day — the dark out here has *seasons* — was the thing no dome-child could ever learn from a picture. Not a color. Not an absence. A *weather*: thin dark near the glass, where you could still see your own coat; fat dark under the ribs, where the ice creaked like a settling house; and down-spiral dark, the deep kind, which the Kindling called *the long morning-lessness*, and which the runners, being polite about monsters, called simply *the quiet*, because it *listened*. The jars, Tam said, tapping his forty, were for it: seal a lamp-shape into glass before the quiet teaches your nose to forget yours.

"You kept my hole's weather," Isolde said, on the fifth day, watching a runner unseal a jar at the head of a sleeping girl and press it to her wrist, and the girl's shivering stop, because somewhere in the glass was the *idea* of a plum-lamp and a cat and a boy's bad temper, and her body believed the idea the way bodies believe anything old.

"Every jar a keep," Tam said. "Dome-kin think we're carrying *nothing*. We're carrying *everybody's nothing*, properly labelled, that's more than your ledgers ever—"

And that was the fifth day, and on the sixth morning they came to the first memorial, and nobody spoke for an hour.

It was a lamp laid face-down in the ice at the head of the stair, glass side *into* the wall, the way you lay a hand on a forehead. Under it, in the frost, someone had scratched the marks of a runner's tally — eleven strokes, and then a twelve, joined — and beside them, a shape that needed no tally, no jar, no label, because the dark out here keeps its own records in the only ink it has: footprints. Dozens of them. Going *down*. And not one, anywhere on the whole wide stair, in all the other direction.

"Runners," Hask said, and did not finish, because the footprints were not boots.

They were bare feet. And they were *processional* — close, single-file, patient, the stride length of people who had stopped choosing where they were going and started being *taken*.

Isolde knelt at the head of the stair, because she was the only soul in the column whose whole trade had been reading people by their marks, and eleven lamps' worth of bare feet was a ward list to her. She counted, because the counting kind cannot stop, and the counting handed her back the one thing she had walked nine domes of ice to avoid: on the seventh row of prints every left foot turned out at the toe, a little, the way a door hangs on one bad hinge — and she had laid the harvest-hands of a man who walked like that. Bed nine, the Fourth Dome, an osier-worker with four years of rent on his keep and a cough nobody had time for, finished in a cold ward while a bell dragged outside; she had said his name for the rite and forgotten it, as trained, forty minutes afterward. He had cost her a *nothing*, and the nothing had now walked the length of nine domes.

She said none of it, out of the professional habit that keeps a Lampbearer pleasant, and Tam, who had learned everything the Ashlands could teach in the smallest sentence available, looked at her face, looked at the turned-out toes, and did the arithmetic for her because she could not do it herself.

"That one's *yours*," the boy said, not unkindly, and did not say another word about it, and put his palm flat on the ice between the prints of two strangers the way a person lays a stone on a grave. And Isolde Vane opened her case in the forty lamps' silence and wrote the count in the margin, in the flat careful hand of an officer filing a loss she had no right to file:

*Item — one (1) gait, Fourth Dome, bed nine, osier-worker, identified on the eleventh stair by the officer who finished him; not returned; carried onward.*

"The standing-folk face outward," Mirelle said quietly, kneeling at the head of the stair with her scarred hand flat on the ice, reading the weather of it the way Isolde had once read a ledger, and Isolde watched her mentor's-mentor go pale under the ash-grey, and heard the thing the woman was reading out loud in the flat professional voice of a Lampbearer, which was the *only* voice anyone out here had for grief:

"They aren't being eaten, Hask. That's what the Court believes and what we told the recruits for two years, and it is *wrong*, and I want the wrongness off my road, tonight: they're being *harvested*. Something down there is *taking them with the rite*. Look at the stride — nobody is dragged in that stride. Those are the drained, walking the length of nine domes' exile to be *finished*, and if they walk willingly —" and she looked up, at the stair going down into the quiet, at the processional bare feet, at the eleven-lamp memorials that every runner who had ever gone down had laid, face-first, into the wall "— *if they walk willingly, then the thing at the bottom knows the difference between a tithe and a theft, and we have been calling it a monster for eleven runners of our own, and it is something worse than that.*"

It is something worse than that.

It is, Isolde thought, following the woman down the stair with her empty case swinging and her dead rite in her pocket — *it is a harvester*. And she had never met one she couldn't read, and she had never been on the wrong side of the funnel, and somewhere far below them, in the long morning-lessness, something that had been digesting the sun for a thousand years turned over in its sleep, *leaned toward the glass* — and waited for them the way a good Lampbearer waits:

patiently, and with the room already prepared.

---

## Chapter 11 · The Procession

They found the procession at the bottom of the eleventh stair, where the quiet gets a floor.

The runners smelled it first — Hask put up a fist and the whole column stopped, and forty lamps leaned together toward the dark ahead like wheat, and in the leaning Isolde heard it: the sound a crowd makes when it has stopped making everything else. No talking. No coughing. Just the soft arithmetic of bare feet on ice, patient, single-file, going down, and — a thing no dome had ever let her hear, because domes do not let you hear the drained for longer than a glance — *breathing*. Dozens of people breathing together in the dark like one enormous sleeper, and not one of them facing the light.

"There," Mirelle whispered, and her hand found the ice wall, flat, reading, and Isolde watched the woman's scar go the color of old fire, which is the way it looks when she is not the locked door and is instead the thing behind it. "Second coming of the season. The Court's census moved early — the bells would have rung in nine domes and nine domes spat out that night's overflow, and our *long road* got longer, and the walkers got to walking, and they are *here*, Hask — they are *here* and they are *well* and that is the worst news of my life, because it means the thing below takes them the way the flame takes a tithe."

Isolde walked out into the procession because she was the only one of them who had ever stood at four hundred and eleven bedsides, and somebody in that line needed a Lampbearer, even — *especially* — now.

They passed her like water past a stone. Hollowed, drained, exiled: coats the color of nothing, faces the color of pages, hands — she counted, because the counting kind cannot stop, she counted fourteen before her own professional soul went out like a snuffed wick and she forgot the number forever, because at the fourteenth place in the line she found what she had sold in the Ninth Dome nine days ago in a cold ward with a bell dragging outside, and what she had sold was *walking*.

She tried, on the first three, the thing she was actually for. She laid a palm on a sleeve and went to read the shape, the way she had read nine hundred walls since the cellar, and got *nothing* — and the nothing had a texture she had never met in seventeen years of texture. Not a blank wall, which is a wall that kept nothing. Not a sealed one, which is a wall that will not tell. A wall that has been *opened, emptied, and left standing*: a house with the door off. The drained keep no shapes. They cannot be read, cannot be asked, cannot be harvested a second time, and cannot be given back anything, because the whole trade runs on a person having *something in them to work on*, and the Court had spent a thousand years building a machine whose waste product was *a person with nothing in them to work on* — and Isolde Vane had walked past four hundred and eleven of the things she had made with her own hands, in nine domes, in seventeen years, and had never once asked what they could still *do*.

What they could still do was lean.

And because Isolde Vane had been trained since she was seven to turn a mystery into a *test* before she turned it into a feeling, she ran one, right there in the middle of the long road, with Hask holding the lamp at her shoulder and the column stopped behind her and four hundred and twelve people walking down past her knees.

She did nine. She picked them the way you pick a ward list — old, young, the ones whose coats still had a dome's cut to them — and she stood in front of each one and used, in order, every voice and every word the trade had ever given her. The flat voice at a bedside: nothing. The four words of the rite, said *at* them instead of *over* them: nothing. The naming — dome, year, the surname off Hask's census slate, read out loud by a woman who had never once in her life said a drained person's name to their face: nothing, and that one she had to do twice, because her voice broke on the third name and Hask, without looking up from the lamp, held the slate steadier. The ward-call she used for the tithe list: nothing. The payment words, the coin words, the words that open a door in the Ninth because everybody in the Ninth has said them at a counter: nothing.

Nine people, nine professional approaches, nine pairs of feet that did not stop.

And then she made the mistake that told her the truth, and the mistake was that she got *hungry*. There was a paper wrap of bread-and-ash in her coat, the last thing she had bought with a legitimate coin in a legitimate dome, and the ninth walker was a woman whose hands were still, after everything, making the motion of rolling something into something else, over and over, on the inside of her own sleeves, and Isolde looked at those hands and thought *that is a bakehouse* before she thought anything at all, and she held out the bread without deciding to.

The walking stopped.

Not the procession. One woman, one step, one beat of the feet going and then not going, and her blank face came around — not to the bread, not to Isolde — to the *hand*, and the hand was empty already, and the fingers opened on it like a door on nothing, and she let it come into her, and stood there with the bread in her palm, doing nothing with it, and then, after a while, walked on. And whatever she had been about to do with it, she never did; she carried it down eleven stairs in her fist; and Isolde would think about that, later, for years, because it was the only honest result in the whole test: the woman had not wanted the bread. The woman had wanted the *letting-go* of it.

She did it again, four ways, on the tenth, eleventh, twelfth, and thirteenth walkers, because she was a professional and this is what professionals do with a fact that hurts them. She *offered*, and it worked. She *sold* — she put the bread in a man's hand and closed his fingers on it and said *that will be a penny*, and the man walked on with the bread and did not know he had been given anything and did not know he had been charged, and the charge had no effect on him whatever, because a price is a thing you put *in* a person, and there was nothing in him to put it in. She *gave it with a label* — *this is from the Ninth, this is mine, I am letting you hold it* — and that one got a lean and lost it again, half a step, like a hand let off a rope too early. And she *gave it and asked for it back*, and the walker simply stopped, and stood, and turned, very slowly, toward the place where somebody had wanted a thing back, and could not find it, and walked on, and Isolde put her hand up against the ice and had to stand there a minute with her face in it.

*Item — the rule, established on the eleventh stair by a Lampbearer of the Ninth in the last census year, nine trials and four controls, and reported in the flat voice at a bedside, in front of a column of the Kindling, in a tone the whole road found insufferable, because the tone was hers: the drained are not empty. The drained are* aimed. *There is no motion left in them that goes into a person — nothing can be put in them, kept in them, sold to them, or returned to them — and there is exactly one motion left in them that goes the other way, which is the lean, and the lean turns, always and only, toward a hand that is letting something go and does not want it back.*

*Item — the corollary, which nobody in the column said out loud for the space of eleven counts, and which Tam Ovre then said:* she has never once in her whole life done that *then*. Out here, in front of them, in the last census year, with a bread wrap in one hand and the whole of the finest harvester alive in the other, in the flat voice: *four hundred and eleven harvests, and every one of them a transfer, and not one of them a letting-go, and the dead in front of me have been standing in a line for a thousand years waiting for somebody to find out the difference.*

*Item — Kindling interfered: none, by the law of the domes, and the law is Mirelle's, and the law is* nothing goes out around me, *which out here means something specific and awful: you do not stop a walker. A walker has one motion left and it is theirs, and taking it off them is not mercy, it is a second harvest, and there is nobody breathing in nine domes with the standing to do it a second time. What you may do — and the Sixth does it, in the long dark, in the season when the bells come in heavy, and nobody has ever written it down because there is no column — is walk a stretch of the line beside somebody, hands out of pockets, and go back up when they have gone down past you.*

Tam did his own version, because Tam did everything his own version. He set a knife on the ice in front of a walker's feet, and the feet walked over it. He held the same knife out, hilt first, and the walker came on and did not take it, and did not see it, and went down past him; and then he put the knife *down*, and stepped back, and *left it there*, and turned his head away like a boy who was pretending not to be watching, and the twenty-second pair of feet in the line stopped, and turned, and came back up the stair two steps, and the hands went down and got the knife, and went on. And Tam stood up, and spat in the dark, which was how the Ashlands said a prayer, and said: "That's the whole thing, isn't it. They aren't *empty* people. They're *finished* people. You cannot put a thing into a thing that's finished. You can only put it *past* them, and hope they're still the kind of person who picks stuff up off a floor."

"That is not a *theology*," Hask said.

"It's a *knife*," Tam said. "Theology's for people who can't afford a knife."

Old Maren. The sea gone, the husband gone, the shape gone — expelled, apparently, the way you expel a used bandage, on the night her rent finally ate her — and hollowed, and *processional*, and barefoot on the ice, and when Isolde stepped into her lane and said her name, level, flat, at a bed, in the only voice left in the world — *Maren* — the old woman's head turned.

Not toward her. Toward her *hands*.

And that was not nothing, runners, and she had already proved what it was on the eleventh stair: she had said a name, out loud, to a woman who could never in this world or the next pay her for it, and the trade has no word for that, and the word she used for it, later, at a bedside, in the flat voice, was *the only payment I have ever made*. The test said the lean turns toward a hand letting go; the test had never said it had to be a hand full of bread.

The drained do not look. The drained do not answer. But the hands — the flame goes through them every night of every life, in every lamp that is carried, in every bracket that is trimmed, and hands remember best of all — Maren's hands came up out of her coat with the speed of a woman answering a door, and opened, palm to palm, the way the dying press hands at funerals, and met Isolde's, and held, and — Isolde felt it, felt *everything*, felt the whole of her career come open like the fourth wedge — the hands *leaned*. Not the memory. Never the memory. The shape: a sea, salt-light, moving; a husband; thirty-one years; a roof; a *gratitude* so enormous that it had survived being eaten twice, because it had never been a memory at all, it had been a *direction*, the way a needle's north is not a place, and the old woman's blank face went on looking past her, down the stair, at the bottom, and her hands went on holding, and would not let go, and could not, and the procession walked *her* on, and her arms came out of Isolde's like a rope paid out by a sailor who had other work to do, and the last of it the woman felt, of everything, of the whole of a life, was the shape of a stranger's hands *not-shaking*.

Isolde stood in the middle of the processional and did, at last, the one thing four hundred and eleven bedsides had not taught her, which was to be the room on the other side of the door, and she made a sound like a bell being dragged, and it was a long time before she heard the small, absurd, perfect voice beside her knee say:

"Right. That's the one I'm *jar-ring*."

Tam had crouched at the frost where Maren's hands had come up, and he had his biggest empty jar off his sledge, and he was scraping the rime of a dead woman's grip into glass with the door-knife and the focused love of a boy doing the only job the Ashlands ever taught him. "Hold still, dome-woman, it's got *three* leans in it, that's a *keep* of a weather, best I've got since the cat—" and then, because he was thirteen and the thing had arrived in him sideways the way the good things arrive: "— she paid you in *grass* and you came down the quiet after her to be *thanked*, and out here nobody thanks, lamp-girl, out here we *label*." And he pressed the stopper in, and wrote on the tag in the dark, by feel, and put the jar in her coat pocket over her heart, and did not look at her again for two days, which was the whole of his mercy and most of his grief.

They camped that night in the lee of a blow-spiral mouth, above the procession's noise, with all forty lamps laid on the wall of the rock facing outward, which was the Kindling's way of saying *we know you're there*, and Mirelle came and sat down beside Isolde in the dark, and did not say *it's all right*, and did not say *she's at peace*, and said instead, in the voice of a locked door, the thing Isolde needed to hear more than any kindness:

"You did the rite properly, at the end. You were a door. Don't let anybody out here tell you that wasn't the bravest thing on this road, because *I* will not, and my people keep their own dead in jars and we are *experts* on what it costs to be brave toward the taken." A pause. The lamps leaned. "Give me your hand."

Isolde gave it, and Mirelle pressed it flat against the rock, palm down, and kept her own scarred hand over the back of it, and said: "Now *stop being a Lampbearer* for one minute. No funnel, no rite, no report. Just the wall, and the weather it kept, and my hand being very interested in whether you can feel—" and Isolde felt it, of course she felt it, she had felt it since the cellar and had reported it to nobody her whole professional life, the rock *humming*, low, patient, a thousand hearth-lamps' worth of old warm shapes moving through the stone toward the deep like a river under ice — and she said, in the flat voice, because it was the only honest one:

"It's all going *down*."

"Yes," said Mirelle, whose hand had not moved, whose hand was not moving, whose *entire body* had gone very still and very warm above her hand in a way that had nothing to do with the quiet. "Everything the fire takes, the walls keep; and everything the walls keep, Isolde — *everything* — has been draining, for a thousand years, down toward that." And she turned her face away, which was the Unlit for *go*, and stood, and buttoned her furs against the deep, and left a woman sitting in the dark with a dead stranger's weather over her heart and a hand still warm where the living had pressed it, and at the far edge of the camp Hask said, quietly, the way men say the weather:

"Every lamp's leaning the same way now. Even the dead ones."

And Hask, who had kept slates for eleven years and had never once been seen to be late, wrote the item that night by a lamp that leaned, in the flat careful hand of a man who has been asked to count an unfunny thing and intends to count it properly:

*Item — eleventh stair, second coming of this season: procession counted at four hundred and twelve feet, single-file, outward-bound, unhindered. Kindling engaged: none. Kindling interfered: none, by the law of the domes. Kindling witnessed: all of us.*

*Note — there is no column in this ledger for the four hundred and twelve, and I have asked, and the answer out of nine domes is that there is no column because there is nothing left to count. I am writing the number anyway, and putting it in the keeps box with the jars, so that somebody, someday, is embarrassed by a number instead of by a wall.*

Isolde read it over his shoulder in the amber and did not say *thank you*, because the road had taught her already that the Unlit do not thank. She put her hand flat on the slate beside his, palm down, which is how, out here, you sign a thing you cannot change and will not forget.

---

## Chapter 12 · The Boy the Dark Keeps

The answer to the boy came for free, on the twelfth day, the way the worst truths do: out of a crisis, in a single sentence, from a mouth that had never been asked.

The crisis was a fall. A waystation roof — two hundred years of honest ice, tired of holding a stair — let go over the camp at the noon turning, and four runners and a Lampbearer and a boy went down with it into a drift-chasm in the dark, and what the dark of the deep *is*, Isolde learned the instant they landed, because it stood up. Not Hollowed. Not processional. A *colony* of the drained, hundreds, settled in the chasm's shade the way crickets settle in the lee of a porch-light — except the light here was the terrified humans, and the drained did not come at them.

They came *around* them.

Slowly, in the dark, in the enormous patience of the taken, they ringed the huddled runners — and past the runners — and *around* the small brown boy in the middle of it all who had gone white under the ash-grey and was not, Isolde saw with a Lampbearer's eye, *shaking at all*: same not-shaking, she had called it, weeks ago, in a warm hole, and the hole had just answered her. The drained closed on Tam Ovre the way a flock closes on a weather, and one of them — a woman with nothing in her face and the seal of the Sixth Dome rotted on her collar — reached out and took the boy's hand, palm to palm, and *held*, and every other pair of hands in the dark went with it, and the whole ring breathed together, and Tam said, in the small tired voice of a thirteen-year-old explaining a thing he had been hoping nobody would ever make him say out loud:

"They're *keeping* me. They keep everybody down here, lamp-girl. That's what I *am*."

They dug out. They got the runners up and the lamps counted and the sledge recovered, and nobody talked, all the rest of that day, because there was a thing coming and everyone with ears could hear it coming; and at the noon turning on the thirteenth day, in the lee of the last stair before the deep, the boy sat down on his own sledge, with his eleven-roll in his lap and his twelfth knife across his knees — the one with no mark, worn at one spot by a hand — and told the Ashlands' only real ghost-story with the focused pride of a child showing off a scar.

"I wasn't *from* nowhere, dome-woman," he said, to Mirelle, who had asked the jar-less-room question four days ago and had been patiently *not* asking it since. "I was *sent* to nowhere. Born down in the quiet, in a keep with a good roof and a bad cough, to a woman who died the same week the thing with the jars did — my mother — and the standing-folk got her, like they get everybody down there eventually, and they stood round the keep and would not *leave*, and then a lady came down the long road, and she did the thing, and she stood proper, and she saw the ring, and she said the words the standing-folk have been saying to me ever since, which are the *only* reason I'm alive to be *rude* about it." He held up the twelfth knife, and the dark rang like glass for three full seconds in apology. "She made this *for* me out of a service-tube seal, and she taught me the eleven *jobs*, and she said: *the drained cannot take what was never given, and you, little scavenger, were never anyone's — not a dome's, not a Court's, not even your mother's, because she gave you to the dark freely, and the dark keeps what light spends* — and then she went down the last stair, and the ring let her pass, and it has kept me for me, ever since, and I am *thirteen*, and the standing-folk are the only family that never once asked me my *name* like it was a *debt*—"

The ring, at that, made the sound. Isolde would carry the description of it, later, to her grave: the soft arithmetic of hundreds of bare feet shifting, all at once, the way a congregation says *amen*; and every face in the dark turned — not toward the light, which was wrong, which had always been wrong; toward the *deep*, which was the rite done backward, the sentinels facing *home*.

"She went down," Mirelle said quietly, on the last stair, when the boy had finished and the whole column had walked a mile in one continuous held breath. "The woman who made the knife. She went down the last stair, and the ice gave her back to nobody, and the lamps you keep at the crossroads, Tam — the cat lamp, the one that has been burning twenty years —"

"It's not *my* lamp, dome-woman, it's *hers*, I keep it because the ring won't let it go out and I *tried* to let it go out, that's the whole *story*—"

And then Mirelle made him say the jobs, because Mirelle made everybody say the jobs — every recruit of the Kindling recited them at the stair-foot before their first downward, and had done so for two generations, and there is not a page of the Charter in nine domes that is worth the eleven plain lines the boy rattled off that day with his sledge under him and his knife-roll in his lap, annoyed at being asked and delighted to be asked, runners, because it is the whole law of how to live in a house with the taken in it:

*One — you do not run. Running is a taking motion, and they have been taken their whole life.*

*Two — you do not call to them by name. Their names are not in the dark, and shouting hands them an idea of you that you cannot take back.*

*Three — if they ring you, do nothing at all, and do it patiently.*

*Four — if one takes your hand, let it. A held hand has never once cost anybody anything.*

*Five — you do not light a lamp AT them. You light it facing them. That is not the same manner, and the whole difference between a dome and a road is in that one line.*

*Six — you say out loud what you are carrying, and you say it flat, because they cannot read you, and they are entitled to be told.*

*Seven — you never take a shape off a wall that has a label on it, and if it has no label you leave it, because the dark is somebody's cupboard.*

*Eight — if you are sick, you say so at the stair-foot before you go down, and you sit down, and the column goes on without you, and nobody in this world is permitted to call that a loss.*

*Nine — you keep one jar empty, always, and you fill it on the road, and you do not ask what for, and one day you will know.*

*Ten — when you come up you count, and when you have counted you count again, and the second count is the honest one.*

*Eleven —* and here the boy stopped, and looked at the four runners, and at the locked door, and at a woman in a dead woman's coat with her whole trade in her pockets, and said it slower, because eleven was the one the standing-folk had had to explain to him himself, in the deep, on the night his mother went under. *Eleven: if you get to the end of your walking, and somebody's hand is on yours, let them hold you. You will go further than your legs ever did.*

Nobody said anything for a while. The eleventh was the one that got Mirelle Dax, and it got her the way things get a locked door — unnoticed, filed, and produced years later at a rail in the gold — and the sixth was the one that got Isolde, because the sixth was written for her trade, and she had been walking six weeks inside a rule she had never heard stated and had never once kept.

"Six," Mirelle said. "Your turn, Lampbearer. You have walked my whole road with a case full of other people's past, and my runners have passed you eleven times a day not knowing whether you are carrying a fortune or a corpse, and I have not asked you, and the dark does not care what you are not asked. Say it. Out loud. Flat. To the ring."

And so at the foot of the last stair, in front of forty lamps and four runners and a boy with a knife made out of a service-tube seal, Isolde Vane set down her case, opened the coat, and itemized herself in the voice she had only ever used at bedsides: *sea-water, one (1), old and salt-light, taken off a woman in the Ninth who paid in grass. Years, nine (9), a family of the low rows, for a rent, taken off hands that were held down. One (1) monk, folded, taken off a Brother of the Order of the Book for a Lord Examiner who could not say so to his face. Four hundred and eleven (411), total, in seventeen years, and I have never once told anybody the number out loud in front of the people who had to walk past me carrying it.*

It was the worst minute of her whole professional life, and the relief of it — the plain, unprofessional, enormous relief of a person who has finally put a loaded cart down in front of witnesses — was the first thing on the road the trade had not trained her for. The second was what happened next: the whole ring of the taken on the last stair, hundreds deep, *leaned toward her at once*, the way a field leans to one wind, and it had nothing to do with her coat and everything to do with the fact that she had said it — and Tam Ovre, in the smallest possible sentence, with frost on his face and absolutely nothing about it, said: "Right. *Now* you're Kindling."

The story went out of them, all of it, all argument, at the bottom of the last stair.

Because the deep quiet is not quiet. The deep quiet *breathes*: the whole floor of the world there is one enormous slow rhythm, in and out, in and out, a thousand years of it, and Mirelle's forty lamps came up to amber at once without being touched, and the drained on the last stair let go of the runners they were holding, and sat — all of them, the whole procession of nine domes' exile, in the only gesture Isolde had ever seen the taken make *with a purpose* — they *sat*, facing the deep, like the front row at a service, and Tam Ovre took his twelfth knife and handed it, handle first, to a Lampbearer in a dead woman's coat, because he understood — the boy understood everything first, he was *born* understanding — that what was coming needed two people holding the same thing, and the only two on that stair who had ever been *braided* were the one with the grass-smell and the one with the knife.

Ahead of them, at the end of the last stair, the glass of the world was *thin*.

You could not see it; that was the wrong word; the dark ahead simply *ran out* — into a smoothness that breathed, a wall with no far side, and laid across the whole of that enormous nothing, at exactly the height of a hand reaching up to wake somebody gently, was a *handmark* in rime. Five fingers. Each the size of a dome corridor. Pressed flat against the inside of the glass of the world, patient, warm through the frost, and leaning — the way the Ledger leaned, the way the lamps leaned, the way the old woman's hands had leaned, the way everything that has been taken learns, at last, to lean —

*toward them*.

And from the other side of the thin glass, in a voice made of a thousand stolen memories all agreeing, for once, to say the same word at the same time, the Vigil — the sleeper, the shepherd, the guest they had named the way you name someone you will have to face someday — asked the two of them, and only them, the question the whole road had been for:

"**WHO IS THERE, AND WHAT DID YOU BRING ME.**"

---

## Chapter 13 · The Dream of Day

"I am Isolde Vane," said Isolde, in the flat voice, at the glass of the world, "and I brought nothing. I came to give something back. That is the whole of what I have, and most of it is a debt."

The handmark in the rime warmed.

That is the only honest way to say what happened: the *frost* of it — a thousand years of the taken's own breath, laid in fingers the size of corridors — went warm from the center out, the way a palm goes warm when it is glad, and the thin glass stopped being a wall and became, instead, what the walls have always been trying to do: *a window*, and on the other side of it the dark *opened*, the way an eye opens, the way a day opens—

And the party of the last stair — forty lamps, the four runners, one boy, one locked door, one dead rite — stood, all of them, at once, inside the thing the domes had eaten.

*Day*.

There is no word, in the vocabulary of the taken, for what it is to *see* it, because the word was burned with the thing, and every one of them understood, weeping in the enormous garden of it without knowing why, that they were not weeping at the sight — they were weeping at the *temperature on the skin*, the weightless weight of it, the way the light came not *from a flame* but from *everywhere*, everywhere at once, like being forgiven by the whole sky. A sun.
