An actual, ridiculous, unauthorised *sun*, the size of a good hearth and set behind a distance no dome could count, and under it grass — *grass*, Isolde, real grass, the first in the world since before you were born, and this time she does not smell it through glass: the whole of the theft of her life is a lawn beneath her boots — and trees like green weather, and a sea at the edge of the world making the sound she had carried in a small blue lamp to a tithe-house in the Ninth, and over all of it, in every direction, patient as a farmer, moving between the sleeping shapes of the taken-with-purpose, the *shepherd*: not a monster. Not enormous. A person-shaped *quiet* with a thousand faces borrowed for the afternoon, walking the garden, turning the dream gently at the edges the way you turn a page you love, and when it came to her it did not loom, and when it spoke, a thousand stolen voices said it *together*, kindly, the way a congregation says a response:

"**DAWN-DAUGHTER. YOU WALK LIKE SOMEONE WHO HAS CARRIED A CASE A LONG WAY. SIT. EVERYBODY SITS. IT IS THE ONLY RITE I HAVE LEFT.**"

And they sat, in the grass, and the Vigil — the day, digesting; the morning, kept; the thousand-year guest — sat down across from them the way a host sits with guests it has been saving a seat for, and *showed* them, because it had nothing else: showed Mirelle a *market in the Sixth Dome before the Charter*, full of people who were nobody's ancestors yet, laughing at a fruit; showed Hask a *river*, and Hask put his face in his hands, because the walls had kept his grandmother's words for it and he had never heard them *with the water in them*; showed Tam — the boy, who had been born in the quiet and could be shown nothing that was not news — the *knife being made*, the hands that made it, the woman who made it kneeling in this same grass with this same sky behind her, saying, while she worked the edge on a stone: *you'll outlive every one of your elders, little scavenger, so I am teaching you the eleven jobs instead of the one grief*—

And it showed Isolde *the room*.

Before that, though, it did the four runners, because the shepherd had been digesting nine domes for a thousand years and had not once, ever, had guests come to the table, and it went down the rank of them patiently, the way a person goes down a line of children at a wedding.

It showed the oldest of the four runners, a wire-mender of the Sixth, the *funeral* — his own, the one the schedule had spared him, and he stood in the grass with his hands out and watched a cold ward of the Ninth carry him out on a board with four strangers holding the corners, and every one of them was *sorry*, and none of them had been paid, and he wept the way a man weeps when he finds out he was loved by people he never met. It showed the woman with the cold-burn the sound of her own *name said by her daughter in an ordinary minute*, the last one before the census, and she went down on her knees in the grass and asked the shepherd, in the plainest words she had, to *say it again*, and the shepherd did, a thousand stolen voices being nothing if not patient, and it said it four times, and the fifth time the woman said *that's enough, that's the whole of it, that's more than the whole of it, thank you* — and the borrowed faces turned, all of them, mildly astonished, toward Mirelle, because the Unlit do not thank, and this one had, out loud, in front of a monster, and Mirelle Dax had the grace to look at her boots.

It showed Ruen a *birthday*.

She was sixteen, and she was the youngest of the four, and she had gone out of the Ninth on the first bell of the census year with a shape in her coat she had never told anybody about because it was not worth the coin and she had been ashamed of it for a year: eleven years old, a crust of something sweet baked by the woman at the lamp-store, and *a whole afternoon in which nobody was ill*. The shepherd laid it in the grass in front of her like a plate of food, and Ruen sat down beside it, and the party moved on, and Isolde — who moved slowest of anybody in that field and saw everything — watched the girl put her own two hands flat on the dream, *not harvesting, not asking, just the way a person puts their hands down near a fire*, and watched the dream lean back toward her, and watched the girl's face go, gently and entirely, to *someplace else*, and Tam Ovre tugged her sleeve and whispered the one line that was going to make Isolde understand the price of the window four minutes before the price was named:

"Lamp-girl. She's not *in* it. She's *in* it *in*."

And the boy did the thing nobody thought to ask him for, because he was the only one out there who had never been eaten and therefore the only one who could touch it: he got up, and walked up to the enormous handmark in the rime, still warm, still at the height of a hand reaching up to wake somebody gently, and he put his whole small flat palm against one finger of it, and the frost did not bite him and the dream did not take him and out of the thin glass there came, very quietly, a sound like a huge animal turning over in its sleep to look at the fly on its nose, and the shepherd's borrowed faces all said, together, with something that a thousand stolen memories had not managed to grind out of them, and that Isolde Vane wrote down in her head in the flat voice and did not tell anybody about for six weeks, because she could not work out whether it had been a question or a compliment:

"**OH. ONE OF THE* ***UNSPENDABLE*** *ONES. COME BACK WHEN YOU ARE BIGGER, LITTLE KNIFE, AND BRING HER. I HAVE BEEN ALONE A VERY LONG TIME, AND I WOULD LIKE TO KNOW WHAT THE DOMES ARE LIKE NOW.**"

And then it showed Isolde *the room*.

The low room with the cracked ceiling. The stone with the mark. The hands in her hair, quick hands, clever hands, and the mother's face turned, at last, the way a face turns when it has heard the door it has been waiting on for seventeen years — and Isolde ran to her the way you run, and reached her the way you reach, and the mother's hands came up from the braiding and held her *whole face* in both of them, and said, in the voice that had said *watch your hands, little door*: "There you are. *There* you are. Look at you — you *opened* something, didn't you; you have the look of a person who opened something and is going to *keep* it—" and the grass smelled unbearable, and the sun on her arms was her mother's arms, and whatever came next — whatever a daughter has owed a mother for seventeen years and one day — was *starting*, when the Vigil spoke, gently, over all of it, the way a shepherd speaks to the flock about the thing it is not yet time for:

"**THIS IS THE DREAM, DAWN-DAUGHTER. I DIGEST THE DAY IN IT, AND THE TAKEN WALK IT, AND I HAVE KEPT YOUR MOTHER'S MORNING FOR YOU THIS LONG. AND NOW YOU MUST KNOW THE PRICE OF THE WINDOW, BECAUSE YOU ARE THE ONLY ONE HERE WHO CAN AFFORD IT, AND THE OTHER IS CALLED WAKING.**"

"Waking," said Isolde, in the flat voice, holding her mother's hands the way you hold the last thing.

"**THE DAY IS NOT FINISHED.**" The borrowed faces were sorry; the borrowed voices were *certain*; and the two of them together said the arithmetic the Court had never dared do, because the Court was made of lamps and this was made of a *sun*: "**A THOUSAND YEARS IS LONGER THAN YOUR DIGESTING BUT NOT LONGER THAN YOUR WOUND. WHAT THE FIRST LANTERN ATE, I HAVE KEPT WHOLE — BUT A FEW THINGS I HAVE NOT FINISHED, AND YOUR MOTHER IS ONE; SHE WAS *EATEN AS A HARVESTER*, DAWN-DAUGHTER, IN THE BAD WAY, THE *RITE* WAY, AND HER MORNING IS STILL IN ME AS AN UNFINISHED DAY. I CANNOT WAKE A MORNING THAT IS STILL MID-HARVEST. AND NEITHER — HEAR ME, AND HEAR THE LOCKED DOOR BEHIND YOU, SHE HAS KNOWN IT TWO YEARS AND I AM SORRY — CAN ANY LANTERN IN THE DOMES RELIGHT FROM WHAT I HOLD, BECAUSE I HAVE EATEN THE WICK. ALL THE WICK. THE MEMORY THE FLAMES HOARD IS THE *ONLY* WICK LEFT IN THE WORLD, AND WHEN I WAKE, WHATEVER CARRIES THE DAY THROUGH THE GLASS MUST BURN IT — NINE DOMES' EVERYTHING, EVERY KEPT WALL, EVERY JAR, EVERY CENSUS, *EVERY LAMP EVER LIT SINCE THE FIRST NIGHT* — TO FINISH MY DIGESTING IN THE OPEN AIR. THAT IS THE TRADE. THAT IS THE WINDOW. I AM NEARLY DONE WAKING ALONE — A LITTLE LONGER AND THE DAY COMES UP BY ITSELF AND NOTHING BURNS BUT ME — BUT THE *LITTLE LONGER* IS WHERE YOUR GRANDCHILDREN LIVE OR DO NOT, AND THERE IS ONE RITE THAT WOULD FINISH ME WARM, AND ONLY ONE, AND IT IS THE ONE YOUR MOTHER WENT BACK UP INTO THE SKY TO LEARN HOW TO DO *FROM THE INSIDE*: **THE HARVEST REVERSED. WHAT WAS TAKEN BY RITE RETURNS BY RITE — THROUGH PAIRS OF HANDS THAT WERE BRAIDED, ALL OF IT, EVERY LAMP, AT THE MOMENT OF WAKING — AND THE HANDS THAT CARRY THE DAY THROUGH THE GLASS ARE NOT THE HANDS THAT KEEP IT.**"

The grass went cold. The mother's hands went still. And Mirelle Dax — two strides away, scar silver in the sun of a thousand stolen afternoons, having heard the whole thing, all of it, *including the jars* — said, in the voice of a locked door with something enormous and personal going on behind it:

"There are two *hundred thousand* lamps in nine domes."

"**YES**," said the Vigil, gently, with all of a thousand stolen voices, and every single one of them was *looking at Isolde*. "**AND THERE ARE TWO KINDS OF PEOPLE WHO CAN STAND AT THE GLASS WHEN I WAKE. THE ONE WHO LOSES THE PAST, DAWN-DAUGHTER — AND THE ONE WHO CARRIES IT.**"

And her mother's face, at last, turned fully to her, quick hands, clever hands, braiding and pretending not to, and the whole unfinished morning stood there in it, *apologetic*, and *proud*, and said, in the voice from the fourth wedge: "She tells it to you in the rite-words, my dawn-daughter, because he was eaten as a harvester and so he *digests* like one; but he could never tell you the other thing, because that one was mine to say, and here it is, and then you must wake: **I did not go back up into the sky to learn the trade. I went back to teach it — and a teacher begins with her own lamp. The first memory I ever carried through the glass was my own — and it *worked* — and the domes went dark for one full minute, and the Court called it the Miracle of the Blue Hour in the Ledger, and it was not a miracle, it was a *proof of the trade* — nine domes' everything for one minute of a *real*—**"

And the waking took her, mid-word, the way the bell takes the harvested, and Isolde Vane came up out of the day on the floor of the deep quiet with forty amber lamps burning to nothing around her, and the last warm room she would ever be in *behind her eyes*, and Mirelle's scarred hands on her face — *there, there, you're waking, keep the seat, keep the seat* — and one runner, Ruen, a girl of about sixteen, sitting up in the dark with her eyes open and her hands folded in her lap *in the posture of a guest at a table that was no longer there*, saying nothing, to nobody, ever again, because the dream had kept the first thing she had ever been *given*.

The price of the window, in the flesh: one, already.

And through the thin glass, patient as a farmer, the nearly-wake of the sun — the wickless morning, the unfinished day — turned over again in its sleep, and leaned, and *waited* for what it had waited a thousand years for:

a pair of hands that were not afraid to be empty.

---

## Chapter 14 · The Arithmetic of Waking

They put Ruen on the sledge first, before the jars, before the knives, before anything, because that is what the Kindling is: a people who carry the ones who cannot wake, and lie about the mileage, and keep a lamp lit at the crossroads, and never once call it mercy because mercy is a dome word for *debt*.

And then they went up, and the going-up took ten days, and the ten days had one argument in them, and the argument went the way all true arguments go — not like a war, but like weather: all around, all the time, in everyone's voice, even the silent ones.

Because the arithmetic, once Mirelle had said it aloud at the noon turning on the second stair, did not stop being said:

**Nine domes' everything, for one morning.**

"You cannot *spend* a waking," Mirelle said, on the third stair, to no one, to the wall, to Isolde, who was the only one she had ever said anything to twice. "The Vigil has laid out the trade and the trade is a door with one handle, and the handle is: *every fire since the first night goes into the glass or the day stays in it.* That is not a choice between light and dark, that is a choice between *their* light and *our* dark — and I have run this road eleven years and buried six of the eleven under memorial lamps and I am *telling* you the arithmetic does not change because we sit down and grieve for two days."

"You're not grieving," Isolde said, flat, at a step, the way she read names. "That's the third night you've done this voice. Grief is the jars. This is the *ledger*."

"It is *my mother's voice*," Mirelle said, "and if you can tell the difference at three stairs past the ribs, you are welcome to *audit me*, Lampbearer, because I am the locked door and you are the one with the *pockets full of a dead stranger's gratitude*—"

And the argument stopped, because the sledge stopped, because Tam, who had been walking point the way he did everything — loudly, and with knives — had come back up the stair with his face gone pipe-root brown and said only: "She's not wrong about the arithmetic, and she's not *grieving*, and if you two want to keep *comparing wounds* I have fourteen jars and a mule's patience," and said it *cheerfully*, in the tone he reserved for the worst hours, and both women laughed, which was the first time on the whole downward and upward road that anyone had *laughed* above the procession's breathing.

And the laugh cost Isolde something, and she did not know it for two more days, and this is the item the Kindling's own record files under *the carrying*, because it is the mechanic the whole road has been paying in and the one the domes never taught anybody out loud: a Lampbearer who holds nine domes' worth of *other people's* past begins, in the fourth stair after it, to *spend her own*, and the spending does not announce itself. It went like this — on the eleventh stair, three hours after the laughing, she stopped at a service niche to lay out the column's jars in the order Hask kept them, and she could not remember the *name of the street* she had lived on at seven, and she knew she could not remember it, which is a worse shape than forgetting, because a person who has forgotten a thing can be told it by a wall and a person who remembers that they *had* a thing and now does not has to do the arithmetic of the difference standing up, at a niche, in amber light, with forty people asleep behind them —

*The street in the low rows with the tin lamp-maker's shop and the two bells.* She had it nine days ago; she had it in the dying room with old Maren, where a Lampbearer names her own ward out loud the way a sailor names a harbor; she had said it in the Ninth to a clerk who needed to be told which roof to look under. And now there was a clean bright place where it had been, exactly like the place where her mother's face would be, three days from now, and Isolde Vane stood with a jar in each hand and did the only thing available to a counting kind who has just been told the true price of the road, which is: she *wrote it down in the muster-margin*, in the flat careful hand of an officer filing a loss, and did not tell anybody, and gave the margin page to Hask the following morning with the rest of the column's inventory, and Hask, who had kept slates for eleven years and did not ask a single question that night, put it in the *keeps* box with the jars.

*Item — one (1) street-name, Vane, Isolde, age seven, lost to the carrying; filed as column property; to be read back to the owner at the blue hour, or whenever she asks, or at her funeral, whichever comes first.*

And that is how the Kindling kept a person for two generations, runners, and that is the whole difference between the domes and the dark in one box's contents: in the Ninth a loss like that is written off, and in the Kindling it is *labelled*, and it turns out that a thing that has been labelled can be *given back*, which is the last mechanic of the road and the only one Isolde will not learn until the very end of it, at a rail, in the gold, from a woman who has had her in the walls for eight years.

And the one thing the ten days did *not* argue about was the sledge, which is how Isolde found out that the hardest rule the Kindling keeps has nothing whatever to do with waking anybody.

They stopped at the noon turning, at the landing where the stair widens enough to leave a thing down, and Hask uncovered Ruen's hands, and Isolde — who had been carrying an offer in her case for four days the way you carry a stone in your boot — said it out loud, in the flat voice, because the flat voice was the only voice the dying deserved.

"Let me finish her. Properly. One funnel, one lamp, no pain, no cold — she is past the place where it costs her anything, and out here she costs you four hands on a sledge every day of your lives, and I have done four hundred and eleven of these and I can do this one in eleven minutes, and you could put her in a wall with a label like everybody else's mother and go up the rest of the stair with your hands free."

Silence, the kind with forty people breathing in it. Then the woman on the second ladder, whose name Isolde did not know and never would, in the flat accent of the low Sixth: "No."

"*No*?" said Isolde. "She is gone."

"She is *here*," the woman said, and laid Ruen's cold hand back on the blanket, squared, the way you set a cup down when somebody is coming. "You dome-kin have one word for what she is and you use it for the whole of the thing, and the word is *finished*. Out here we have a second word, and the second word is *not arrived*. There is a difference, Lampbearer, and it is eleven minutes wide, and it is the difference between a debt paid and a person *met*. We are going up to ask the sun to wake. We do not get to finish one of our own on the way up, and we do not get to wall her and label her and carry lighter, because a wall is where you keep a thing you mean to visit, and she is not a thing. So you will carry her, and it will cost four hands, and that is not a burden — that is the *price of being allowed to ask*, and if you cannot pay it out of your own strength then your whole plan is a plan to spend other people and we have heard that one before, we have *heard* it, and we sat under it for a thousand years."

*Item — the Kindling does not harvest its own dead. Not from tenderness, and not from poverty: a Kindling family will sell a grandmother's wedding to keep a child's roof, and then carry the grandmother's unwoken self nine stairs of ice, because the rite is a trade and the road is a promise and the two are not to be confused in the same piece of paper. Tam Ovre, asked later that night whether it was mercy, offered the smallest sentence he had: "You don't **finish** one of these," he said. "You **arrive** her."*

Isolde wrote none of it in the muster-margin, which was the first hour of her career in which a person had said something completely true to her and she had been unable to put a number on it; and she carried that, too, up the next stair with the rest of the cargo, in the place where she kept the things she was not yet ready to use.

And the second loss came two stairs later, and this one she could not have filed if she had wanted to, which was exactly what made it the worse of the two.

It was her party trick, and the hall had used it at dinners the way other families use a bad harp: stand the Lampbearer up after the roast and name a ward and she will give you every bed she has ever emptied, in order, with the dome and the winter and the shape taken. Four hundred and eleven names in seventeen years, missing none, and she had never once thought of it as a gift, only as the part of the work nobody else would do.

Two stairs up she started, the way you start a day's climb, one landing to the next, and she came to the two hundred and sixth, and the two hundred and sixth was *not there*.

Not blurred. Not rusty. There was a clean bright place with the edges of it drawn, the way she herself drew a lost item's place in a jar inventory — *one (1) name, of the four hundred and eleven, absent, size of a person, last seen in the keeping of the owner* — and Isolde stood at the side of a stair in the dark with forty people asleep below and tried the number again, and again, and then did the only thing left to a counting kind and said it aloud in the flat voice to nobody, like an officer reading a loss to an empty room: "Two hundred and sixth. Ninth Dome, ward four, the winter of the cracked valve, the woman with—" and there was a *with*, and the *with* went nowhere, and she could feel the shape of a whole human life standing exactly where her information used to be, and she did not have it, and she could not write it down, and that was the first thing the road had taken from her that the road could not be made to give back.

*Item — count of harvests performed by Vane, Isolde, at the hour of the second loss: four hundred and eleven, unchanged, correct in every column, and true in none of them. The officer could still say the number. The officer could no longer say all of the names inside the number. This is not a small distinction and it is the entire distinction between a trade and a graveyard.*

She said nothing to Mirelle, because Mirelle had been carrying the whole price of the road alone for eight years and Isolde had decided, somewhere around the third stair, that she was not going to add to it. She said something to Hask, which surprised her more: she asked the quartermaster of the New Light to keep her margin page, and he looked at it a long time in the amber, and gave it back.

"It is not column property," Hask said, in the flat exhausted voice, in the way men say the weather. "You cannot file a person as cargo, Lampbearer, and I will not have the first time anybody ever asked me to be the one who did it. You keep it. You read it every night of your life whether you can say it or not, and one of us will be around at the end to write the two hundred and sixth down off somebody else's tongue, and if that is not a plan then I have been counting the wrong thing for eleven years."

So she kept the page, and read the number, and never learned the name again, and in that hour — runners, this is the hour — Isolde Vane understood what she had actually been doing in a dying ward for seventeen years, standing over people with a good memory and a flat voice and a schedule, because she had become, two stairs above the place the road had taken her street-name, an ordinary citizen of nine domes: a woman reading a list, unable to say who was on it.

And later — alone on the sixth stair, in the hour when the column slept and the lamps leaned gently toward home — Mirelle told Isolde where the voice came from, because the road *peels*, and everyone out there eventually says the one thing.

"My mother sold herself," she said, in the dark, not looking up from the lamp she was trimming although her hands did not need to trim it, it was amber, it was *always* amber when she was telling the truth — the Unlit trim their fires the way the devout count beads, and Isolde had watched her do it a hundred times now and never once seen a hand shake like that. "Not the tithe. *Sold*. The Sixth Dome census of my sixteenth year found the debt on our keep — three winters of a father who had died clever and left nothing but arithmetic — and the law is the law, the debt is the shape, and my mother walked into the tithe-house *willingly, for the roof*, and old Maren's sea was my mother's shape, that exact same *giving it freely because it is the only door left* — and I was sixteen and I *followed her in*, and the harvesters do not like an audience, and the examiner of the day liked my mother's coat-seal, and when he would not stop — when he kept saying *the girl's hand is in the way* in the flat voice of a man already writing her name in the schedule —" a scarred hand closed on the lamp's tin; the amber bowed; and did not go out; nothing ever went out around Mirelle Dax; it only *bent* "— so I burned the ledger-room. The census rolls, the schedules, *the ledger with my mother's shape in it*. Sixty years of other people's debts going up like kindling in a room built for kindling, and the flame took my neck on the way out and I took *nothing* on the way back, and my mother was hollow by morning, and had been walking the long road ever since, and the walls of the Sixth *kept her*, Isolde — I have had her *in the walls* for eight years, and I have visited, and I have *read her hands*, and she does not know me, she has never known me, and every year the census she is *expelled a little further into the dark toward a bottom I did not know existed* until I made the road myself to keep her *off the procession*—" the lamp came steady; the locked door opened, one inch, on its true hinge at last "— so yes. Say it properly. Say it in your flat voice and I will stand it: *I have been wanting to wake the sun since I was sixteen, and every dome in the world is the reason I could not*, and the arithmetic that says nine domes' everything for one morning says *my mother's last walks* in the *everything* — and I want you, Lampbearer, the *counting kind*, to tell me whether that is grief, or ledger."

Isolde did not say *it's all right*. She did not say *she's at peace*. She said, in the flat voice, the only one that had ever been true for either of them, the way you read names off beds and press hands at funerals and open a monk's fist on the last mile of a road a dead woman taught him to walk:

"It is both. It has always been both. That is what the rite *is* — they built the whole economy out of *grief pretending to be bookkeeping*, and you have been doing it to yourself for eight years to keep the road honest, and I have *carried four hundred and eleven of you* in my case, Mirelle, and I could not tell your shape from my own." She reached across the amber, and took the scarred hand off the lamp, and pressed it flat against the wall of the stair, palm to ice, the way the dying press hands, and the whole column's forty lamps leaned, all of them, toward the two of them, which no one slept through and no one ever mentioned again, which was how you could tell it had finally happened. "Now. *Audit me*. If we wake the Vigil before the Grand Tithe, your mother and the procession *are* the wick — the walls, the jars, the keeps, *every single one we have ever loved enough to label* — and Ruen died for less than that arithmetic. And if we do *nothing* and let the valves close on census-night, the domes shear their year into the great lantern, and the procession gets *longer*, and your mother walks another winter toward the same bottom, only *faster*. So there is no third door and I am the only Lampbearer alive who has *sat inside both sides of the funnel*, and what I am going to tell you on the fourth stair when the runners wake up is going to make both of us rich and the Court *insolvent*, and I need, tonight, the ledger-voice, not the grief, for the arithmetic."

"Then say it," Mirelle said, in the voice of a locked door with nothing behind it now but a woman.

Isolde looked down the stair, at the breathing dark, at the processional faint sound of the taken, at the handmark leaning warm through three miles of ice, and said it, in the flat voice:

"The Vigil never said the wick had to be *lost*."

---

## Chapter 15 · The Sealed Word

They found the Court in the ribs, on the ninth stair up, which was impossible, which nobody did, which — Mirelle said, flat, professional, reading the ice like Isolde had once read a ward — meant that someone with a *seal and a grudge* had paid for the first survey of the return-lines in the history of the Ashlands, and had brought the lamps *from the domes* to do it, and that the whole quiet road the Kindling had kept for two generations was now, in the season of the Grand Tithe, *paved*.

The patrol was eight Wardens, one surveyor with a chain and a slate, and a seal-chest, and they were *terrified* — Isolde saw it the way she saw the blue hour, at once and professionally: men in uniform three days from any dome, guarding a survey of pipes that fed a lantern they did not believe in, in a dark that *leaned*, and they fell backward into ranks at the sight of the Kindling column cresting the stair, forty lamps and all, and the shouting started, and stopped, because the *officer* had gone to his knees.

Not in combat. He had looked at the sledge — at Ruen, folded, awake-in-the-eyes, gone-out-of-them — and his helmet had come off and his hands had gone to her like a man who has been told all his life that the drained are *fuel* and has just met the first one he is *allowed* to grieve for, and the eight Wardens watched their officer do that, and Isolde watched the eight Wardens *not write it down*, and she thought: *yes — that — that is the ledger-voice I came up four hundred stairs for.*

Then the letter found her, and the letter found her because the Court had stopped pretending, the way weather stops: the seal-chest was opened with the surveyor's slate on top of it, and on the slate, in a hand as flat and careful and terribly familiar as her own, and the only hand in nine domes she would have known with her funnel taken away:

And before the letter there was the *signing*, and the signing is the item the Kindling's record files as the beginning of the end, because it is the one place in the whole road where Isolde Vane had to choose what her own name was *for*, and she chose it in front of eight armed men who had come to pave her people's secret with lamps. The surveyor was a small, professional, terrified man who had been told the Ashlands was a myth and had found forty lamps and a sledge and a *civilization* in it, and his slate had the return-lines on it — the first survey in the history of the dark, paid for by a seal and a grudge — and every line on it ran *through* the ribs, and every line in the ribs ran through the Kindling's eleven rooms, and if the slate went back up the stair the eleven rooms were *nine days from a census*, and Mirelle stood behind the man with her hands at her sides and her neck's scar going the color of old fire, and said nothing at all, because she had *trained* for this and did not want it, and Tam had a knife out, and Hask had *counted the Wardens twice* and reported, flat, that eight of them had families in the Seventh.

And Isolde took the surveyor's slate off the seal-chest, and did the one thing nobody in nine domes had ever done with a Court slate, which was to *correct* it, in the flat careful hand of a Lampbearer who had signed four hundred and eleven harvests and knew exactly what a signature costs: she wrote out the eleven rooms, and struck them, and wrote in their place, over her own signature, in the officer's column, the one item the Court would have to carry down the stair and file and *answer for* in front of its new Lord Examiner pro-tem nine days later:

*Ashlands survey, return-lines, item one: no rooms. No people. No lamps. Nothing here but pipes and the dark and the* ***when***, *and the undersigned has been that way for two generations and intends to remain so.*

*Signed: VANE, ISOLDE — Lampbearer, exiled of the Ninth, late of the Charter, and at this hour holding the whole of the Court's road in her two hands.*

"You have just," Mirelle said, in the voice, slowly, when the eight Wardens had been sent back up the stair with their survey intact and their *reason* for it filed in a hand they would not be able to trace, "put your name in a Court record for the first time since you were *seven years old*, and you have done it to *protect us*, and there is a *line* in the arithmetic of mercy you and I are now going to have to live inside of, Lampbearer, and it is: *a man like Crail does not need to know where the rooms are; he needs to know that you* ***signed***."

"I know," Isolde said, in the flat voice, at the bottom of the slate, and handed it back to the small professional man, who had taken his helmet off twice that day and would do it a third time before he got home. "I have *always* known. That is the whole of what I have done with my hands for seventeen years — I know exactly what a signature costs, and I have just *bought* you all with mine, and the reason I can do that, locked door, is the reason I am going to be *unable* to do the next one: *there is nothing left on the form.*"

Which was, runners, the truest sentence spoken on the ninth stair, and it landed on her a week later at a ribs-landing with a clean bright wall where a face had been; and the letter she read next, in the hand she would have known with her funnel taken away, was the reason she did not think about that until the very end of the road of it:

*My dear — I have known where you were going since the bathhouse, because the Ledger's First Leaves lean, and I have counted that leaning for twenty-two years, and you would be the first person in history to walk toward it instead of away, and I was right, and I want it in writing that I was right, because I am about to ask you to be wrong on my behalf. — The Grand Tithe is in nineteen days, not six weeks; I did not move it, and I did not stop it, which you will read correctly: Assessor Crail, whose countersign was on the seal that emptied your monk, has been made Lord Examiner pro-tem, because I have been — with great dignity and no paperwork — *retired*, and there is a room in the First Dome where I am supposed to be, and instead I am walking. — I have told the Court's riders that the Ashlands is a myth and the Kindling is a rumor and the deep is a story mothers tell to save lamps, which is the only work I have ever been paid for that mattered, and it will hold for nineteen days because I have been lying to that particular clerk for twenty-two years and I know his *weather*. — Come to the Seventh, my dear. It is the only dome whose valves I cut wrong, on purpose, and the only place between here and census-night where a Lampbearer with a stolen case can *learn to carry what she is describing* without nine domes watching. — I have read your mother's leaf a hundred times off the outside of my own lantern; I could never scrape it; you may have noticed I never could. Bring the boy. Bring the locked door. Tell her the arithmetic is *not* both — tell her I have spent twenty-two years at the flame learning what she has spent the road learning and you are both going to be *insolvent* together and I find I cannot bear to audit it any longer, and that, my dear, is the closest I will ever come in this life to saying —*

The letter ended there, mid-sentence, the way Corvin Aelfric's letters always ended, because the man had spent his whole career being weather and had just, apparently, decided to *stop*.

"He's alone," Mirelle said, at the bottom of the page, reading over Isolde's shoulder, and her voice was doing the locked-door thing where it went *soft* instead of hard, which Isolde had learned to fear the way sailors fear calm. "An exiled Examiner walking the ribs with no seal-chest and a *guilty conscience* and nineteen days of *stolen authority* — that is not a surrender, that is a *man doing a final harvest with his own two hands*, and if we walk to the Seventh with forty lamps and a sledge and a girl who woke up *empty*, your Court-kin will look at *eleven of us* and I will have to be the kind of person I train other people not to be, and I *warn* you, Lampbearer, for the four-hundred-and-twelfth time, that my *training* is *very good*—"

"He cut a valve wrong," Isolde said, in the flat voice, on the stair, in front of the whole column, on purpose, so the ledger would have it in writing before anyone's grief could *re-file* it. "On purpose. Twenty-two years of being decent at speed, and his last act as a free man is to leave *one door in the machine that opens from the inside*, and to walk nineteen days' weather to tell me about it in a letter he cannot finish. *That* is not a final harvest. That is — " and Isolde heard herself, out of the corner of her professional soul, and did not stop, because the counting kind cannot stop, and out here nobody thanks, they *label* "— that is the man who held the funnel *asking the braided hands how to put it down.* I'll take the meeting. And the locked door can *be* the kind of person she trains people not to be *at* the meeting, which is where, I have *noted*, you have *always* been at your most *legible*."

Mirelle looked at her for four full seconds, which is nine seconds in Unlit.

"Forty-three," she said.

"*What* is forty-three—"

"You are counting us *down the stair* again, Lampbearer, you *announcing* it now, out here in the open, like a woman who has *decided*—" and the locked door came *all the way* off its hinges, in front of the column, in the amber, in the way that took three miles of ice to do, and what Isolde did *next* she would refuse, forever, to describe — the wall kept *both their shapes* and the Kindling would *read it for two generations* and label it, out here, the way you label everything you love enough to keep: *the stair where the arithmetic changed*—

The surveyor's slate, left on the seal-chest, filled itself, in the dark, with letters tightening like skin over a healing wound.

Somewhere very far below, in the deep, the procession stopped breathing.

And on the fourteenth stair, in the wrong direction, in a coat the color of nothing, the *first walker they had ever seen coming back* sat down on the ice, and turned his face *toward the light*, and waited for the column to arrive, and when it did, no name was on his tongue — none of the drained have names — but his hands came up, open, palm to palm, and *handed the column a jar*: old, cracked, resealed nine times, a Kindling keep, and on the tag, in a faded hand that had been a *working* hand once, the letters quick and small and running slightly uphill, the single word every one of the taken on that stair, in the whole dark between the domes, was leaning *down* to say:

***NOT YET.***

---

## Chapter 16 · The Seventh

The Seventh Dome was the poor one, the glass one, the dome with the oldest pipes, and the Court had never bothered to build a tithe-house there because the Seventh had spent two centuries paying its rent *in place*: its people trimmed the great lantern's feed-lines from the inside, the way you farm a fence you did not build. It was into that dome's lowest pump-hall, three levels under the market, past a Warden cordon that Mirelle had walked *through* without once breaking stride, that Isolde found the exiled Lord Examiner of nine domes sitting on an upturned bucket, without his coat, reading a jar.

Before the pump-hall there was the Seventh itself, and the Seventh is worth one page, runners, because it is the dome the Court never had to lie to.

You come down the market stair and the light changes first: the Seventh's glass is the oldest in nine domes, ninety years of frost-work in it, and the lamp-light comes through *bent*, the color of weak tea, and every face in the market looks like a face seen through the wrong end of a telescope. The people here do not have hearth-claims. They have *feed-lines*: two centuries of a Court decision that the Seventh, being poor, being nearest the ribs, being nobody's investment, would pay its rent *in place* by keeping the great lantern's pipes from the inside — which is to say that every lamp in the Ninth and the Sixth and the First burns on the labor of a woman with a ladder in the Seventh, and the labor is the rent, and the rent is invisible, and the invisibility is the policy.

So there is no tithe-house in the Seventh, and there is no ward, and there is no Lampbearer. There is a hall with a stove in it, and in the hall on the morning of the column's arrival there were eleven sick people and one girl of about sixteen with ink on her sleeves who had been doing, ever since her grandmother died, the nearest thing the poor dome had to the rite: she sat with the dying, she held their hands, she *had no funnel*, and so she could not take the tithe, and so the Seventh's dead went out of the world *unharvested*, which is the one sentence about the poor domes the Court has never managed to print in its own defense.

Isolde stood in the doorway of that hall for exactly as long as it took the girl to look up and see the gray-blue coat, the case on the arm, the whole trade in the hands — and what the girl said was not a plea. It was a *ledger question*, and Isolde has thought about it every day since:

"Are you here to take, or to sit?"

"To sit," Isolde said, in the flat voice.

"Then sit on that side," the girl said, "and hold the one with the cough, and be quick about it, because I have no tithe for you and no money, and the pumps come on at the turning."

And so the finest harvester alive in a thousand years sat down on the cold of a Seventh Dome hall floor and held the hand of a pipe-keeper's wife with a cough, and did *nothing* — no funnel, no wedge, no pull, no report — and was *paid*, and that is the item that finished breaking something in her that had been ready to break since the market. She was paid in bread. She was paid in a thank-you. She was paid in the girl's exhausted and entirely unkind "you're not bad, for a dome-woman", and Isolde Vane found that she could not carry it, and had to put it down in the case with the rest of her inventory, and could not afterwards name it.

He looked up. He looked — she filed it flat, at a bedside, because she was going to have to be honest about this face later — like a man whose weather had finally run out of *fronts*.

"Forty," he said, "I counted you at the stair-mouth. Forty lamps and one sledge and a *walker* who handed you my property." He turned the jar in his hands — the resealed one, *NOT YET* — with the care he used to use on the Ledger's leaves. "That is a Kindling keep — older than I am, resealed nine times — and it went out of this dome seventeen years ago in a runner's coat, and the runner went out with it and did not come back as a person, and last night a *drained man* — a man with nothing in his head, my dear, nothing, the way you taught me to check — carried it *up* fourteen stairs against the leaning of every fire in the world, and sat down, and *waited* for the column, and gave it *back*."

He turned the jar once more, and stopped turning it, and set it down again, and when he spoke the next words he spoke them to the jar and not to her, which was the only way the Lord Examiner of nine domes had ever managed anything personal.

"The keep is older than I am. The *tag* is not. I know that writing, Isolde. I stood on the far side of a bed with the funnel in my hands, twenty-six years old and junior and obedient, while that hand wrote a tag for its own harvest, because she would not let me do it for her and there was nobody in the room she trusted with the arithmetic. She packed herself, my dear. She chose the jar off a Kindling stall the year she was made a keeper, she wrote the word on it with her own quick pen, she put it in a runner's hands and sent it out into the black *before* they came for her, and I have spent seventeen years telling myself the runner was lost, because that was a great deal easier to believe than what the runner actually was, which was a *delivery*. Do you understand what you are holding? I have counted that leaning for twenty-two years and this is the first *counter-lean* ever measured. Something down there is not only digesting — it is *returning*, slowly, the way a good harvester returns a case when she thinks the family has finally earned it, and it gave it back to *you*, to the column, to a nameless man walking up fourteen stairs with nothing in his head and one instruction in his hands, and if the Vigil gives back *one jar*, and the Court arrives in nineteen days and closes *nine domes' year* down these pipes into my lantern at the *wrong side* of that slow grace —" he set the jar down, gently, on the pump-housing, between the two of them, the way they had set every lamp between them since the first ward, "— then the Grand Tithe does not wake the sun, Isolde. It *lights* it. Unasked. Unguided, with nine domes' wick and no hand to pour it, and a nearly-wake that has been *kind*, for a thousand years, *done being waited on*, and what comes up through the glass on census-night is not the day your mother ate — it is the *digestion*, the whole unlearned morning, and *everything that leans* goes with it, procession and Kindling and jars and the Ninth's walls and every last *kept thing* in nine domes, to feed a fire that will not know, ever, that it was *meant to give something back.*"

Nobody moved in the pump-hall. Somewhere above, the Seventh was haggling over turnips, which Isolde found *intolerable* and then, one second later, the most beautiful sound in the world.

"You cut the Seventh's valve wrong," Tam said, from behind Isolde's shoulder, in the flat register he saved for counting and for dying, which he had apparently merged. "On purpose. *Why*?"

"Because it is the only valve in nine domes," Corvin said, "that a harvester can *work from this side*." He stood, and he was old in the way of the Undercroft, in the *finished* way, and he held out, to Isolde, palm up, empty, the way he had held out his hand on a service stair that felt a previous life ago: "I cannot carry what I described to you, my dear. I have *tried* for twenty-two years to be the door, and I have known for nineteen days that I was only ever the *frame*. But you — *you opened the fourth wedge on your bed in the dark and have not closed a thing since* — you can stand at the thin place with your hands, and *learn the volume*, and I have spent the whole of my last freedom hiring this pump-hall, sealing this Warden line, and stealing — " a gray sleeve came up, and in it, a small brass thing with a hairline crack, which the light moved through oddly, "— one *leaf*. From the Ledger. Page one of my own hand, the night after your mother, when I copied what I had done *before the wall could*, which is the only reason I am alive and the only reason I deserve not to be. The First Leaves lean. This leaf *is* leaning. Stand at the thin glass with my theft in your pocket and *carry something small*, Isolde, and let the old man watch the *volume* for the first time from the *outside* of the funnel, because there are nineteen days and nine domes and *one pair of braided hands*, and I would like, before I am *done*, to see whether the arithmetic you two have been *insolventing each other over* all the way up my road is *real*."

The thin place in the Seventh was under the pump-hall, in a service-alcove where the dome's foundation met the old ice and the glass of the world ran up both walls *lean*, leaning with the whole dome's weight. It should have been impossible. It was *merely* cold, the way a chapel is cold: a temperature you agree to be quiet inside.

Isolde stood at it with her sleeves up, and the stolen leaf warm in her pocket, and Corvin's hands on her shoulders, *placed where they could be seen*, for once only because they were.

"The rite goes *one way*," he said, and his voice was weather, the last of it, weather that had come around from the far side of the world. "Bedside, funnel, *pull*, lamp, flame, *spending*. To carry the other way you do not *push* — a thousand harvesters have tried push, and push makes wick — you do what your mother did, and what old Maren did, and what the monk did in my doorway at the last stair, which is the one thing the rite never trained you, because the rite *cannot* train it, and I held the funnel my whole life and could never once *do* it and you must be *trembling*, my dear, you must be *trembling* and *ready*, because it is: **you ask. You press your palms to the wall and you *ask*, the way you would at a bedside if the dying were the *fire* and you were *dying*, and you let it come into the room, all of it, all the shapes, and you do not keep *one*—"

She asked.

The Ninth's hum came up through the Seventh's pipes the way tide comes up an estuary: the whole *kept* dome of it — nine generations of sea-water and grass and ordinations and pears and *gratitudes*, every shape the flame had ever taken from the Ninth and the walls had ever held, three hundred thousand keepers' worth of *lean*, arriving at the thin glass because a door in it had finally *opened from the inside* — and Isolde Vane put both palms flat against the wall of the world and *carried*, all of it, through, for the space of one held breath and a half, and in that space she *was* the Ninth Dome, she *was* every bedside, she was old Maren's hands and Harb's *honesty* and a scriptor's ink-stain and the *Miracle of the Blue Hour* and her mother's *face*, and—

—and then Mirelle's scarred hand closed on her wrist from behind, *took her off the wall* like a lamp off a bracket, and the hum went back down, uncarried, and Isolde sat on the cold of the Seventh's floor with three people leaning over her, and she was *fine*, she could say *fine* in the flat voice, she was—

"You said *four hundred and twelve*," Corvin said quietly, kneeling beside her, and he was not looking at her face. He was looking at her hands, which lay open in her lap, *not shaking*. "The procession count is four hundred and twelve. Ruen is number one hundred and thirty on Hask's muster-list, and you have been saying *four hundred and twelve* for forty seconds, my dear, and I want you to *audit me*: what did you keep, and what did you— **what did you lose**."

And Isolde opened her mouth to say *nothing*, in the flat voice, the only honest one, and the lie would not come, and the *room* in her where her mother's face had been — the braiding room, the cracked ceiling, the quick hands, the *turning* — was a clean bright wall with nothing hung on it, and she could still *hear* the voice, she could *still*—

"I can't picture her," Isolde said, in the flat voice, at a bedside, her own. "One held breath and a half, and I *can't picture her*." The sea was in the jar over her heart. The voice was in the fourth wedge forever. The *face* — what the Vigil had held *unfinished* a thousand years, what the leaf on the lantern had cost a monk his walk, what had come *back up the fourteen stairs*—

"That," said Corvin Aelfric, at the thin place of the Seventh, in a voice with no weather left in it at all, "is *one dome*."

And under the market, the Seventh's pumps went quiet, and in the deep, for the second time in three days, the procession *started walking*.

---

## Chapter 17 · Two Handles

Word runs faster than lamps, out here, and the Kindling had heard everything by the time the column got home.

They had heard it the way the Kindling heard all things — through the walls. A runner who had gone down to the deep with Ruen's blanket and come back *unable to stop describing the sun* had pressed her palms to the stair-ice at the crossroads, *trembling and ready*, and the ice had taken the shape of the sermon the way it took everything: whole, patient, and *loud*. By the second stair, three hundred people had read the wall. By the ribs, the number was a rumor; by the amber, the rumor was a *faction*.

They called the meeting in the listening-room, under the ribs, where the pillars came up through the roof of the world like the bones of the drowned leviathan the low rows sang about, and every dome-kin and deep-kin and walker-and-keeper in the Kindling came, and what came *with* them — that was the thing Isolde stood at the front of the room and looked at, level, flat, in the amber, and filed away for the ledger of the rest of her life — was *hope*, which is the most dangerous cargo in nine domes because unlike grief it cannot be labelled, jarred, or *kept*: hope gets *spent*, and the Kindling had been saving for two generations, and now the savings were in the room, in a thousand faces, all of them leaning.

Here is what the seven days home had been, since the counting has to be kept somewhere in this record. The column came up out of the Seventh with a sledge, a spent Lampbearer, and a wall-sermon that had *gone ahead of them*, and at every stair they stopped at they found people already waiting — deep-kin who had pressed their palms to the ice at the crossroads and read the whole of it, and who now wanted the *particular* thing that walls cannot give anybody, which is what a sermon means for *your* hand. So Isolde read. That was the seven days: at every waystation she went palm to wall and told strangers what the stone had kept of her mother's last night, and at every waystation she gave away a little more of the one professional skill she had not yet been billed for, and by the sixth stair she had stopped reporting the cost of it, and Tam, walking point as usual and counting as usual, started keeping the number of times she sat down afterward. It came to nineteen. He did not tell her the number, which was the kindest thing anybody did in the whole second half of this book.

"They say," said a voice from the left aisle, and the left aisle stood, and Isolde learned the name of it later, from Hask, in the flat exhausted tone of a man introducing weather: *the Woken* — runners and keepers and three of the returned walkers' own keepers, led by a broad woman with a rope-worker's arms, Vess Kel, whose brother had gone down the last stair in the year of the eleven and come back *not himself* and *not empty*, which was a third condition the Kindling had no word for until the wall-sermon, and now they had a *leader* for it — "they say *the sun is a jar*, and the glass of the world is *thin*, and one pair of braided hands can pour it out, and the Court will spend nine domes' everything in twelve days to light it *as a fire* — and I have read the wall myself, Lampbearer, I have read it *eight times*, and there is only one arithmetic in it that is *new*, and here it is in my mouth for the thousand: **why wait for them to light it?** The Seventh's pipes run *down*, the lean runs *down*, and there is a woman standing in our room who has *already opened one door in the machine* — we go up tonight, four hundred lamps, and we *ask at the glass together*, and we wake the morning on *our* hour, not Crail's, and if the domes lose their year — *the domes that made the year a debt* — then let the great lantern eat *their* wick for a change and *choke*, and the sun come up over a world that *owes it nothing*—"

The room *went* — the amber went, the walls went, a thousand hands went flat against a thousand pillars in the old Kindling blessing or the old Kindling *riot*, Isolde could not tell which, and could not move, because the arithmetic in the woman's mouth was *almost* right, and *almost* is Corvin's most dangerous word—

"And my mother," said a second voice, from the aisle at Isolde's right hand, *seated*, and the room quieted at that, the way it never quieted at hope: it quieted at Mirelle Dax's *ledger-voice*, which everyone in the Kindling had followed for eleven years and which no one had heard *ask permission* before, "is in the procession. So is Vess Kel's brother. So is number one hundred and thirty, *Ruen*, who is *in the deep camp* because she *cannot come home* until somebody carries the shapes *right* — and what the wall-sermon says, *I* have read eight times too, and there is a second arithmetic, and it is in the *same words*, and it goes: if the waking is asked at the glass wrong — unguided, ungrieved, with four hundred lamps and a *choke* in the lantern and nine domes shearing at a bell they did not hear — then it is not the morning that comes up, Vess, it is the *digestion*, and the Woken will be *unwoken*, and the walkers will walk *forever*, and the sun will be a *fire* with *everybody in it* and I have *buried six of eleven* under memorial lamps to learn the difference between *brave* and *ready*—"

"**AND I HAVE BURIED BROTHERS IN THE *DARK* WHILE THE DOMES TRIMMED THEIR LAMPS AND CALLED IT *RENT***—" and the amber *bowed*, and a thousand hands came off a thousand walls, and Isolde Vane — exiled, braided, emptied of a face, standing in the listening-room of the Kindling with the whole road in her coat pockets, sea-gratitude and leaf and knife — did the thing the road had made her, in the voice the rite had broken and the wall had re-braided, level and flat and *total*, and the room went quiet the way the deep goes quiet, not because she was loud but because for the first time in the argument somebody had *finished the sentence*:

"You're *both* right and you're *both* spending the wrong coin, and here is the ledger." She crossed to the center pillar — the oldest in the room, the one the low rows touched their children's foreheads with on census-day — and she put both palms flat against it, *trembling and ready*, and said: "The Vigil said *through pairs of hands that were braided*. Plural, runners — it has *never* said *one*, it was *describing a trade*, not sentencing me, and every one of you has stood at a bedside in your life and *let the flame go through your hands to the lamp* without *keeping* the memory, which is the *exact same muscle*, trained nightly, nine domes, two generations — *you have all been practicing*—" and the room did the thing rooms do when a lie they have been *not-telling themselves* finally arrives in the honest voice: silence, and then the sound of a thousand hands turning, palm-up, in amber light. "Corvin's valves close in twelve days on nine domes' *year*, and the year is *small* — one season, sheared by weight, and *everything else* — every kept wall, every jar, every shape the Kindling has *loved enough to label* — is the *wick he never counted*, because the Court's ledger cannot see what left the tithe-house legally, and that, runners, is the *only* arithmetic that makes the sun *cheap*: **we do not wait for the bell and we do not ring it early — we *empty the domes into the glass ourselves*, the night the valves open, *all of us, every pair of hands, asking at the walls of nine domes at once*, and the Grand Tithe arrives at the great lantern *starved*, and the wick goes *down*, whole, welcomed, *carried*, and the Vigil wakes *fed and forgiving*, and what comes up through the glass on census-night is the *morning*—" The room was *held*, a thousand faces, the amber bowed, the walls sang — and Isolde Vane finished, flat, in the voice, because the counting kind cannot stop and because it was true and because Mirelle Dax's hand was finding hers at her side in the light: "— and nobody chokes, and nobody walks the long road another winter, and the domes wake up on census-morning *poor as us* and *free as the Kindling*, and for the first time in a thousand years the light in this world is *given*, runners, *given*, *not spent*—"

Then the Kindling voted, because the Kindling are not a cult, whatever the low rows say about a people who live in jars.

They do not vote by voice. They vote by *pillar*: you put both palms flat on the nearest stone, and if you mean it the stone takes the lean, and Hask walks the room and counts the leans, and an abstention is a hand in a pocket and gets written in the slate exactly as such. The vote of the faction that had spent two generations keeping its hope in glass took about four minutes, and it came out, on the second count (the honest one), as: the plan accepted; nine columns to nine domes; the fourth column to the First; and Vess Kel's four hundred *not* accepted, *not* refused, and *not* left in a pocket.

Which is where the sentence this chapter is named for arrives, runners, because Vess Kel came up the aisle with her arms crossed and did not shout, and everybody in the room said afterward that the not-shouting was the frightening part.

"You have two handles on one door," the Woken said, level, at the center pillar, and she put one hand flat on one side of it, and the whole room watched her do it, and Isolde felt the thing land before she had an answer to it, because it was the first *correct* sentence spoken all night and the woman saying it did not know yet what she was offering. "Your plan says *ask at the walls of nine domes*, my people say *go up at the glass tonight*. That is not two doors, Lampbearer. That is one door, and the two of us standing at a different handle of it. So put my four hundred somewhere in your ledger, and put them at the glass, and I promise you on my brother's third condition that we will not touch the wheel — I will spend my own body keeping any of mine from it. Do that, and the Woken will *stand*; and if the Woken have to stand, we will stand *better than anybody in nine domes*, because we have spent two years practicing standing in a room full of people telling us to wait."

"I cannot put your four hundred at the glass," Isolde said, in the flat voice, because she was finished with being pleasant and the road was twelve hours from needing her honest, and it is the second hardest sentence she ever said to a crowd. "There is one door in the machine that opens from the inside, and it takes *one pair* of hands, and I will not argue with you about whether that is a punishment or a privilege, because it is a *mechanic*. But I can put your four hundred where the *lean has to go*, which is the outer ring of the crossroads, holding the glass from the outside, so that when the door opens the whole nine-dome weight finds a *wall* and not a *crowd*. That is not the handle you wanted. It is the second handle on the same door. Take it, and stand, and I will go in at the hour with four hundred of your people's leaning in my coat like a borrowed lamp, and I will say your brother's number out loud at the glass — and I will not tell you it is the same thing as what you asked for, because it is not."

Vess Kel was silent for three counts, and then she did the thing that made eleven years of Mirelle Dax's authority quietly and permanently unnecessary: she *uncrossed her arms*, laid both palms flat on the pillar beside her own hand, and leaned.

From directly above, through two miles of ice and the ribs of the drowned leviathan and the whole patient dark, came a sound none of them had ever heard except in the wall-shapes of a dead century: bells. Nine sets. Ringing *out of season*.

Hask went to the stair-mouth, and came back with the frost on him and the flat exhausted voice, the way men say the weather, and said two words, and the ledger of the rest of Isolde's life opened on its worst new line:

"*They've cut the calendar.*"

Census-night was tonight.

---

## Chapter 18 · Nine Columns

There is a number for how many hours it takes a plan to meet the world, and the Kindling found out what it was at the bells: the plan had twelve days, and the world had tonight, and the whole of the difference — twelve days of a plan folded into one night, at the rate of about a dome an hour — was what a thousand hands now ran on, and the running is the chapter of the book that nobody writes, which is this: *the arithmetic of mercy, done at speed, in the dark, by people who have been practicing for two generations.*

Hask took the counting, because Hask had always taken the counting, and the counting kind cannot stop, and by the turning-lamp he had reduced Isolde's sermon to columns: nine domes, four hundred and twelve trained hands in eleven rooms — *eleven rooms*, the number Corvin had kept for twenty-two years, the sleeper-cells the Unlit had kept *so deep in the domes that even the Kindling forgot them, and Corvin's own counts forgot, and the Court's census never thought to look for people who were already counted* — plus nine hundred Kindling runners, plus the walkers, plus *the jars*, which was the line that stopped the whole muster, three times, because nobody out here said the word *jars* in a sentence that also said *empty* without looking first at every wall in the room, and every wall in the room looked back.

"The tubes," Mirelle said, over the heads of the crowd, doing what she did — the ledger, no grief, the coin that keeps roads — "the tubes run glass to glass from the First to the Ninth, and the Court seals them at census because *warmth travels*, so we do not take the tubes. Every service door between here and the ribs that the Court believes is *welded* has been opened by a Kindling hand at some hour of some night for twenty years, and the ones that were never welded are the fourteen we keep lamps for. Columns leave in the hour. Three to a dome, staggered — one column to *ask*, one to *teach*, one to *carry the jars to the walls* — and the fourth column—" she stopped, in the amber, and did the locked-door thing with her whole body where it went very still instead of very hard, and turned to Isolde across the muster-slate, "— the fourth column is the First. The lantern itself. The valves open at the blue hour and the whole *lean* of nine domes has to have somewhere to *go* at the moment of the bell, and the thin glass at the crossroads will take the weight of one dome, which we *measured*, and nine domes is nine hundred of that, and the *one* door in the machine that opens from the inside is not under the Seventh, it is at the center of the First, where a starving flame already curls in a ring of shadow and *has been waiting to be asked* — and that door does not have a column."

"It has hands," Isolde said, in the flat voice, because the counting kind cannot stop, and because the road had taught her the word she used to hate most in nine domes, the one Corvin said at a landing and Mirelle said in a cellar and Assur said in a boiler hall and her mother had braided into a fourth wedge: *you are so good at giving things back.* "Plural, locked door — I know you counted me singular on that slate — *pairs of hands, plural*, the Vigil's own arithmetic, and if the lantern at the center takes the whole nine-dome lean on one pair it will spend the pair, which is *one dome's hum for a held breath and a half equals a mother's face*, and I have *done the sum*, Mirelle — I have done it *on your stair at the ribs, in your amber, out loud*, and the answer that has been in the room the whole time and *nobody* writing it is—" and the listening-room, a thousand faces, the muster-slate, the whole arithmetic of mercy, went silent with *that*, because it was the smallest suggestion ever made in the Kindling, and every wall in it *leaned*: "— *borrowed hands.* Ask the lantern *together*. Four hundred and twelve, plus nine hundred, plus every trained pair that can stand at a wall in nine domes *at the blue hour*, plus the walkers, whose hands lean, whose hands *lean*, and whose hands have never in the history of the rite been *asked*—" and there it was, the sentence she had not said to the room because she had not had the room's guts for it, said now, flat, total, at a bedside, hers: "— *plus the ones who cannot wake.* Ruen's in the deep camp with her hands folded at a table that is not there. The dream kept the first thing she was *given*. There is — runners — there is a *fourth circle* in this plan and it is *the taken themselves*, and if any one of you thinks I have gone mad on the road, press your palms to the nearest wall and *read what the Kindling kept of my mother's sermon*, because she wrote the last line of it *down there in the dream, unfinished, mid-word*, and the word she was saying when the waking took me was *real*, and a *real* minute of a real morning is what the fourth circle is *for*—"

The amber bowed. The walls sang, all thousand, and at the edge of the muster, where the returned walkers sat in their ring with the patience of weather, the oldest of them — coat the color of nothing, face the color of a page — put both palms flat against the nearest pillar, *trembling*, and *ready*, and *the pillar took the lean back*, and the whole Kindling watched a *drained man* do the rite backwards for the first time in a thousand years and *not keep it*, give it clean to the stone like a lamp to a bracket, and when his hands came off the wall the man did not know what he had done, and did not need to, and sat down in himself again, and — *leaned*. Which was the sign. Which was the *answer*. Which was why the procession had started walking, and why the jar had come *up* fourteen stairs, and why a nearly-wake had been kind for a thousand years and *nearly*: the taken were not the wick.

The taken were the *fourth circle*, and they had been practicing for nine hundred years.

And then Hask read out loud, a fourth time, the line that had stopped the muster three times, in the flat voice, so that nobody could ever say afterward that it had been done by accident:

*Item — the keeps. The Kindling surrenders its own jars.*

You have to understand what that was, runners, and the only way to say it plain is this. The Unlit do not bury. A dome family buries a person in the ground and keeps the ground; the Kindling keep a person in *glass*, and a keep is visited, and a keep is read aloud at a rail, and a keep is the only record a people who live in a dark with no paperwork have ever had. Two generations of the Kindling's own dead stood in those forty sledges — every runner lost to the quiet, every walker who came up fourteen stairs and sat down in the amber and did not wake again, every child of the deep camps who had one name and one kept morning — and the plan needed all of it *gone into the walls of nine domes* at the blue hour, because the lean that wakes a sun has to be *carried by somebody*, and the only cargo light enough to travel that far is a shape that somebody is willing to let go of.

Mirelle asked the room. That is the thing to remember: she *asked* it, with the muster-slate open and the bells still ringing down the stairs from nine domes, and she said it in the voice of a quartermaster who intends to be answered honestly or not at all.

"We are not spending our dead tonight," she said. "Understand the difference, because the Court never has: a spent thing is gone, and a *lent* thing comes back if the morning does. If the morning does not, then we are the people who kept jars for two generations and then sat down in the dark beside them, and there is no worse way to be a Kindling, and I will not do it by a show of hands. Anyone who cannot give theirs, keeps it. Say nothing. Put your hands in your pockets and let the slate go past."

And the sledges were opened, and the amber came up, and the Kindling of nine darks went down the ranks and put their palms flat on their own dead — *palm to glass, the way you press hands at a funeral* — and lifted them out of the sledge and handed them to a runner, and said, each of them, the two words that are the Unlit for *goodbye*, which are: *it leans*.

Eleven families kept their jars. Nobody wrote the eleven names down, and the eleven jars rode out and came back with the columns, and the Kindling had never in two generations owned a *no* before and did not entirely know what to do with the shape of it — which is the honest way for a free vote to look, runners, and the only way a free vote has ever looked.

The slate came past Isolde Vane, because the slate came past everybody, and she looked at it for a long time with her thumb on the tally-mark, and then she said, in the flat voice, "The coat is not mine to spend."

It was the first honest sentence she had ever said out loud in a room full of people who wanted her to be brave, and Mirelle did not soften it and did not take it back. "Nobody asked you, Lampbearer," she said. "You are not Kindling, you are not one of ours, and your coat is not my cargo. But understand what you are holding, because I have watched you carry it for six weeks and I will not have you carry it wrong: that jar over your heart has a dead woman's name on a boy's tag, and it was *given* to you and not *sold*, and the Kindling does not spend other people's free gifts by permission. When the morning comes — *if* — you will have to give that one yourself, with your own hands, to whoever is standing in front of you, and you will find out then whether you have the motion in you or not, and either way it will not be tonight, and either way I will not be the one standing there, and *that* is the whole of my vote on your coat, quartermaster's voice and all." She pushed the slate on to the next runner. "I did not say *no*," Isolde said afterwards, to nobody who asked. "I said *not mine*. Out here, runners, that is the same word with a different owner on it, and it took me a road to learn the difference."

And one thing stayed out of the walls on purpose. There was a folded muster-margin page in the *keeps* box — a street-name, an officer, an age, a loss filed as column property — and Hask took it out of the box in front of the whole amber room and put it on top of his own slate, because a paper is not a keep, and a thing written down can travel with the person who wrote it, and the quartermaster of the New Light had decided, at the fourth reading of the item, that he intended to be alive to hand it back.

"Columns," Hask said, in the flat exhausted voice, writing it into the slate as though it had always been so, because the counting kind cannot stop and because this was, at last, a number the world could *print*: "Nine domes, four circles. First column leaves in the hour. — And the fourth column to the First, Lampbearer — the lantern door — that is you, and the man who retired you, and—" he looked at the slate, and at Isolde, and did the sum, and *hated* it, the way men hate weather "— *nineteen days ago* there were nineteen days of *weather* in that column. Tonight it is four of us, a sledge, and the *bell*. And Crail holds the First."

"Crail," Corvin said, from the back of the room, where the exiled Lord Examiner of nine domes had been standing this whole time without a coat and without a *front*, and did not say anything else, which was not Corvin's way at all, and everybody with ears heard what the silence was: *I know. I made him. I counted his room and did not burn it, and here is what he has become with my twenty-two years inside his seal.* He buttoned what he had left. "Four of us is a column. It has been four of us this whole time, my dear. Ask me what it *costs*."

And Isolde Vane, at the muster-slate of the Kindling, nine hundred years of practice in the walls and one mother's face in the fourth wedge, did the thing the road had made her, and said it *ledger*, and the ledger was:

"Everything. And the jars. And *not one shape of it lost* — that is the *whole point*, tall coward, that is the *only* point, we are spending *nine domes' everything* tonight and I have decided, out loud, at a bedside, in the *amber* — **it goes to the glass, not the fire, and it comes back as morning, and nobody — *nobody* — is written off tonight, not Ruen, not your mother, not one low-row sick in the Ninth's ward, and if Crail wants the wick he can come to the thin glass and *ask for it himself***—" and the ninth column left in the hour, and the tenth, and the eleventh, and the Kindling emptied into nine darks the way a held breath empties, and the ribs stood, and the lamps went out one by one behind them, all facing down the road, the way the taken face the light: *at last, correctly, all at once.*

---

## Chapter 19 · The Fourth Circle

The tubes ran glass to glass, sealed at census, *warmth travels* — and the Kindling's own rule, two generations deep, was that a sealed tube is a *promise*: every one of them had been opened by some Kindling hand at some hour of some night, and the fourteen that were welded were the fourteen with lamps kept for them, and so the nine columns did not take the tubes, and the fourth column — Corvin, Isolde, Tam, and the locked door who had not asked to be asked and had come anyway, trimming a lamp at the stair-foot because the road peels — *did* take a tube, the oldest, the one under the Seventh whose service door opened for the hands she braided, and the four of them walked the black straw across the frozen sea with the white light of the First growing ahead of them like a bad idea about to be *proved*, and nobody said what it was, until Tam said it, the way Tam said everything, in the smallest possible sentence, holding the twelfth knife between the two of them like a rail:

"The Court *knows*. That's what Crail is. He moved the calendar *because* of the wall-sermon — you don't cut twelve days off a census for *weather*, you cut it for a *plan you can see coming* — and if he's standing at the lantern tonight, he isn't standing there to stop the bells, he's standing there to *light it himself while the whole world is looking the other way*—"

"*Nineteen days*," said Corvin, softly, at the glass, where the First was a moon getting nearer, and the four of them saw the thing the boy's arithmetic had made flesh: the plaza was *full*. Not Wardens. Not riders. *Every clerk of the First, every bracket-keeper in the lantern corps, every seal-chest and census-robe between the ramp and the lantern*, all arrayed on the plaza at the blue hour *before* the hour, and at their head, on the lantern's own stair, a tall man in a seal too big for his coat reading a slate, and the slate was *pointed at the tube they were in*, and the reading began, and did not stop, the way weather does not stop.

"He counted us," Isolde said, in the flat voice, at the glass. "The whole road. The eleven rooms. He has Corvin's own counts for twenty-two years and he has *my* ledger signature nine days old and he has the *wall-sermon* — Crail does not need to stop the fourth circle, runners, Crail needs to *attend it* — he has brought the *whole dome of the First to witness the Miracle*, and there is no way to reach the lantern that is not *through a plaza of everybody who will ever write down what happened on it*, and the one person alive who has *printed* a miracle on a lantern-plaza—" her head turned, on the black straw, to the gray coat, and the gray coat did not look away, which was, for Corvin Aelfric, a *scream* "— *has to go in there and be* ***audited*** *on it, in front of the whole Court, by the man whose countersign emptied your monk.*"

"So that is the column," said Corvin. "Walk."

And they walked the black straw, and it is the only quiet scene left in the whole book, and the counting kind cannot stop and neither can a road, so here it is in the plain words, because nine hundred years of the Kindling's own history is going to be *filed* in the next ten minutes and this part — the part where a man tells the truth on a frozen sea to the four people he is about to spend himself on — this part the walls did not keep, because there was no wall out there, only straw, and straw keeps nothing, and that is why it is the only honest place in the world:

"I will tell you the twenty-two years once," the gray coat said, at the head of the four of them, in the *weather* voice gone down to something a great deal smaller and a great deal harder, "and then I will be *audible* for nineteen minutes in a plaza, and afterward you will have only the record, and the record is *me at my worst and my best at the same time*, so listen now at the *middle*. There are eleven rooms.
