I have counted them every census since the year of the eleven, and I have written *none* in every one of them, and the *none* is not a *lie* to the Court; the *none* is the *truest* item in nine domes, because in the fourth year of it I did go in, and there was a *sleeper* in the third cell of the tenth room, and she was old — older than the dome she was hiding in, older than the Charter — and she was *hiding* in the dark in a dome coat with forty other people *trained to be still*, and she had a *name*, and I have written *none* over that name for eighteen years, and not one of you in this column — not the *girl*, not the *locked door*, not the *cat-boy* — has any idea what it costs a clerk to *be a clerk* for eighteen years with a name under his pen. That is not *fondness*. I want that *on the record* for what it is worth, and it is worth nine hundred years of the Kindling not *quite* forgiving me, and I have *made my peace* with the not-quite, and here it is, itemized, out loud, on the straw, at the last of the *middle*: I did the *decent* thing at a speed I could *file*, for twenty-two years, and tonight I am going to do the *only* thing at a speed I cannot, and there is a *junction* at the end of this plaza with a *wheel* on it and a *tide* behind it, and the tide through a *wall* feels nothing, and the tide through a *person*—"

"*Do not promise me the tide,*" Isolde said, in the flat voice, at the glass, because the counting kind cannot stop and because she had *done the sum* on his own stair at the ribs a week ago and had not gone back to it since, and had been *not-looking* at it the way you do not look at a ward-door.

And Corvin stopped, on the black straw, in the white light of a moon getting nearer, and did the thing with his cuffs, and turned, and stood there the way he had stood in forty-one rooms, *weather*, *front*, *the whole disease*, and said:

"Then *make me a promise you can hold me to*, my dear, because I have spent twenty-two years on *filing* and I have *nineteen minutes* on *honest*, and I would rather be *audited by you at a bedside* than *recorded by them at a plaza*: **promise me you will keep your hands off the wheel**. The wheel is *filing*, my dear; the glass is *mercy*; and there is exactly one way tonight comes out *with the walkers in it* instead of *digesting them*, and it is the way where the whole nine-dome lean finds *a door* at the center and *not two*, because if you put your palms on that junction-wheel beside mine then the *trade* has taught you *one lesson too few* and the tide comes through *two people* and there are not two people alive who can spend *two people*, and every single one of the four hundred and twelve and the nine hundred and the eleven and the *walkers* gets *digested* instead of *woken*. That is the promise. You will *run to me* if you must — I know you, I have *counted* you, you will *come* — and I am asking only the *one* thing: when you get there, *stand on my side of the wheel with your hands out of your pockets and off my brass*, and let an old clerk do *one item of paperwork without an audience*, and then you will *not* have to say *the rest of that sentence* over a body in a key-room, because I will have *saved you the arithmetic*."

And Isolde Vane stood on the black straw with the whole road in her coat pockets and her mother's unfinished harvest in a jar under her chin, and gave the first promise of her life to the man who had exiled her, level, flat, total, *trembling*, and *ready*:

"*My hands stay off your wheel.*"

"*Good,*" Corvin said, and walked on, and did not look back, and the four of them came up the last of the straw into the white light of the First, and it was the last quiet minute of the whole trade.

And then it stopped being quiet, because the straw keeps nothing, and a road has a way of collecting what it is walked on, and there were three more people out there with eleven minutes and no wall.

Mirelle said it first, in the flat voice, without looking at anybody, because that is how she said everything that mattered: "There is a jar in the Sixth with my *own* whole shape in it — my mother sold it for a roof the year I was sixteen, and I have been the locked door of nine domes ever since, and the jar is the only piece of me that was ever mine to keep — and twenty years later I have never once opened it. I know what is in it the way you know a door in the dark; I can tell you there is a room in it with a lamp in it and a girl in it who is not in the trade anymore, and that is the only read I have ever given myself, and I have *never* billed it, and I have never once *visited* it either, and the difference between those two things is the whole of what you two have been trying to teach me since the cellar, Lampbearer, and I have spent eleven weeks not learning it, and I want it said on the straw, where it cannot be filed, that I am *afraid* of an inch of glass in a dome I do not live in."

"Open it after," Isolde said.

"*After*," Mirelle agreed, in the tone of a woman writing a date she intended to keep.

And then the boy, because the boy could not help himself, and because it was eleven minutes and the white light was getting nearer and there was nowhere left on earth to put a thing like this except a frozen sea that would not write it down:

"There's a jar," Tam Ovre said, at the sledge, still whittling nothing, in the register he used for counting and dying. "Forty-first. No label, no inventory, and it is *empty*, and every soul out here has asked me about it twice and I have never once told anybody the reason, because the reason is not a good story and the Ashlands only lets you tell the good ones. The reason is a lady who came down the long road into the deep the week my mother went under. She made the twelfth knife for me out of a service-tube seal, she taught me eleven jobs, and job nine is *keep one jar empty, always, and you fill it on the road* — and I have kept it four years, and lamp-girl, I have never once in four years found a thing worth putting in it that anybody else wanted. There's the arithmetic for you, out here where the straw keeps nothing: an empty jar is not *hope*, it is a *hole with a shape round it*, and a boy that nine domes never gave a single thing to and a ring of drained strangers gave everything to has been carting one empty pot up and down the dark since he was nine, waiting to be able to *hand somebody something*, and there has never once been anything in it worth the handing, and tonight I will have to stand at the door of the sun holding a knife, which is the identical problem said a different way."

Nobody said anything for eleven counts, which is a long silence on a road and a very short one on a frozen sea. And then Isolde asked the only question the boy's story had left her, in the flat voice, because she could not ask it in any other:

"Would she have taken it? The lady. If you had had something."

"She took the *knife*," Tam said. "She took the eleven jobs I do out of her. But you don't *get* those, dome-woman, she *made* me take them, there's a difference and it's the whole difference, and it is exactly the difference you are going to have to work out for yourself before you can stand at that glass and be any use to anybody."

He went back to the knife he was whittling, because his hands needed something to do that was not this. "The one who is standing in front of me with her mother's unfinished harvest under her chin," he said, "is not going to answer that for me, because she has never given anybody *anything* in her life and she would have to think about it first, and I have waited four years, I can wait for you to think." And then, because he was thirteen, and because the thing arrived in him sideways the way the good things do: "That's the question, dome-woman. Out here on the straw, in front of the door, in the last eleven minutes, that is the *only* question, and there it is: *have you ever given anybody anything*, and I want it honest, and I want it in your own voice, and I do not want it in the one you say at beds."

And Isolde Vane had forty-one ward lists, nine domes, a road, a sledge, a knife between two palms, and two hundred thousand lamps going out one by one behind her in the dark, and she opened her mouth to say *the sea, and the readings, and the jars at the crossroads*, and the arithmetic arrived first, the way it always did, and it said: *the sea is in a jar because a dying woman put it in your hands and you billed the hall for it; the four readings were given free and cost you a career, which is a payment, which makes it a purchase you made out of yourself; and the crossroads is in nine days, and it has not happened yet.*

"No," she said.

The word came out in the voice from the ward lists. Not flat. Not professional. Not *good*. It came out small, and it came out in the wrong order, and she had to say it a second time because the first one had no air in it.

"No. Not once. Eleven weeks, four hundred and eleven beds, and one road, and I have never in my life put a thing down for anybody and left it there, and every single kind thing I have ever done I have *paid* for, and the paying made it safe, and the safe made it *nothing*, and I have known this since the cellar and I have been *waiting for somebody else to say it out where the walls can't get it*, and you are thirteen years old, Tam Ovre, and you have just done that on a frozen sea, and I want it noted that I am *not grateful*, because gratitude is a coin, and I am *changed*, which is worse and which you did not ask for."

"I know," Tam said, and went back to not whittling, and after a while, in the smallest possible sentence, which was the entire text of his mercy: "That's nine domes' worth, dome-woman. Say it again in a week and I'll believe you."

"*Nobody* gets a straight answer out of you," Mirelle said, at the sledge.

"Nobody gets a *wrong* one," Tam said. "That's not the same job."

And that was the straw, runners, and there is no item for it, and there cannot be, and it is the reason the Kindling's record of the fourth circle begins at the plaza and not eleven minutes earlier: what happens on the black straw stays on the black straw, which is the whole of its use and the whole of its price, and if the New Light ever forgets that, the way to remember it is to go out at the blue hour with a jar of your own worst truth and stand in the middle of a frozen sea until the cold takes it out of you, because there is no wall out there, and straw keeps nothing.

And then the exiled Lord Examiner of nine domes did, at the glass door of the First, the last of his twenty-two years, and it was the most Corvin thing that ever happened in the whole road of it: he *set his shoulders the way a man sets them going into a room where he intends to be the only weather*. "My dear. I am going to walk up to that stair, and I am going to *surrender my seal* — the real one, the Charter one, the one he has wanted since the bathhouse, because Assessor Crail is not an evil man, my dear, he is a *promoted* one, and a promoted man wants the *record*, not the flame — and while he is *processing the paperwork of my entire crime* in front of every clerk who will ever write down what happened on this plaza, you are going to go *past* him, *under* him, into the Lantern Chamber, and stand at the great flame *trembling and ready*, because the bells ring in the hour and the *ask* is already begun in nine domes — I can *feel* it through the straw, runners, the whole sea is *humming* — and when the lean comes down the tubes to the lantern it must find *a door*, and the door has *hands*, and the hands have *nineteen minutes*, which is nine more than Corvin ever spent on anything, and *that*—" the gray coat went up into the white light, and the shoulders went *out*, one last time, the way they had stood in forty-one rooms, *weather*, *front*, *the whole disease*, "— that is the closest I will ever come, in this life, to the rest of that *sentence*."

He walked. They went under.

And the plaza, nine hundred years of a people's held breath arriving at the center of it, *did not look at the girl in the dead woman's coat*, because a Lord Examiner of twenty-two years standing on their stair, cuff-deep in the *record*, is the only *weather* clerks are trained to see, and the four of them — the sledge, the bell, the boy, the locked door — reached the Lantern Chamber door at the blue hour's *edge*, nineteen minutes out, and behind them in the plaza Corvin Aelfric's voice, carrying, the last weather of his life, said to Assessor Crail: "*Let me tell you how it goes.*"

And the door of the Lantern Chamber opened, from the inside.

---

## Chapter 20 · Let Me Tell You How It Goes

On the plaza, the audit of Corvin Aelfric took nineteen minutes, and every one of them was *spent*, and here is how it went, because the clerks of the First wrote it down afterward exactly as it happened, which was the last time the Court's own record ever told the truth by accident:

*Item — the late Lord Examiner approached the stair without coat, seal-chest, or counsel, and surrendered to the Assessor pro-tem the Charter seal of his office, and requested the record be opened on twenty-two years of his counts, rooms, and counter-signs, item by item, in the hearing of the assembled corps.*

*Item — the Assessor, being a promoted man, granted this, for the reason stated in his remarks ("let the record show"), and proceeded to take the count of eleven rooms and four hundred and twelve souls in which the late Lord Examiner had, in each instance, written* none. *The corps, on the first such instance, grew uneasy; on the fourth, silent; on the eighth, several clerks were observed to be doing arithmetic on their cuffs.*

*Item — the Assessor inquired by what authority the late Lord Examiner had falsified nine censuses, and the late Lord Examiner answered: "By yours, sir. The countersign that emptied the monk Assur bears my office's blank and your hand's decision, and I have come to tell you how it goes: bedside, funnel, pull, lamp, flame, spending — you cut the calendar twelve days to be in the record of the Miracle, and you will find, in the auditing, that the only miracle in this plaza tonight is* ***the paperwork of a man who meant to spend nine domes' wick and did not know he was the kindling too.***" *The corps at this point was no longer a corps but a* crowd.

*Item — the late Lord Examiner produced, at the ninth instance, a folded paper being his own count of the deep camps, carried in his own coat since the second bell of his own retirement, and desired the record to note that the eleven rooms are not the only uncounted souls in nine domes; that the walkers of the long road appear in no census of any dome whatever; and that in twenty-two years he had written against the column headed ASHLANDS, POP. the same figure nine times running, to wit:* not applicable*, and had believed it every time, which is the whole of his crime in two words.*

*Item — a clerk of the second rank, name entered, inquired whether the four hundred and twelve of the eleventh room were "souls" or "figures", and was answered by the late Lord Examiner, in the hearing of the corps, with the single word* both*; whereupon said clerk requested the record note that he had asked, and had it noted, and stood from that hour on the record-side of the question — which is the manner, runners, in which the Court of the First ceased to exist: not by open refusal, but by a clerk asking the arithmetic to be honest in a room where honesty cost nothing.*

*Item — at the twelfth instance the Assessor requested the late Lord Examiner to state whose countersign appeared on the retithe order of Assur, B., monk, late of the Order of the Book; and the late Lord Examiner having answered* yours, sir, in decision, and my office's, in blank*, the record notes a pause of some eleven seconds, in which the corps of the First understood the entire machinery of the trade in a single lesson — namely that the* blank *is the part that kept forty-one people alive for twenty-two years, and the* decision *is the part that damns one man in nineteen minutes.*

*Item — the Assessor ordered the Lantern Chamber sealed and its warden —*

*Item — before that item was reached, the corps was observed to have developed, in the eleventh minute, a second and unofficial agenda, which the record afterward classified as "interests of the audit" and which was in plain fact the following, and here the stenographer's hand slowed down so as to have more of it, which is how the record got the whole of this far:* a bracket-keeper of the lantern corps, fourth rank, twenty-one years of trimming the tubes of the First in shifts nobody thanked him for, put his palm flat on the pillar beside him — *the way the Unlit do*, the record says, and then, after a line drawn under it, *the way the drained do* — and did not take it off, and a seal-chest clerk stood on the fifth step of the stair with the Charter's own inventory of the eleven rooms in his two hands, having taken it from the late Lord Examiner at the fourth item without, he said afterward, deciding to, and three census-robens who had come to *witness the Miracle* were seen at the ramp-foot with their backs very clearly turned to the lantern and their faces very clearly turned to the *plaza*, which is to say to *whatever was coming in from the domes*, and the Assessor's own first name — the one in the real voice, the one under the forgery of honor, the one the whole plaza had now been *read at*, in item after item, for eleven minutes, like a man being taught his own religion by his own paperwork — *Crail, we have kept eleven rooms unburned for twenty-two years under our own noses* — the sentence was not said out loud on the plaza. It was said, later, by a great many people, in nine domes, in the flat voice, and *the walls have all of them, unlabeled.*

*Item — the seal-chest clerk having read the eleventh room aloud, the corps was observed to turn, as one body, toward the service doors; and a woman of the crowd (unnumbered; low rows; Fourth Dome, by her coat) asked the assembled officers of the Court, in the plain words and in a voice like nothing at all, whether the four hundred and twelve named in the inventory were to be harvested in the morning; and was answered — by whom the record does not say, which is itself the item — with silence; and the record further notes that the said silence measured, by the plaza clocks, six seconds, and that in those six seconds a body of trained officers discovered that no form existed for the question, and that the discovery is what the record afterward calls* ***uneasiness***.

*Item — the Lantern Chamber having no warden since the exile of Vane, and the post of Lantern-warden having been* created, with no paperwork, *by the late Lord Examiner on the night of the exile, and the Assessor having* retired *that night's superior without abolishing that night's* act, *— the chamber had, in law, one occupant, and she was inside it.*

What the record did not write, because there is no form for it, was the eighteenth minute: how Corvin stood in the white light with his cuffs done up and his *front* gone out of him like a lamp going down a pipe, and how Assessor Crail — tall, correct, the third son of a bracket-keeper, no monster, no merchant, only a *promoted* man, which is the scariest kind there has ever been in nine domes — leaned past the seal on the desk and said, softly, in a voice only two people in the history of the plaza had ever heard *unpleasant*:

"You kept eleven rooms for twenty-two years, and I want to know, *before the bells*, whether you would keep *one more* — because if the girl in the chamber is what the wall-sermon says she is, and if the lean is what your own *counts* say is coming down the tubes tonight, then in one minute there will be no Court, no census, and no *record*, Corvin — there will be nine domes of people who have just been *robbed of every kept thing they ever loved by a* plan — *and you and I are the only two men on this plaza who have read the arithmetic both ways, and I am asking you, as one clerk to another, in the last minute the record will ever hold us: which side of the funnel do you* sign?"

And here is what the crowd saw, and what the record made "uneasiness" of, because the record cannot hold the real thing: the late Lord Examiner of nine domes, with the Charter seal on the desk between them like a lamp between two people at a bedside, did not answer. He *looked down the spiral ramps of the First Dome, toward the service doors, toward the black straw, toward the sea*, the way a man looks at weather he cannot file, and his hands went to his sides, palms out, the posture of the long stair, and what he said, when the bells began, was not a signing at all.

It was, in the hearing of every clerk who would ever write down what happened on that plaza, in the flat voice — *Corvin had a flat voice, it turns out, he had learned it from a woman with a funnel in the next room, twenty-two years ago, and had spent his whole career* not *using it* — and what he said was:

"*Let me tell you how it goes.*"

He walked down. The bells walked up.

And the plaza came apart, quietly, in the way an institution comes apart when the one thing it has never been able to file walks off its own stair: no riot, no shouting, no *hour* of it — the corps of the First had been trained forty years to stand at a railing and record, and the trained thing held, and *what held it was what broke it*, because the trained thing says *the record comes first*, and the record had just been handed, itemized and countersigned, to a *crowd* — the bracket-keeper with his palm on the pillar; the seal-chest clerk reading the eleven rooms aloud to whoever stood near enough, in the flat professional voice of a man doing a duty nobody had assigned him; the census-robens at the ramp-foot turned bodily now, facing out, *watching for the columns*, which is the sentence that ends a court, runners, that is the whole of the end of a court in nine words, *the watchers have started watching the other direction* — and Assessor Crail stood alone on the lantern stair with the Charter seal of the office of Lord Examiner on the desk before him, unclaimed by either of the two men who had any right to it, and read, in the real voice, to no one, the first line of an item he was not yet in a position to write, having no desk, no corps, and no side: *the undersigned, being the last officer of the late trade—*

And he did not say the rest of it. He put on his coat the correct way, and took the seal, and went *down* after the gray coat, because a promoted man, in the last minute, does the one thing he has always done, which is to be *in the record where the real thing is*, and the plaza let him go, because the plaza had bigger weather to see.

And in the Lantern Chamber, with nineteen minutes of somebody else's audit coming up through the pipes as a sound like a heart heard through a wall, Isolde Vane did the only trade she had, which was to make a ward.

She laid it out on the floor of the chamber in the white light, because hands that know a shape will make the shape even when there is no room left in the world to make it in: her own case, the one she had carried out of the Ninth and had never once been able to leave anywhere; three lamps — two small and the big one; the badger brush; the silver funnel shaped like a flower that had given up, which had come with her through eleven weeks and a road and was the only object in the world she had ever been paid to touch; and forty-one muster-margin leaves, which was the only book she owned now.

Then she wrote the list, in the flat careful hand, at the foot of the starving flame, the way she had written it at the head of four hundred and eleven beds.

*Patient: the fire in the glass under the plaza of the First Dome. Age: a thousand years, and it is not well. Dome: nine of them; all of them; the wrong ones. Day: this one, and after this one there are no other days on the schedule. Prognosis: not applicable.*

And she stared at the last two words for the length of a held breath, because she had written them nine times herself, and because that was the whole crime, runners — the entire shape of the thing they had built was a trade in which every hard question ends with a clerk writing *not applicable* and going to supper, and she had been the finest writer of it in nine domes, and the god at her feet was dying of the same column.

Under the fourth heading — *shape* — she had nothing to write.

So she did what she had done in every ward since she was nine: she said the tariff out loud, in the flat voice, to the dying, because the dying are the only customers left who are owed an explanation.

"A single pleasant day: three days of oil," she said to the sun. "A face you loved: a winter of roof. A wedding: a season, and the hall takes the dance. A child's first walk: eleven days. A death: nine. A trade: two months. A language: five. A song: one, and it goes badly. The shape of you: a year, in full, and it is the cheapest thing we own." She put the funnel down on the glass shelf, very gently, the way you put a cup down when somebody is coming. "And a morning. There is no column for a morning. Nobody has ever written a price for a morning, because nobody in nine domes ever intended to ask one for *free*, and that is the line I am going to have to put in the front of the book, and I do not know how to say it, and I have four hundred and eleven of these lists in my head and not one of them has ever told me what to do when the patient is not poor but *starving*, and the debt is not three winters but a thousand years, and the debtor — the debtor is everybody who has ever lived, and they are all in the walls, and they cannot sign, and I am the only one in the room with hands."

*Item — the chamber of the great lantern, nineteen minutes before the blue hour, occupant one (1) Lampbearer, exiled; and the first rehearsal of the ask, which is on no record because there was nobody in the chamber to be ashamed of her, and which went as follows. She stood with both palms flat against the cold glass and said "I have brought something," and her fingers closed on it. She said it again, and her fingers closed on it. She counted the failures, because the counting kind cannot stop, and the count was nine, and the tenth time she said it to the glass in the real voice and her hands stayed flat and open and she had nothing whatsoever in them, and that was the eleventh failure — because an open hand that is empty is not the motion, runners, it is only its shape, and the difference between the two is the whole of the road that is left to learn.*

And at the twelfth try the flame did something it had not done in a hundred years of leaning, which was to answer.

It did not speak. It did not brighten. The ring of shadow at the bottom of the glass came up her sleeves — she felt it arrive the way you feel a draught in a room with no window — and the thin blue curl of a starved fire touched the pad of her right forefinger where it lay against the glass, and took a thread out of her the way Maren had taken the sea, gently, and without asking, because it had forgotten how, and had not had a harvester's hands on it in nine centuries.

Then it let go, and Isolde sat down on the floor of the chamber in the white light and looked at her hand.

There was a white line across the pad of the first finger, no bigger than a grain of salt, and it hurt enormously, and it was the first scar her hands had ever had.

Because that is the item the whole chamber was waiting for, runners, and it is not on any form: old Maren, at a bedside in the Ninth, at the beginning of everything, had held up Isolde's two hands as the evidence that she was nobody — *no ring, and no scars* — and the flame, starved, untaught, and now at last supplied with a harvester, has amended one of the two, and the domes will take a good deal longer on the other.

"So," she said, out loud, to a god, in the real voice, eight minutes before the bells, holding up the finger the way you hold up a thing somebody has handed you that you did not know you were waiting for. "*That* is how it is done. Somebody has to *pay*, and there is nobody in nine domes left who will, and so it has come down the pipes to a woman with no ward, no seal, no mother's shape to offer and no idea whatever what a morning costs — and you took a *fingertip*, and you took it *badly*, and you have been doing it badly to everybody for a thousand years because there has never once been anybody in this room willing to stand still and let you practise on *them*." She put the finger in her mouth like a child and sat with her back against the cold glass of the lantern, and laughed, once, which was the first laugh anybody had heard in the chamber of the sun in nine hundred years, and the glass took the shape of it and kept it, and the Kindling will read that shape aloud to visitors for two generations and nobody will ever be able to say what is funny about a fingertip. "All right," she said. "All right. I am going to ask you for the biggest thing that has ever been asked, and I am going to have to do it with my fingers open, and I have failed eleven times, and I have got eight minutes, and the only thing in this coat that anybody ever gave me freely is a dead woman's sea I have not been able to hand back — so let me tell you how it goes. You hold the funnel, and I ask for the memory, and it has to be *given*, and you will feel a pull, like drawing a thread from a sleeve, and afterward you will not miss it, because you never miss what you do not remember."

*Item — the large lamp of Vane, Isolde, exiled Lampbearer of the Ninth, at the hour of the rehearsal: the shape of it is not filled in. The owner has written the four headings of her own trade on the floor of the chamber of the sun, has been answered with one (1) scar, and has understood, at the eleventh failure, that the ask cannot be rehearsed, only paid for, and that whatever she intends to put into the glass will therefore have to be something she has not yet decided to give away. This is the last item the road wrote in her hand before the bells. It is filed under READY, and the word is doing enormous work.*

And in the Lantern Chamber, at the exact blue hour, in the dark white light under the starving flame, Isolde Vane put both palms flat against the glass of the great lantern, *trembling and ready*, and asked—

---

## Chapter 21 · The Blue Hour

The bells began in the Ninth, which was where they always began, because the poor are punctual.

And tonight, in the Ninth's low rows, three things happened at the one sound, and none of them were in the schedule, and all three had been *practiced for nine hundred years*: the ward-fires burned blue, as the law demands; the census-criers opened the tithe-house doors, as the law demands; and in the spaces under the market, in the eleven rooms the Court never thought to look for, four hundred and twelve hands put themselves flat against four hundred and twelve walls, *trembling and ready*, and *asked*.

The hum went up.

And because the counting kind cannot stop, and because nine domes did the strangest thing ever done to a bell on the same night for the first time in a thousand years, here is the whole of the stroke, itemized off the wall-shapes the kindling have since read aloud at a hundred bedsides, in the plain words, dome by dome:

*In the First* — the Plaza Dome, the rich one, the one with the lantern at its center and the Charter on its wall — the bells rang and the tithe-houses opened and the keepers came out of the good rows with their year in their hands to put it in the box the way they had been taught to put it in the box all their lives, and found the *box* already *empty*, having been emptied forward, and the clerk on the desk, being a decent clerk, told them the plain truth he had no form for: *it has gone somewhere and I cannot write where, and you are not being robbed, and I do not know which of those two sentences will help you.* And a bracket-keeper's wife in the queue read his face and said the sentence the whole dome then said, in nine languages of the low rows: "*Then it was given.*"

*In the Second and the Third* — the scriptor-domes, where a memory was worth more than a roof and the debt-tables had run for nine generations — the census-criers opened the doors and *nobody came*, which is the item the record still cannot classify, because the second and third domes were *trained* by the Kindling three winters back by a runner with a scarred neck who taught them the way she teaches everything, which is *by not telling them what it is for*, and at the bell-stroke both domes stood in their own keeps, doors shut, hands flat, *asked*, and paid nothing, and *nobody in either dome has ever since been able to explain to a Court why the room felt richer after the bell than before it.*

*In the Fifth* — the wedding dome, the one with the ordinations in its walls, the dome the sermon in the amber named first because a shape of ordinations is the loudest shape there is — the bell rang *mid-ceremony*, which is the only reason anyone ever got the whole of it in the right order: the pair at the pillar had their hands flat on the wall in the *rite of two houses*, which is the old domestic shape of the same posture, trembling and ready, and when the hum came *up* through the pillar the whole hall went with it, sixty keepers at once, and the preserver of the fifth dome — an old woman who had said the words four hundred times and had never once been *asked* by anything — put her own two hands on the pillar *over* theirs and said, into the bell, in the voice she had used for the ordinations all her life and never once for herself: "*Yes. Both of them. All of it. Go on.*" The Fifth went *short* by four hundred ordinations and gained, in the same stroke, the four hundred wall-shapes of a dome that had watched an old woman say *yes* to the sun. That is the trade's arithmetic, runners. It has *always* been that. It only ever got filed on one side.

*In the Seventh* — the boiler dome, three levels under a junction, the dome that had buried the most and remembered it least — the bell rang in a hall of forty-one lamps that had been *kept* for eleven years by a woman who was standing in the Sixth's lane at that exact moment, and the man who took her place at the Seventh's stair-foot was the old clerk Harb, who had come up from the tithe-house desk on the *when*, who had a pension to protect and had just, at the age of sixty, decided otherwise about what to do with it, and who put both palms on the Seventh's center pillar in the *full* hearing of the boiler watch and gave the year and *did not sign it*, which is the only line in this book's whole paperwork that cost a man anything on purpose, and the Seventh's hum went up *through* him, and the watch *watched*, and *nine of them did the same thing on the following bell*, and that is how a court ends, runners: not when the mob comes, when one clerk will not countersign.

*In the Eighth* — the cold dome, the low one, the one nobody wrote about — the bell rang and the census opened its doors and found on the step, in the frost, already laid out in the shape the deep camp uses for the ones who cannot walk, *its own year*, in jars, unlabeled, forty at a time, given by forty walkers who sat down in the drifts afterward, facing the thin glass, hands in their laps, and could not have told anybody in nine domes what they had done or why, which is the item Hask read aloud in the Sixth two days later off his own slate with a line under it: *the Eighth gave it and does not know it gave it. Write that one down as the last word of the whole trade. Write it first.*

That is what the record called it, afterward — "an unwont vibration of the dome, attributed to the pipes," because the Court's record can hold anything except the real thing — but what the *Ninth* felt was this: the whole dome *breathed out*, every wall at once, a nine-generation sigh of sea-water and grass and ordinations and pears and gratitudes going down into the pipes, up into the tubes, out across the black straw — *the year is small*, Isolde had said, *the everything is the wick, and the everything is leaving by the door we opened* — and in the market rows, at the same moment in the same bell-stroke, nine hundred Kindling runners stood at nine hundred service doors and *took* it as it came: the keepers of the low rows fell to their knees in the drifts and *carried*, the walkers stood in rings and *leaned*, and the jars came up off the sledges, unsealed, forty at a time, the whole labelled love of two generations going out into the glass—

And at the Sixth, in the procession's lane, where the drained had been arriving all night from the long road with the bells in their stride, Mirelle Dax — who had not asked to be asked, who had come anyway, who was trimming a lamp at the stair-foot of her own life — found, in the last ring of hands that came up out of the deep toward the Sixth's thin place, a pair she knew *by the trimming*: quick hands, clever hands, hands that had once given a roof *freely, because it was the only door left*.

"Mama," Mirelle said, in the voice of a locked door, in the bell-stroke, in front of nine hundred runners and forty-one trained hands and the whole arithmetic of mercy, and did *not* say *it's all right*, and did *not* say *you're at peace*, and knelt in the frost in her runner's furs, and held out both hands, palm to palm, the way the dying press them at funerals, and asked — because the road peels, and the rite can be spent one way or the other, and there is exactly one pair of hands in the world she had been *labelling* for eight years — "Mama. *Come with me instead.*"

And the drained woman's hands — which had walked the long road nine winters, which the Sixth's walls had kept and the Kindling had read and the procession had *taken down toward the deep* — the hands *leaned*, and came up, and *opened*, and *took*.

Under the great lantern, at the center of the First, in the dark white light, Isolde Vane put both palms against the glass and *asked* the thing she had been made to ask, the whole road in her coat pockets, sea-gratitude and leaf and knife, and the starving flame — which had eaten its own body for two centuries, which had curled in the ring of shadow the day her mother proved the trade, which had been *leaning, leaning, leaning* down the tubes of nine domes toward the thin glass of the world — the great flame came *down* to her hands the way tide comes into an estuary, and she did not keep *one* shape of it, and it went *through*, and—

The bells skipped.

Every clerk on the plaza felt it in the cuff-arithmetic: the stroke that should have rung *nine* rang — *eight*. And then the thing no schedule in the history of the Court had ever held, the thing Assessor Crail had cut twelve days to prevent and Corvin Aelfric had spent twenty-two years counting toward, the thing the whole rite was *built* to keep from ever happening at once: in eight domes, at the same bell-stroke, on the same practiced night, the year sheared *short*.

Because the wick was already gone. Because nine hundred years of the fourth circle had emptied every wall, jar, and keep into the glass before the lantern could *pull* — and what arrived at the Ninth, and the Sixth, and the low rows of them all, when the census opened its doors to the tithe, was a dome's worth of *nothing to take*: clean walls, cold pipes, and in the market, a thousand people standing in the blue hour having lost a *year* and *kept*— having kept, having *been asked*, having hands full of humming *everything* that was walking, right then, down the black straw toward the First Dome, and behind them, out of the deep, in a sound none of them had ever heard except in the wall-shapes of a dead century —

*turning over, in its sleep.*

The ninth bell did not ring. The great lantern's white light went, in front of every clerk who would ever write it down, *out* — the first darkness in a thousand years at the center of the world — and in the dark, under the glass of the great lamp, Isolde Vane stood with her hands still pressed to the flame's bracket, *empty as a room after a bell*, and the floor of the Lantern Chamber, the oldest stone in the world, *breathed*:

in.

And out.

And *waking*.

And the cost of the *ask*, which was paid in that minute at the flame-bracket in the first dark at the center of the world in a thousand years and which Isolde did not report for two days and which nobody in the Kindling found out until the last hour at the crossroads, because it is the sort of thing the counting kind is worst at telling anyone: the nine-dome lean, taken *through* the hands that were braided rather than *around* them, did not spend her shapes this night — those she had kept, and kept, and would carry to the crossroads, and give at the end — it spent her *instrument*. The hands that had read a wall in the Ninth at fourteen years old faster than the examiner of the day, the hands the whole rite had been *built around*, the hands that could put a palm on cold brick and come back with a dead woman's sea: when the great lantern's white light went out and the dark came into the Lantern Chamber proper, Isolde Vane stood with her palms flat on a flame-bracket in the oldest room in the world and *could not read it*. There was *nothing in the walls*. Not no shapes. There were shapes — she could feel *that* there were shapes, the way a man whose tongue has gone can feel that there is food — but the *reading*, the ninety generations of a profession's whole gift, the one thing she had ever been *good at*, had gone *through* with the hum, out across the straw, into the glass, *spent*, because a door that lets nine domes through in one bell-stroke does not come out of it a *door you can read with*, and the flat voice said nothing at all about it, and she stood in the dark, and the floor breathed, and she did not know yet that this is the reason the ending of the book is going to have to be *other people keeping her*, and could not have known that, runners, because you cannot spend nine domes through your own two hands and then be expected to say, out loud, at a bedside, in the amber, *I have lost the thing that made me me* — the bells were still going. There was no minute. So she put her hands down, and did not say it, and went to find the others.

Which is the first thing, and the last thing, and the only thing anyone needs to know about why this book ends the way it ends: *she got robbed in the act of robbing nobody*, and said nothing, because there was a dome to walk out of.

---

# PART THREE — THE RELIGHTING

---

## Chapter 22 · The Waking

The First Dome had never been dark in the memory of anything living inside it, and the living inside it found out, in the one minute after the great lantern's light went out, what the low rows of the Ninth had known for two generations: that darkness is not the absence of a thing.

It is a *room* — and the room was full.

Outside, down the spiral ramps, the plaza of the First became the sound Isolde had only ever heard from the *other* side of a ward-door: nine hundred years of people squinting into a white light that had finally blinked, and the noise that came up through the Lantern Chamber floor was not panic exactly; panic has a rhythm; this was the sound of a crowd discovering that its *weather* had been a *flame*, that the flame had been a *debt*, and that the debt had just gone *quiet* — and then the tubes began to sing, and the singing was the other sound, the one she had crossed a sea of black straw to answer: the whole lean of nine domes, the emptied walls, the unlabelled love, the jars going up off the sledges and the walkers' rings and Mirelle's mother's *opened hands*, all of it, nine domes' *everything*, arriving at the center of the world through glass and iron and the pipes of a machine built by twelve liars to spend it.

Isolde Vane stood at the base of the great lantern with both palms flat against the glass, *trembling and ready*, and the tide came into her.

She had asked for *borrowed hands*, and the hands had come — she could feel them, thousands upon thousands, a fourth circle and a fifth and a ninth, every trained pair in nine domes pressed to a wall at the same bell-stroke, and the lean moving *through the whole network of them* like a held breath passing from lung to lung — and it reached the First *warm*, whole, welcomed, *carried*, exactly as she had said it in the amber, and it was coming through the only door in the machine that opened from the inside, which was *her*, and here was the thing the rehearsal at the Seventh had not taught her, the thing there is no way to learn except *at the bedside, yours*:

The tide does not pass through a Lampbearer the way it passes through a wall.

A wall keeps nothing and feels nothing; a Lampbearer is a *person*, and a person holding the whole lean of nine domes in her hands *recognizes things* — she felt the Ninth come up through her left wrist like a word on the tip of a tongue, and the Sixth through the right, and the shape of every dome was a room, and every room was a face, and she could not keep any of them, that was the *trade*, and the rite of the body is the same rite: *you let it through* — and letting it through *hurt* the way letting go hurts, which is the only way it can hurt, which is the whole of the difference between *grief* and *ledger*, and Isolde stood at the glass of the great lantern and did *both*, at once, in the dark, for nine domes, and did not fall, because the counting kind cannot stop and because Maren's gratitude was in a jar over her heart and the tide was *reaching for it*—

And here is the list of what she recognized, filed in the order it came, because this is the one page of this book that is not a scene but an *inventory*, and the kindling will read it at a bedside for nine hundred years, and it is the only place in the whole trade where a person is permitted to *name the things while losing them*:

*A pears-shape* from the Fifth, so ripe it was a *sound*, from a keepers' garden that had never had one and made one in a wall. *An ink-stain* from a scriptor of the Third who had copied four hundred pages of another man's law and kept, unlabeled, the one page where he wrote his own daughter's name in the margin, in the wrong order, on purpose, as a child does. *Two ordinations* from the wedding dome, arriving *in the middle of each other*, because the pair at the pillar had gone down with the preserver's hands over theirs. *A boiler-hall's forty-one lamps*, and the exact weight of a mended coat on a stair, and a monk's pear, and a grandmother's *grass* — forty years old and *still the first thing the sea did when it came back up*, and Isolde Vane had *eleven seconds* in which to have all of it, and did, and could not keep any, and the not-keeping was the *point*, because on the far side of the not-keeping was a morning, and she had done this arithmetic out loud at a bedside in the amber and there was *nothing* in it now that surprised her except how much it *cost per second*, and *that* was the one item in the ledger the Court had never managed to price: not what a memory is worth, but what it is worth *to the hands that are holding it at the moment they must not*.

And the last thing she recognized, in the last minutes she had left of the gift, before the seam bent overhead, was *not* a shape of anybody dying at all, and it is why the kindling do not call this chapter *The Waking*; they call it *The Minute She Was Nine Domes*: a *child's own seventh year*, arriving up through her left wrist from a wall in the Ninth she had never touched, in the hands of somebody she had never met, because at some hour of that bell-stroke a low-row keeper of the Ninth had put her palms to her own cold brick and *asked* for whatever she had in there, and what she had — unlabeled, unloved, unclaimed, never once bought, never once *paid* — was *a Lampbearer girl of seven, braided in a low ward, who came back up the rows every census-year for eleven years and never once wrote the keeper's name on a slate because she had been taught it was not hers to take*, and the girl in the wall *turned her head*, and Isolde Vane had the shape of her own smallest self in her hands for *one held breath*, and could not keep it, and did not try to, and did the thing in that second that the whole of this book has been walking toward without ever once saying out loud: she *gave it back to the stranger*, palm to palm, nine domes away, in the only minute in the history of the trade where a memory was *read* instead of *pulled* — and it is the item she never tells anybody, and the item Mirelle Dax tells *for* her, in the gold, at a rail, in the voice, when the counting kind has finally stopped and the day has begun:

"*She was kept all along. Nine hundred years of it, and she never had to be* ***anybody's*** *keep; she had only to be* ***asked for***, *somewhere, by a stranger, at the same minute she was giving it away. And she gave the stranger back the shape of herself, and did not tell anybody, and there are two hundred and forty reasons in her coat pockets why she would rather have died at the Seventh, and I have eleven years of slates and I am telling you now, in the last chapter of the trade, because the day has begun and the polite version is* ***over***."

That was the second thing the rehearsal had not taught her.

Kept things *lean*. A jar, a wall, a fourth wedge, a heart with a label on it — the lean that was passing through her recognized her pockets the way water recognizes a wound, and what she had kept of the whole road — the sea-gratitude, the leaf, the twelfth knife's share of the boy's story, the *voice* in the wedge that said *my dawn-daughter* — came *up* in her hands like a tide reaching for the sea, and the trade had no exception printed anywhere, and there was no bell left to ring, and the great glass under her palms was *waking*, and Isolde Vane reached with both hands for the thing she had decided in the amber at the muster-slate, out loud, at a bedside, *hers*, and meant to do it now with her whole chest:

And the coat did not open.

That was the third thing the rehearsal had not taught her, and it is the thing I promise you she never once got over, runners, not at the crossroads and not inside the glass — not then, in the largest transaction in the history of the world, with nine domes' hands on the walls at one bell-stroke and the morning coming up through her arms like warm water, did Isolde Vane find out in front of everybody that Mirelle's jar on the stair in the Ninth had not been a *lesson*. It had been a *diagnosis*. The sea-gratitude came up to her palms and would not go out; the boy's knife-shape came up and would not go out; two hundred and forty jar-shapes came up in the lining of a carried life and stood there at the open door of her coat like passengers who have heard the stop called and have decided, politely, to stay on the vehicle. She had the whole third motion available to her — no funnel, no rite, no price, no witness but the glass — and every motion the trade had ever built a hand for went one way, and the one thing she had spent six weeks learning how to do was the thing she could not do at the exact minute she had chosen to do it, and she understood, flatly, professionally, at the end of the world, in the voice of a woman reading a ward list, that the shape she had *managed* to give away under the market, the child's own seventh year, had gone only because it had never been hers.

You can hand back a stranger's coat. It is the keeping that takes the hands.

So she gave what she could give, which was the leaf — off her pocket, pressed flat to the flame-bracket, gone into the waking in one second, because a leaf she had picked up off a cellar floor for free was the only thing in her possession she had never *paid* for and therefore the only thing she could spend — and she kept the rest, all of it, every single jar, in front of nine domes, at the door of the sun, and the *voice* stayed too, the one thing that had never been in a jar at all, because it had been in the wedge, which is in the *hands*, which is where the rite cannot reach.

The voice stayed. The coat stayed. Everything the tide had borrowed went on into the waking and nothing whatever of Isolde Vane's went with it, and it was the largest transaction in the history of the world, and she was inside it the way a door is inside a doorway, and—

The tide *stuttered*.

It was the smallest wrong in the pipes Isolde had ever felt with her palms — a *skip*, a knock, the way a hearth-pipe knocks when the flue is shut — and the great lean that was coming up through the First from nine domes *met the shut*, and backed, and the glass under her hands went cold where it had been warm, and the singing in the tubes changed key into the sound a machine makes when the thing it was built to *spend* begins to arrive at it *undigested*—

And far below, in the pump-hall of the Seventh, a door that should have been open *was not*, and behind it a tall correct man with a valve-key in his hand was performing, with perfect calm, the last entry of his career: shutting the return-lines *at the junction*, so that nine domes' everything would back up into the tubes of the First and burst them, and the Miracle would arrive in the record exactly as Assessor Crail had always intended—

*as a flood, credited to the girl who opened the door.*

---

## Chapter 23 · The Knocks in the Pipes

The knock came again, and Isolde heard it with a Lampbearer's ear — the ear of a woman who had spent four hundred and eleven deaths learning the *sounds a body makes before it stops* — and she knew, in the one second the Lantern Chamber had to spare, what the noise was: not a failure. A *decision*. Somewhere downstream of her hands, a person had made a choice that nine domes were about to pay for with their *pipes*, because the tubes of the First were old glass on iron legs, and glass does not bargain, and the tide does not *stop leaning*—

The door of the Chamber opened the way everything had been opening all night: from the inside, for the hands she braided — and it was not Tam, it was not Mirelle, it was a gray coat in ribbons, an exiled Lord Examiner of nine domes who came in out of the dark with frost on his shoulders and the Charter seal in his bare hand, and behind him, on the stairs, the plaza was *noisy* in the bad way, and he looked at the glass under her palms, and at the cold seam crawling down it like a healing wound opening in *reverse*, and he said, without weather, flat, the voice he had learned in the room next to the braiding:

"The Seventh junction. Crail has the key-room. He closed it *legally* — there is a standing order, my dear, mine, from the first week of the retirement, that the junction may be sealed *to protect the lantern* in the event of *unauthorized flow*, and he has read it to the corps, and the corps has *signed it*, and in nineteen minutes the tide you have been giving into that glass since the bells will arrive at a *shut*, and the tubes of the First will do what old glass does, and the record will say *the exile burst the domes*, and the Court will spend the morning *building the rite back out of the pieces of this night*—" he laid the Charter seal on the flame-bracket, between her hands and the glass, the way a lamp is laid between two people at a bedside "— I am telling you the arithmetic because there is one door in the machine left, and it is not *here*, and it is *three levels under the Seventh*, and the man holding it shut has spent his whole life wanting one thing, and I *made* him, I counted his room for twenty-two years and did not burn it and here is what he became, and I know the *one sentence* a promoted man will trade a junction for, and I cannot say it, my dear — I have never been able to say the *one thing*, it has been my whole disease, you should have *studied it*—"

"Then let me say it," Isolde said, in the flat voice, at the glass, at the waking, at nine domes' tide backing up under her palms. "You taught me the rite and the road taught me the other thing — *which side of the funnel do you sign* is not a question a clerk asks a *crowd*, locked door, it's the only question a *crowd* ever leaves a clerk alone to answer — and what does Crail want, *exactly*, in his own ledger-voice, the *real* one, not the one at the muster—"

And Corvin Aelfric did, in the dark of the Lantern Chamber, with the sea of black straw under the glass and the oldest stone in the world *breathing* two feet from his boots, the last thing he was ever going to do that was *not* arithmetic: he *laughed*, once, the way weather does when it breaks, and it came out of him rusty, because the man had not used the sound since a boiler hall, forty-one faces, *fondness*, and he said:

"A *miracle*, my dear. He does not want the flood. The flood is his *duty*. He wants — Assessor Crail, third son of a bracket-keeper, whose name is on the seal that emptied your monk and whose countersign is on *nothing else that matters* — wants to be *in the Ledger*. There is a line, in the oldest glass, that reads *Miracle of the Blue Hour*, and one woman proved the trade on it twenty years ago and the Court wrote it as a *flame* and the woman into the *schedule*, and Crail has read that line every day of his correct life, and if the tide bursts these tubes at the shut, the next line is *flood, exile's crime* — but if the junction is *opened at the moment of the waking by the hand of the Lord Examiner pro-tem*, my dear, then in the morning the Ledger of the New Light will say that *he* — the *promoted man* — *let the miracle through*—" He had the Charter seal off the bracket in his hand again; he had her empty case — when he had picked it up, she did not know — under his arm like a *book*; and the exiled Lord Examiner of nine domes walked toward the door of the Lantern Chamber, into the dark, down toward the sea, toward the three levels under the Seventh where a correct man was standing at a shut with a key in his hand, and he turned, once, at the threshold, and said, in the flat voice: "A clerk will trade a junction for *one line in the record*. It is the only thing I have ever understood better than the flame. *Go keep the glass warm, my dear*, and when you next hear the knock — it will *stop* — and you will know the price of the line I have just gone to *pay*—"

The knock came again before he had gone three steps.

And it was not one knock now, and it was not *below*: it was in the wall of the Chamber, in the door-frame, in the frost of the Charter-seal on the bracket, and Isolde heard it with the ear of a woman who had spent the whole night letting things through, and she understood the *second* thing the rehearsal had not taught her, which was that the tide, shut downstream, does not wait politely for clerks: nine domes' everything was *already in the First's pipes*, and the glass on the iron legs was older than the Charter, and the first of the tube-seams overhead, out on the black straw, in the dark, on the way to the Seventh—

*sang*.

A seam the length of a corridor, nine hundred years old, *singing* where the tide pressed, and the whole of the Lantern Chamber going cold upward, and Isolde Vane had the choice she had come the whole road of her life to make, and it looked, at the last, like nothing more dramatic than a pair of hands: she could keep them on the glass — hold the Chamber door open, keep the waking fed, save the *center* — or take them *off*, run the black straw, and reach the junction with the tide *behind* her and Corvin *ahead*, and—

And the locked door came in through the Chamber door at a run, furs full of frost, Sixth-Dome dark still in her hair, and behind her the boy with the sledge and the *ring*, forty drained walkers coming up through the First's service doors *of their own accord*, and Mirelle Dax took one look at the singing seam overhead and at Isolde's hands still pressed flat to the waking glass, and said the whole of her eight years of knowing in the voice of a locked door with the hinge finally *gone*:

"*Ask the wall and let it through your hands — NOT YOUR BONES, LAMPBEARER*, there are *four thousand* hands under the plaza, *use them*, the junction is a *shut* and shut things need *two* people and the tide needs *everybody* — *I have my mother's hands on my left and Hask's column on my right and a boy who is HARVEST-PROOF standing in the middle of a singing pipe*, and I have followed your *arithmetic* from the cellar of the Ninth to this *room*—" she seized Isolde's free wrist, *palm to palm*, the way the dying press them at funerals, and the way the stair at the ribs had kept for two generations, and the fourth wedge went *warm*, and the voice in it said *my dawn-daughter*, and Mirelle Dax looked at her with everything spent, everything *freely given*, and said: "**Let the glass keep the waking for one hour — it has had nine hundred years of practice — and come DOWN the straw with me, and let us go open a door in a machine, TOGETHER, like the whole *insolvent ledger* of a plan you made me at a muster-slate, in the *amber*, in the *voice***—"

Overhead, the seam *bent*.

And Isolde Vane, Lantern-warden of the First, keeper of nothing, gave the great glass one held breath of her palms, and *took them off*, and ran down into the dark toward the Seventh, into the singing, toward the tide — and the promise she had made on the black straw was not about the *running*, runners, it was about the *brass*, and it is the only kind of promise two clerks can make each other in a world with nine domes and one tide in it: *come as fast as you can, and stand on my side of the wheel with your hands out of your pockets*, and she ran with both hands *flat at her sides*, holding nothing, in the dark, at the speed of the whole book, because a road peels and a promise *holds*.

Here is what the black straw was like in the minute the domes' whole year went down it, filed plainly, because nobody who was there can describe it and everybody who was there can describe exactly one piece of it.

The straw is a road of frozen harvest-flotsam, a finger of old glass and black ice running from the roots of the First down past the Fourth to the throat of the Seventh, and it is where the pipes sweat, and on this night it *rained shapes*. Not water. Wherever the tide behind them found a seam it had not been given, a shape came out of the pipe like breath off a mouth and *stood* there in the dark, uncollected, unlabelled, unsold — a wedding's fourth hour, a man's first frostbite, a whole boiler-hall of somebody else's Tuesday — and the running column walked among them the way a family walks among furniture in a house they are leaving in a hurry.

The Kindling had a law for that, and the law was job nine: *you keep one jar empty, always, and you fill it on the road.*

Tam Ovre ran with the empty jar in his teeth and gave it to her at the fourth seam without a word, because a boy who has been carrying one clean jar his whole life has never once had a road that needed it, and when it finally did he gave it to the person with the worst hands on the straw. And Isolde Vane, who had not been able to read a wall since the blue hour, scooped nine strangers' shapes out of the dark with her palms and got them into glass, and could not have told you what any one of them was.

That is the item she did not say out loud, runners, and it is worth putting plainly in front of you at the moment it happened, because it is the truest thing in the chapter: she had nine hundred years of unlabeled kept things raining around her head, and the only person on the straw who had ever been able to name them was running with an empty jar full of them and *naming nothing*, and the boy beside her knew, and did not comment, and the seam overhead *bent another finger's width*, and she had to decide what to do with a jar she could not label, and she did the only trade-legal thing available to her, which was to press her thumb on the glass where a tag should be and write one word with a knife-point and hand it to Mirelle without a word: *ANY*. And the Sixth's quartermaster put it in her furs against her chest, and that jar came out again years later in the New Light, and it is still there, and it has no owner on it, and every keep in nine domes has a line for it in its inventory, and the line reads the way the Kindling write all the things that were saved and not sold: *the unnamed one; for whoever asks*.

And one stop. The column stopped, because the column had to — there is a stair on the straw where the low rows of the Fourth come up to meet the pipes, and there were people *on the stair*, a family of six in coats, and the mother had a wall-piece out of her own house in her arms, a brick, the size of a book, and she held it up at Mirelle and then at Isolde, and said: *it's my husband, he went in the first bell, and the year is coming down the pipes and I want to know if it is him before I let my children put their hands on it.*

Four thousand hands were under the plaza asking to be used. Nine domes' tide was one seam from bursting tubes older than the Charter. A correct man was standing three levels below them on forty minutes of paperwork, and Isolde Vane took the brick out of that woman's arms, and put both palms flat on it the way she had been taught at seven years old, and pressed, and asked, and got — nothing. Not a blank, not a refusal. *Nothing*, which is what a wall that has been emptied does when the person who used to fill it is standing in front of it with hands that will not answer.

She gave the brick back, and she said, level, flat, at a bedside, in the voice: *It is him. Put their hands on it, he will not bite*.

"You did not read it," Tam said, at the fourth stair, when the column had moved again and the woman had not heard him because the woman was crying in the way that is not about sound. "She asked you to read it and you *told* her."

"I told her the truth out of a jar I cannot open," Isolde said, which is the closest thing to an explanation the counting kind produces when the counting kind is frightened, and she put her hands back at her sides, and ran. It was not a lie, runners, and I want the difference written down plainly because it is the whole of what is left of her: she did not know what was in the brick, she said what was in the brick, and the horrible, beautiful part of the trade is that she was *right* and had no possible way to know it — the brick *was* her husband, the tide *was* carrying him, and the woman put her children's hands on it and got him back a week later at a crossroads rail with a label on him that somebody else had written. The trade had built her out of certainty. What was left was a woman with a knife-scratched word on a jar and a road to run and a stranger's face she would have given anything, *anything*, to be able to name.

And the locked door, at her shoulder, reading the ice as they went because that was the work available, said, without looking up, in the voice of a locked door that has spent six weeks learning somebody else's trade the hard way: "Say it to the quartermaster later, Lampbearer. Out here you can say it to *me* — I have had four thousand hands in my furs all night and not one of them could tell that woman what was in her own brick, and you will not carry an invention about this up the stair, because I will not *audit* you on the run and I will not *pity* you on the stair, and I would rather be *robbed* than pitied, and we are nearly at the pump-stair, so put it in the ledger and *hold still for the brass*."

So Isolde put it in the ledger — the counting kind cannot stop, and neither, it turns out, can the locked door — and the column went down the last slope of the black straw into the singing, and three levels under the Seventh, a small brick room that smelled of oil and authority was waiting for all of them with a shut wheel and one correct man in front of it, and the knock came up through the floor of the stair, one stroke, patient, the way a hearth-pipe knocks when the flue is shut.

*Item — the black straw, blue hour, first dark: shapes lost from ungiven seams, number uncountable, number recorded by the quartermaster as* IMPOSSIBLE *, which is the first time that word has appeared in a Kindling ledger and the last time it will ever appear in a Court one.* *Item — one (1) jar, stoppered, knife-scratched* ANY*, carried into the New Light unlabeled, in perpetuity, for whoever asks.*

---

## Chapter 24 · Which Side to Sign

Three levels under the Seventh, the key-room was a small brick room that smelled of oil and authority, and Assessor Crail stood at the junction-wheel with the valve-key in his hand and his back very straight, and he was *not surprised*, which was the worst thing about him, because surprise is what you can build a sentence with afterward, and Crail had evidently spent the whole night deciding to be a man who was never surprised.

The four of them arrived in pieces — Corvin first, up the pump-stair with the Charter seal in one hand and Isolde's empty case under his arm like a borrowed book; then the boy through the pipe-crawl, because the boy goes where the tide cannot take him, which is the whole of his education in one line; then Mirelle, with her mother's hands still held, four thousand hands' worth of column at her back in the passages, and Hask, and the ring, and Vess Kel, who had come with the Woken because an argument does not end when it is *won*, only when it is *spent*.

And Crail looked at all of it — the exiled Lord Examiner, the exiled Lampbearer, the harvest-proof child, the rebel with the drained woman's hands in hers, the faction, the column, *the crowd* — and said, in his real voice, the ledger one, quiet, correct, the voice of the third son of a bracket-keeper:

"You are forty minutes early and nine domes' tide is in the pipes of the First, and every one of you in this room is a *line I have read*, and I want it noted for whatever record survives tonight that I shut this junction *under a standing order*, in good faith, *for the protection of the lantern*—"

"Nobody has ever protected the lantern, Assessor," Corvin said, pleasantly, and the pleasantness was the rust, the *break*, the laugh still in his voice like weather after the front; he crossed the key-room and laid the Charter seal on the little desk between the two of them, the way a lamp is laid between two people at a bedside, and under it, flat, in his own old hand, a *page*. "I have come to make you an offer in the only language we share. This is my seal, my counts, my twenty-two years of *written none*, the grey book the size of a psalter that has been in the pocket of my coat through eleven rooms and every tube since, with four hundred and twelve names in it in columns small enough to need a lamp an inch away and which has never once in twenty-two years been filed anywhere because a desk opens for a warrant and a coat opens only for a question, and the whole of my crime in itemized form, and on this page is one line in the record of tonight: *'the junction being opened at the moment of the waking by the hand of the Lord Examiner pro tem'* — signed *Crail*, not countersigned, *first name of the New Light*, above the exile, above the monk, above your mistress of the fourth wedge, and *I* will attest it under my seal, which is the only attestation your corps still believes. You will be *in it*, Assessor. The only man alive who wanted to be *in it*."

And the key-room was very quiet, and the junction-wheel behind Crail *sang*, and the singing was nine hundred years of a world's kept things leaning at a shut door, and everyone in the room watched the promoted man's hand go to the page, and stay there, and — *not take it*.

"You think," Crail said, softly, in the real voice, and something in it was *not* duty, which is how every honest sentence in this book has arrived, sideways and personal, "that I want *credit*. That I moved the calendar to stand at a plaza and be *seen*. I want — sir — I want the line to be *true*. There is no other thing in nine domes worth a life of ink. You are offering me a *forgery of honor* with your own seal on it, and you — *you*, who kept eleven rooms *unburned* for twenty-two years and told the Court it was weather — you are standing in my key-room offering me the *one crime* I have never been able to commit, and I have wanted to *strangle you* since the first minute of the audit, and the answer is *no*, the answer is *no*, I will not be the hand that opens the junction in the *record*, because the record will be *read* by the people who will *spend* this—"

And there was one more thing in the real voice, and Isolde Vane was the one who got it, because she was the only soul in the room who had ever been *audited* by him, and therefore the only one he would explain himself to.

"You," Crail said, at the girl in the dead woman's coat — not cruel, not even cold; *tidying*, the way a man tidies a desk he knows he will never sit down at again — "are the question in this room. Nine domes have read your wall-sermon, and the sermon says the waking goes *through pairs of hands that were braided*, and you are the only pair in it. So tell me, in the forty minutes you have left me, what I am to write in the census column headed VANE, I. when the census comes round again, because there will be a census — somebody always runs the census — and I have this one small talent remaining: I will not write a *lie* in a column I have to sign."

"Write *warden*," Mirelle said, before anybody could stop her, in the voice of a locked door choosing the wrong hinge on purpose; and Corvin opened his mouth to be *Corvin*; and Isolde Vane, who had not been able to read a wall since the bell-stroke of the blue hour in a chamber nine domes above this one, said the truth, flat, at a bedside, in the only room left where it mattered:

"You cannot ask me what I am, Assessor, and I am the person who would have told you. I have read nine hundred walls since I was seven years old and I was never once in my life *unsure* what a person was made of, and I am not able to do it anymore — it went through me with the year, at the flame-bracket, in the ask, and I have not said that sentence out loud to a single soul in this room until this second — so if you want the column right, do what the Kindling do: *have it told to you*, and write what a witness says instead of what an officer reads. And what a witness says is that she was the hand that was braided, and that she emptied a coat into four thousand strangers' palms without being asked, and that she has spent tonight doing the one thing your Charter never trained anybody for, which is *letting go of it*."

"*Ask*," said Corvin, quietly, from under the wheel, in the voice already going thin; "write *asked*. That is the verb. It takes *pairs*, plural, and of the four in the trade it is the only one that has never once been entered in a Court ledger — I have watched her do it twice and I have never seen it *written*. Not on my page, Assessor; I withdraw the page, take it off the desk, that was a *bribe* and you were right to call it one. In the *census*. Put it in the census, in your own correct hand, uncounter-signed and unforged, and then you will be in it the honest way, which is the only way you were ever going to get in."

And the promoted man took the page off the desk after all, and did not sign it, and put on it instead — in the column headed VANE, I., where nine censuses had said *titheable* and one had said *expelled* — a single word, in a clerk's careful hand, and nobody in the key-room saw him do it, and it is in the Ledger of the New Light still.

And the seam burst.

It did not burst loudly; the great glass on the iron legs gave way at the Seventh's throat with a sound like a whole choir taking one breath, and the tide came *into* the key-room pipes from above, and every joint in the room went cold-white *where it had been warm*, and the junction-wheel — shut, shut, nine domes' everything leaning against one small brick room's *decision* — began to *lift its own weight off its seat*, which is what over-pressure does to a valve that is closed and *good*, and Crail stepped back from the desk, and the promoted man did the last thing his whole correct life had trained him for: he *reached for the key to lock it further*, because a lock is what a clerk has when the world is saying *open*—

And the tall coward who had never been able to say the one thing put down the seal, crossed the three steps, and *took the wheel off its seat with his two hands*.

"Assessor," Corvin said, level, flat, weather that had come around from the far side of the world, *asking*, the way a man asks at a bedside when the dying is the fire and the dying is *himself*, "— *a door in the machine* — let me tell you how it goes — *you ask*—"

The wheel turned in his hands; the tide came *through* the junction; and what the whole of Corvin Aelfric's life had never done, the trade did in one held breath and a half, because the tide that passes through a *wall* feels nothing and the tide that passes through a *person* spends *everything*, and he who had stood at forty-one lamps and counted rooms for twenty-two years went out of the key-room *clean* — not hollow, *clean*, the way a lamp goes out when it is *finished* and *fed* and *thanked*, which is the only way a Lampbearer should ever go, which is the one thing in the rite the rite never taught anybody—

The boy was on the wheel before it stopped; Mirelle's hands were on the seal on the desk before it settled; Vess Kel's column was in the passages before the singing stopped; and *nobody in the whole trade can tell you where Isolde Vane stood in those seconds, and the wall-shape has her at the door of the key-room with both hands out of her pockets and off the brass, flat at her sides, holding nothing, keeping the only promise she ever made*, and the tide went *down the straw toward the crossroads* with the whole four circles walking *ahead* of it, and in the middle of the key-room, at the wheel, in his gray coat in ribbons, the Lord Examiner of nine domes sat down on the oil-floor, and kept the last three seconds of his life for the only sentence he had ever left unfinished, and the flat voice at his bedside — *her* bedside voice, the one he had taught and then *become* — bent down close:

"*Say the rest,* Isolde. *I have run out of stairways.*"

And she let him say it himself, because that is what the road had taught *her*, palm to palm, and what he said, with the wheel still turning under his hands, was not *love*, not *sorry*, not the word the letter had bled off the page; what he said, in the record's own voice, itemized, *audited*, was:

"*the closest I will ever come, in this life, to saying — you were the* ***decent thing***. *All forty-one rooms. It was* ***never*** *the* ***fondness***. *It was the* ***only*** *— "*
