And Hask, at the door, in the flat exhausted voice, wrote the line in the muster-slate *as said*, because the counting kind cannot stop, and the Kindling would keep the wall-shape of it for nine hundred years, and the Ledger of the New Light — which Crail, in fact, began that night, at the little desk, in the real voice, and never once forged afterward — has under the date, in a clerk's careful hand, the only entry that has ever needed no translation:

*Item — the Lord Examiner Aelfric, C., having surrendered his seal and offered one forgery of honor, which was declined, opened the junction with his own two hands and was spent. Read this line first. Everything else tonight is in the* ***decent*** *language.*

---

## Chapter 25 · The Crossroads Under the Ribs

The glass of the world was thin at the crossroads the way a promise is thin, and on the night after the greatest bell never rung, *everybody* was there, which is the only sentence the Kindling's own records ever wrote about the waking, because every other sentence anyone tried was either too big or too small and the wall kept all of them and let people *read* which.

The four circles came down through the ribs in nine columns and two more nobody had counted: Hask's bearers with the sledges and the forty jars going up off them, unlabeled, unkept; Mirelle's Sixth with her mother's hands *held* at her side like a rope paid out by a sailor; Vess Kel's Woken, who had come to force a door and found everyone *already asking*, and who stood now at the back of the crowd with her rope-worker's arms down and her face doing the thing a face does when the thing it wanted arrives *better than it was planned*; the eleven rooms of the sleeper-cells, four hundred and twelve trained hands in nine domes' coats, standing in the amber under the ribs beside the people they had been pretending not to know for twenty-two years; the ring of the taken, hundreds deep, seated facing the thin glass *at last correctly*, and in the front row the walker who had come up fourteen stairs with the jar — who had *followed the tide back down*, because the counter-lean, it turns out, was never the Vigil *returning* things—

It was the Vigil *practicing*.

Tam Ovre stood between the two of them at the glass, left hand the Lampbearer in the dead woman's coat, right hand the locked door with the hinge gone, the twelfth knife laid flat across both their palms like a rail on a bridge, and nobody had to explain it: the boy had been explaining it since the warm hole, *this is what it needs, two people holding the same thing*, and the whole arithmetic of mercy had arrived at the crossroads with four thousand faces and one small brown scavenger in the middle *because he was born there*.

And there were, in the hour before the sermon, four arguments left to close, and the kindling settled all four of them under the ribs of the world in the space of one lamp-trimming, in the plain words, because there was no more time for the other kind:

*The first argument was Vess Kel's*, and it was the best argument in the Kindling, and Isolde had never once managed to answer it, and she answered it now, in the flat voice, at a pillar, with four hundred Woken behind a woman whose rope-worker's arms were folded and whose face was the face of somebody who had come to a funeral with a hammer and found a *wedding*: "You are right that we should not have waited twelve days, because waiting is what the domes do to the poor and I have *seen* what it does. But your waking is a *pull* with no hands in it, and a pull with no hands in it makes *wick*, and wick is what the lantern eats, and if you ring those bells early the Miracle arrives *hungry* and eats the walkers *first*, because the walkers are the nearest lean. That is not a *caution*, captain; it is your *brother's arithmetic*, and there is one way in this world your brother comes up out of the deep *awake* instead of *empty*, and it is the way where *every pair of hands in nine domes is at a wall at the same stroke*, and you have four hundred of them, and they are *trained*, and they have been *angry* for two years, which is the same muscle as *ready* with the direction wrong. So keep the hammer. Put it down *at the glass*, in the outer ring, with your hands flat, and be *angry at the thin place*, because that is the one place in this book where being angry at it *helps*."

And Vess Kel had no answer, and the Woken had no answer, and what the Woken did instead is in the record of the New Light in nine words: *they went to the outer ring and stood.* Which is, runners, the whole of the history of that faction in one line: they had wanted for two years to *do something at the glass*, and a woman in a dead man's coat finally told them the something was *standing*, and they stood *better than anybody*, because nobody else in nine domes had spent two years practicing it.

And Isolde kept, at the glass, the one small promise she had made at a pillar in the listening-room: she said the number out loud, in the flat voice, with the captain of the Woken standing close enough to be counted — *one (1) brother of the captain of the Woken, third condition, year of the eleven, number one hundred and thirty-one of the procession's list, walked up out of the deep awake, and not empty, and not written off* — which is the item that teaches a people what a promise is for in the New Light. Out here they are not *felt*. Out here they are *filed*, and handed over at the hour, and said again in front of the person who has to hear them, which is the same thing the domes had been doing with a ledger all along except that this time the entry came out *right*. And Vess Kel, who had spent two years hammering at a door and eleven minutes being argued out of it, did not thank her — the Unlit do not thank, they persist — she simply put both palms flat on the thin glass beside the number, and left them there, and *stood*, which is the loudest yes this book ever records.

*The second argument was Hask's*, and it was a numbers argument, and it went: nine domes' trained hands, four hundred and twelve sleeper-cell hands, nine hundred runners, four thousand at the crossroads — *the arithmetic of a held door*, the slate said, *is not the arithmetic of a pulled one*, and the answer to it came from the one quarter nobody had counted, in the front row of the ring of the taken, and it goes in the next chapter of this book, which is the chapter where the whole trade finds out what the fourth circle has actually been doing on the long road for nine hundred years.

*The third argument was the boy's*, and it was eleven words: "what about the ones who *cannot* stand" — meaning the walkers who could not hold, the sleeper-cells' sick, Ruen at her table that was not there, the low-row sick in the Ninth's ward who had been *asked* at a bedside by a Lampbearer and had nothing to give but the *leaning* — and the answer to it was the reason the ring was seated facing the glass instead of standing: *you do not have to hold a door with your hands; the Kindling have been saying so for eleven years and nobody wrote it down*, and a seated person's *lean* is a whole person's contribution, and the ring of the taken was, at the noon turning, *the largest single pair of hands at the crossroads*.

*The fourth argument was Isolde's own*, and she did not have it until the sermon, and it is the reason the sermon arrived *exactly on time* and she did not: it was the argument she had been having with herself since a landing in the Seventh with three people leaning over her and a clean bright wall where a face had been, and it goes — *what is a life for, if the sum of the giving is that nobody can read you afterward?* And there is no answer to it in this chapter, and there is no answer to it in the amber at a bedside, and the walls under the ribs kept the question the way walls keep everything: *whole, patient, loud, and available to whoever asks at the right hour*, which turns out to be the last hour of the last night, at a rail, in the gold, in a voice that is not the flat one.

But Hask's numbers argument got, in that same hour before the sermon, the first half of its answer, and the half it got came from the front row of the ring of the taken, which is where the walkers who cannot hold anything sit, and which is where, on this particular day, sat the man who had once come up fourteen stairs with a jar in his hands and no name in his head.

The quartermaster of the New Light had brought a plumb line, because Hask had not been a clerk of the First for eleven years without learning that an argument about weight is settled with a string and not with a speech. He hung it from the rib above the thin glass, forty feet of thread and a lamp-seal for a weight, and he marked the frost at rest, and then he asked the standing rings — the trained hands, the runners, the Woken, all four thousand of the ones with hands — to put their palms flat and *lean* on the glass, and the string did not move the width of a knife.

Then he asked the seated ring to do nothing whatever except sit facing the glass and *want*, and the string moved.

It moved four inches, runners, and every clerk in nine domes who ever wrote *not applicable* should have been made to stand under that rib and watch it, because the four inches was not a trick: the taken cannot hold a door, and the taken have spent nine hundred years leaning at a wall nobody built for them, and the Kindling's whole argument with the Court had always been one argument in two words — *the lean is not idle* — and here at last was the lean with a string on it, measured by a quartermaster who had decided that if the New Light was going to be built out of people, it would be built out of *counted* people. Hask wrote the number in the slate, said *that is the first half*, and sent somebody to find the second half, which is in the next chapter and costs more.

And then Tam Ovre did the thing the boy had been doing his whole life with a knife and a stub of charcoal, and did it in front of four thousand people, and dared anybody to call it small. He went down the seated row with the census-book Crail had not yet surrendered — Hask's own copy of the deep-camp list, the one with no columns — and at the end of the row he stopped at the nameless man with the empty places in his hands, and said, loudly, in the register he used for counting and dying: "Right. You. You brought the jar up fourteen stairs, and nobody wrote anything down about it, and I am *fixing* that. You haven't a name, out here we don't *guess* at names, so you get a number and a keep and a trade, which is more than the domes gave you and both of us know it. Four. You're *Four*, of the crossroads, and your trade is *carrying*, and your keep is this ring, and when the morning finishes I will get you a *better* name out of whatever is left and I will *visit* you and read it to you until you know it. Any objection?"

The man who had been a runner seventeen years ago, and had been drained, and had carried a dead woman's packed harvest back up out of the dark without knowing he had done it, looked at the boy for a long time with the flat empty attention of a person who has never once in nine hundred years been *asked*, and then he shook his head, which is the Kindling's way of saying yes, and the ring of the taken made the sound that four hundred throats make when they cannot shout, which is worse and better than shouting.

*Item — deep-camp census, crossroads ring, first entry: FOUR, trade carrying, keep the front row, entered by Ovre, Tam, age thirteen, in a book that had no column for the drained and now has one, because the boy held the charcoal and would not give it back. It is the only page in the whole trade that has ever been written by a child and countersigned by nobody, and it is filed at the New Light under* ***witnessed***.

And in the last hour before the tide came, Isolde walked out to the black straw at the edge of the ribs to fail privately, because there was nowhere left in the world to fail politely and four thousand people who needed her to be *good at giving things back*, and she stood in the dark with her hands open and said *I have brought something* at the straw, and her fingers closed, and she said it again, and her fingers closed, and she counted, and the count got to nineteen, and at the nineteenth failure a child walked across the straw with a pot.

It was the child from the boiler hall, the one who had corrected her mother twice in five minutes without ever once hearing herself called anything but *little blue*. Eleven weeks had gone by since that room, and the child had a pot of soup wrapped in a coat, and she sat down next to Isolde on the frost without asking, because out here you do not ask, and poured a cup, and held it out.

The low rows of the Ninth had come up the tubes and out through the ribs in the hour after the bell, the whole of them, because nobody who had ever been poor in the dark wanted to be indoors for a morning they had paid for, and Mirelle had found room for every one of them in the ninth column without being asked twice, which is what she had been doing for eight years and what the domes had never once put in a ledger.

"I can't," Isolde said, in the flat voice, because it was the only voice that had ever kept her safe. "I have no form for that. It is a *keep*, it is somebody's, you are meant to be *keeping* it, and if I take it out of your hands I have to write a price and I have nowhere to put it, and you are not supposed to give me things—"

"It isn't for you," the child said. "It's because you're *here*, and it's hot."

And Isolde Vane — who had billed four hundred and eleven dying people in nine domes and had never once in her whole life had anything handed to her by somebody who wanted nothing, who had spent eleven weeks learning the motion and nineteen failures failing it, and who found, at the twentieth try, standing on black straw at the edge of the last night with both hands full of a child's soup and no words at all in her real voice — drank it, and said thank you out loud where four hundred and eleven records said nothing whatever.

*Item — one (1) bowl of soup, given at the crossroads, in the last hour before the tide, because she was there. Filed as* OWED, *in the margin of the muster-slate, in the flat careful hand of an officer who has spent a career writing receipts and has discovered that somebody has handed her one she cannot pay. There is no column. The officer invented a column, and put the word* THANK YOU *in it, and the New Light will use that column nine hundred times before winter, and every one of them will have been started by a child with a pot.*

She asked the child's name afterward — the real one, the home one, the one that is not on a schedule — and the child said *Blue*, with a big B, and Isolde wrote it in the margin next to the soup, in the column she had invented that same hour, and it is the only entry in nine domes in her own handwriting that is a gift rather than a loss.

And at the noon turning, the tide came up the last stair.

It came *up through the floor of the world* — the hum that had left nine domes through pipes and straw and the hands of a dying clerk in a key-room — it came *up* and the ribs rang, every pillar, every jar-shape, every memorial lamp laid face-down on eleven memorials, and the thin glass went *warm*, and the handmark in the rime — the fingers the size of corridors, pressed against the inside of the world for a thousand years — came *off the glass*, for the first time since the first night, and where it had been, the frost *thawed into a shape*, and the shape was a *door*, and behind the door the day began to come up the way morning comes up anywhere in the history of the world that has ever *had* a morning:

*slowly, and from the wrong direction, and asking whether everyone is ready.*

"**DAWN-DAUGHTER**," said the Vigil, and the borrowed thousand voices were *right there*, on the other side of an inch of thawed glass, and every hand in the crossroads went flat — walls, pillars, each other, the taken and the trainers, four thousand palms in one posture — and what the whole road had been for arrived in the voice of a shepherd finishing its sermon: "**THE DAY IS ALMOST DONE. IT HAS ONE UNFINISHED THING. IT IS NOT A LAMP, IT IS NOT A JAR, IT IS NOT A SHAPE THE WALLS CAN KEEP — IT IS A HARVEST THAT WAS INTERRUPTED MID-PULL, AND A TRADE CANNOT WAKE ON A BROKEN RITE. SOMEONE MUST GO IN AND** ***FINISH HER***. **THE MORNING WILL HOLD. THE MORNING HAS WAITED A THOUSAND YEARS; IT WILL HOLD A LITTLE LONGER. BUT IT CANNOT WAKE THROUGH A DOOR THAT IS STILL MID-HARVEST, AND THE HAND THAT OPENS IT MUST BE THE HAND THAT WAS BRAIDED, AND THE HANDS THAT HOLD THE DOOR WHILE SHE GOES IN MUST BE EVERYBODY ELSE. — IS EVERYBODY ELSE ***ready***?**"

And four thousand hands did not answer, because the answer is not a word, and the Kindling have never in the history of the dark said the word *yes* to anything — they simply *leaned*, all of them, the taken and the trainers and the Woken and the walkers, the whole fourth circle and the fifth and the ninth, toward the thin glass, *trembling*, and—

And Isolde Vane put her palms flat into the thawed shape of the door, and did the last thing the rite had ever trained out of her, which was to be *afraid*, and the boy's knife went out from between their hands, and the locked door's hand closed on her wrist — *palm to wrist, the last inch of the hold* — and the voice in the fourth wedge said *my dawn-daughter, watch your hands*, and the voice on this side of the glass said, in Mirelle's own, low, personal, *spent*:

"Then *go finish her*. And *come back through the same door*, Lampbearer, because I have eight years of stair-shapes and a mother's hands and a boy with *twelve knives* and the whole insolvent ledger of a *plan* — and *nothing* out there is keeping you, *not one shape of you*, do you *hear* me — **I am keeping you**."

And Isolde went in.


---

## Chapter 26 · The Whole Paperwork of Honor

The day after the greatest bell never rung came up *nearly* — the tubes of nine domes burning low on a spent year, the sun-gold in the thin glass not yet *through*, the whole world lit like a room at the hour when a candle and a dawn are the same brightness and you cannot tell which one is arriving — and into that nearly-light, the Court of the First came down to the ribs to be *audited*, which is the only verb the nine columns had left for it, because you cannot execute a Miracle and you cannot imprison the side that the *record* says won, and Crail, who had moved the calendar twelve days to be in the record of the Miracle, discovered with a clerk's total clarity that he *was* in the record, and that the record had *other items*.

He came alone, in the gray, down the fourteen stairs with no corps behind him — the corps was at the crossroads already, or home, or in the Sixth eating a stranger's bread; corps do not survive blue hours — and what he carried was not the valve-key, which had turned in another man's hands at a junction three levels down and was *finished*, and not the Lord Examiner's seal, which sat on the little desk in the key-room where he had begun the Ledger of the New Light at the real hour of the real night with the *real voice*, and which he had deliberately left there, because a seal carried is a seal *offered*, and he had been offered everything that morning and had declined all of it on principle. What he carried was the census-book, current year, nine domes, and he laid it on the pillar-head at the front of the ring of the taken like a penitent laying down a weapon he is not entitled to surrender, and he said, to Hask, because Hask was the only officer present, having appointed himself quartermaster of the New Light on the stair with two words (*"the jars"* and *"not yet"*):

"The countersigns," Hask said, in the flat exhausted voice, not looking up from the muster-slate, "are void. All of them. The *Executor* line is void. The *expelled* column is void. What I need from you, *Assessor* — and the title is a wound, I can hear you know it's a wound, hold still—" and the slate came up, and the counting kind looked, and it was, Hask at his most terrible and most kind, "— I need the *numbers*. Nine domes. Two generations of the taken. The procession's walking-camps, the deep camps, the *years*. The Ledger of the New Light has the *decent* language and the decent language is *weather*; I can keep weather. What the walkers need, *before the glass finishes doing whatever it is doing*, is the *lists*, so that when the shapes come *back* — *if* the counting is right and they come back — somebody in this world can say *your name was number so-many* and *you were not written off* and *here is the year* and *here is the roof*. I need the whole paperwork of your honor, Assessor, and I know the phrase is *yours*, and I did not have to borrow it, and I am *borrowing it*, because Corvin Aelfric spent himself tonight on one page of a thing like this, and you are going to hand me nine years of them *willingly*, or so help me I will have the eleven rooms read your *own* census back to you off the walls of the Sixth and it will *not* be a polite reading—"

And Assessor Crail — promoted man, calendar-cutter, the first signature of the New Light, *the only man alive who wanted to be in it* — did the thing the record would have hardest time classifying, and classified it himself, in the Ledger, that evening, in the real voice: *Item — the undersigned, being the last officer of the late trade and holding its only complete lists, surrenders same to the quartermaster of the Kindling, and requests one entry in consideration, to read verbatim: that on the night of the blue hour he stood at the thin glass with everybody else, trembled, and was* ***not*** *ready, and asks the record to note the difference between those two words for the use of whoever comes after.*

"You stood at the glass," Mirelle said, level, because the glass at the crossroads had gone *warmer* that afternoon and every hand in the rings had felt the leaning change, and nobody in the Kindling had ever *let* an officer keep a polite sentence without an audit. "You cut the calendar on the *way down*."

"I cut the calendar to be *in it*, Miss Dax," Crail said, and the real voice was the worst sound in the room, because it was *tired*; forty years of clerks had worn it down to the grain like water on a step. "I have read your late colleague's forgery of honor three times since midnight, and I will tell you the professional opinion of the last officer of the trade on the miracle of the blue hour, itemized, since you will make me anyway: *he bought nineteen minutes, you needed twelve days, and the arithmetic between those two numbers is the only miracle on this pillar, and I want in the record that I saw it, not that I caused it.* Now. The numbers."

And the numbers came out, over the nearly-light, onto forty sledges, in nine columns, while the glass of the world got *warmer* at the rate of a held breath going out — and one line of them gave the whole quartermaster's corps a bad winter, and Hask put it in the ledger himself, with the two counts side by side, because he had decided on the stair that the New Light would not be allowed to be embarrassed by an arithmetic coincidence:

*Item — the coincidence, investigated twice and found to be counting and not design: four hundred and twelve (412) souls in the eleven rooms of the sleeper-cells, which the Lord Examiner wrote NONE against for twenty-two years; four hundred and twelve (412) feet in the procession, which the glass spat out over the same twenty-two years. Both numbers are correct. The Corps has asked, and the honest answer out of nine domes is that the trade made so many people that the numbers began to repeat, and that forty-odd of the walkers sat in those cells as children, which is forty-odd and not four hundred and twelve, and the rest is the ledger doing what ledgers do. Two columns are kept. Nobody has ever been able to stop comparing them, and the Sixth has now printed the pair on the front page of every free keep, so that the comparison is at least being made out loud.*

And at the front of the ring of the taken, in the amber hour, a woman in a Ninth Dome coat she had worn for eight years of walking sat down *facing the glass* with her hands in her lap, name unreturned, and did not know why she had seated herself, and *leaned*; and three pillars down, DRAINED-NAMED-NO-ONE, the hollowed mother of the Sixth, stopped *pacing* — she had paced since her sixteenth year — and stood still at a pillar like a tool returned to its hook, her hands going flat of their own weight, *trembling*, and *not yet ready*, which is the exact posture, Hask noted in the slate with a line drawn under it, of *every single walker in the ring at the glass's new hour*:

*Item — the taken are not waiting for the morning. The taken are* ***practicing for the knock***.

And there was one more item that nearly-light, and Hask made Isolde file it herself, because the quartermaster of the New Light had decided on the stair that nothing went into the glass unlabelled and nothing came out of a coat unpaid for — and this one was going to be the hardest order she ever took, and it took her the whole of the hour, and she did not know when it started working.

The coat had been full for six weeks — fuller than it had any right to be, because she had stood at the door of the sun at the blue hour with her hands open and her whole chest offered and got nothing out of it whatever, and had carried the shame of that failure up through the ribs and into the nearly-light like contraband — and what it held now was Tam's forty jars at her chest, and two hundred shapes in the lining and the pockets and the inner belt, the whole itemized remainder of a carried life, the Fifth's pears and the Third's ink-stain and the weather of a thousand harvests, every one of them paid for out of somebody's head and never once, in seventeen years, put down for anybody at all — and Isolde Vane had never once in seventeen years put a jar down that somebody had not paid her for.

"Give them to the ring," Hask said, without looking up, in the voice of a man who had already counted how many hands at that glass had never been handed anything. "Not the walls. The *hands*. There are four thousand people standing at a door who have spent their whole lives being *read*, and I want every one of them given something before their Lampbearer walks through it, and I want it counted, and I want it *free* — which is the difficult part of the order, so take the hour. Take the hour and take your time with it, because the girl in the Second who has been running lamps since she was nine has never in her life been given a thing she did not have to survive."

And so Isolde went along the rings with the jars in both hands — *palm to palm*, of course, that is the only hand-shake there is out here — and gave them. To a Woken woman with rope-worker's arms who held the small round shape at arm's length for a full minute before she put it under her chin, and then did not take it off again. To a sleeper-cell trainer who could not say the word *thank* and said *this is not nothing*, which is the Kindling for it. To a bracket-keeper of the First, fourth rank, twenty-one years of trimming tubes, who took a shape of somebody's ordinary evening — a table, a lamp, a boy's bad temper about the garden — and sat down on the frost with it and put his hands over his face. To the taken, who could hold nothing, and so she gave the shapes to the people *holding* the taken, which is the same gift going one hand further, which is the only mechanic in this book that has ever worked. And one jar she did not give, and every soul in the crossroads saw which one, and Hask wrote the item anyway, in the flat hand of a quartermaster who has decided that the new ledger will not flinch from a number:

*Item — two hundred and thirty-nine (239) jar-shapes, list impossible, distributed free at the crossroads rings in the hour before the noon turning, by an officer who could not afterward say what she had given away and did not need to, because the record of what a lamp is for has never been kept by the person holding it.*

*Item — one (1) jar, kept back over the left heart, in front of the whole ring, not by stealth: the sea, the grass, the gratitude of Maren, Ninth Dome, died the first night of the census year; label held; not spent at the glass of the First; not spent at the crossroads; owner states she intends to hand it over personally, to whoever is standing there, at the end, and asks the quartermaster to leave the line open, because an open line is the only kind of line a future can be written on.*

That was the third motion, runners. It came out of her at the fortieth jar without any lesson, any rite, any amber, and any warning whatever, and the only soul in the crossroads who noticed was the boy — and the boy, who had never once in his whole life let a thing like that go past without a comment, said nothing whatever, which was the largest thing anybody said all morning.

And somewhere in the deep behind the ribs, in the last hour before the noon turning, Isolde Vane sat on an upturned sledge with her mother's jar — *NOT YET*, the label in a dead woman's quick hand, the pulled thing held at a standstill across a sea of black straw, seventeen years of *not yet* standing inside a second that was a thousand years old, warm as a pulse against her palm — and Tam Ovre sat across her whittling nothing because there was nothing to whittle and he needed the motion the way the counting kind needs the slate, and Mirelle stood behind Isolde's shoulder *holding*, the last inch, and nobody said the arithmetic out loud because the whole crossroads had *heard* the Vigil's sermon and the arithmetic was now *public*:

*Someone must go in and finish her. The hand that opens the door must be the hand that was braided. The hands that hold the door must be everybody else. And what goes in the hand is the hand's own kept life, and what comes out the other side of a finished harvest is* ***nobody's mother, nobody's Lampbearer, and one day older than the world***.

"You don't have to say it," Isolde said, in the flat voice, to the jar, to the *NOT YET*, to the dead woman's handwriting, which had known — not by prophecy, Tam never trusted prophecy, by *paperwork*; the arithmetic of a keeper who packed her own harvest into a sixty-year-old Kindling keep, wrote one word on the tag, and put it in a runner's hands years before there was a daughter old enough to need it, so that a seven-year-old girl could someday sit on an upturned sledge under the ribs of the world with her mother's unfinished harvest warm in her palm and be *able to do the sum*. "You said the whole thing already. In the fourth wedge. *Watch your hands, little door.* That was never a warning, Tam. I have had seventeen years and the rite to learn it and the whole road to say it back. That was a *handover.*"

"Mm," said Tam, in the smallest possible sentence, which was the entire text of his sympathy and all of his agreement, and he stopped whittling, which was how the boy swore.

And the glass got warmer. And the ring got ready. And the last night of the trade — the *real* last night, the one nobody rang anything for — *closed its books*.

---

## Chapter 27 · The Hold

The noon turning came up the ribs the way a tide comes up an estuary — *up*, against every direction the world's water had ever run — and the crossroads put itself in its rings without being told, because the taken had been rehearsing a thousand years and the Kindling only eight, and *everyone*, runners, the record of the New Light is agreed on this point, understood at the same moment what the Vigil's sermon had actually described, which was not a ceremony but a *posture*: **the hand that goes in cannot be held, and the hands that hold cannot let go mid-pull, and there is one inch between those two facts, and the inch is the whole of the rite**.

They built the hold in the hour before, and here is its itemization, because Hask wrote it and the walls remember it and someday a child with empty enough hands will read it off the nearest pillar exactly as filed:

*Four thousand palms to the glass, ring within ring, taken in the front row because the taken are the practiced hands, trainers behind them from the eleven rooms because the sleeper-cells were built to be* ***asked*** *someday, the Woken on the outer ring with their rope-worker's arms crossed and uncrossed, uncrossed — Vess Kel's column having spent its hammering on the stair and arrived at the glass to do the thing it had mocked for two years, which is* ***stand***. *Mirelle Dax at the door-inch with one wrist. Hask at the slate. Crail, in the real voice, at the back, standing, having surrendered the lists that morning and kept one request: to be* ***not in it*** *and* ***watch***, *which is the sentence every late officer in history has asked and only this one got.* *And the boy in the middle because the boy is always in the middle, the twelfth knife laid flat across two palms, the bridge*. *Four thousand hands, one inch*

"It's not a door," Tam was saying, to nobody in particular, in the hour before, having been asked by three separate adults to explain the knife and answered all three the same way. "It's a *rail*. You don't hold the bridge. You hold *the person's hand on the bridge*. That's the whole thing. I don't know why nobody has—"

Teaching four thousand people to hold a door took, in the end, about ten minutes, and a boy did it, and here is the piece he made them drill, because this is the item the Ledger of the New Light files under *mechanics* and every later generation gets wrong: you do not *grip*. A grip is a taking motion. Four thousand hands had to learn, in ten minutes, to put themselves on a stranger's wrist the way you lay a lamp down between two people at a bedside — flat, warm, and *open enough to let go of* — and twice in the teaching the ring got it wrong all at once, four thousand hands closing like one fist around one idea, and the tide coming up the ribs *jerked* at the mistake, and every lamp in the deep went down to blue and stayed blue until they loosened.

"You're holding the *door*," the boy said, horrified, to a rank of trained runners who had been holding doors for eight years and had never once been told they were doing it badly. "Nobody is holding the *door*. You hold the *person's hand on* the door. Say it back to me." And four thousand voices, in nine domes' accents and one enormous hesitation, said it back to him, and the lamps came up amber, and Mirelle Dax stood at the head of the ring with her one wrist already reserved and her face doing the thing a face does when the thing it has wanted since it was sixteen turns up in the hands of *a child* — and not one person in the crossroads looked at her, which is the exact kindness the Unlit reserve for their best days.

"*We* did," said the oldest of the returned walkers, in the front row of the ring, in a voice unused since a bottom nobody had known existed, and every head in the crossroads came around, because the taken *do not talk*, they lean, and this one had been the first to put palms to a pillar and give the lean back, and what he said, slow, the way a shape surfaces in clean water, was the mechanic the whole trade had missed with nine centuries of scholarship: "In the procession, in the deep, in the *last pull* of the long road — when one of us finally cannot walk another step, the ones who can *hold the hands all the way across*. We do not know why. It is not *kindness* —" and the ring *bowed*, four thousand faces, at the word used by a man with no name left, the *exact* word Corvin had spent himself on three levels down in a key-room a day ago — "— it is that the *one who cannot walk* goes *further* when held. We have been *finishing harvests* on the long road for a thousand years with our hands, and *nobody wrote it down*, and *nobody asked us*, and —" and the palm went flat on the pillar, trembling, ready, *the rite done backward by someone with nothing left to keep* — "— it turns out that is *what we were*. The fourth circle is not the *fuel*, child in the coat. The fourth circle is the *hands*. We have been the hands this whole time. We have been *practicing the hold* on each other in the dark for the use of a *door* none of us could see—"

And in the last quarter of that hour, Isolde Vane did four things, and they are filed here in order because they are the whole of her preparation, and not one of them was a prayer, and the fourth one is the item this book has been walking nine hundred pages to get to.

**First**, she ate. Mirelle made her, in the flat voice, with a tin cup and a heel of bread the size of a stopper, saying *you have not eaten since the turnips, I have watched you for six weeks and I have counted that too, and I will not have the hand that opens the glass shake on me at the inch because the dome's poor diet is a Court policy and not a personal failing* — and Isolde took it, and did not argue, and the arguing was gone out of her, which was the frightening part, and the bread was bad, and it was the best thing she had ever been given for nothing, and she has never told anybody which of those two facts is the reason she cried, forty minutes later, with her hands flat on a wall.

**Second**, she went down the ribs to the place where they had laid him, and she put her two hands on the collar of the Lord Examiner of nine domes and straightened it, crooked, uselessly, the way you do it because it is what there is to do, and she took out of the inner pocket of that coat the grey book the size of a psalter, and she carried it up through the rings and put it into Hask's two hands without a word, and the quartermaster of the New Light looked down at it, and read the first page, and did not look up again for a long time.

"It is not a keep," Isolde said, in the flat voice, at the sledges, with four thousand people leaning around them. "It is not a shape, it is not a weather, and I cannot give it away, because it does not belong to me and it does not belong to him anymore either. It is four hundred and twelve names, and it is the only document in the world that has ever said *these people were not written off*, and I want it read at the glass the minute the second opens, out loud, by the person in nine domes who is best at reading lists aloud, and I want it finished, and if it is not finished by the morning I want you to keep reading it, whichever morning that is, and I want you to say the small items at the end of the lines — *the goat*, *the bell*, *bad with cold*, *sings while working* — because he wrote them in an official document on purpose and nobody ever asked him about them and he was the most correct man alive and they were the only words in his whole life he chose himself."

"That is a large order, Lampbearer," Hask said.

"It is the only order I have ever given," she said, "and I would like it filed under *given*, because I have spent forty-one harvests filing things under *taken*, and the quartermaster may note that the difference is the entire difference between the two trades, and that it took a corpse to point it out." And the counting kind took the grey book up off her hands with both palms, the way you take a lamp down from a bracket at the end of the oil, and put it in the sledge under the tarpaulin next to the keeps box, and did not say *thank you*, and she had learned by then that that was the whole of his mercy.

**Third**, she made the boy say the eleven jobs back to her, out loud, in front of the ring, standing, the way recruits do at the stair-foot before their first downward, and she made him stop at the eleventh and say it again, and the second time she had him say it to her and not to the ring: *Eleven: if you get to the end of your walking, and somebody's hand is on yours, let them hold you. You will go further than your legs ever did.* And when the boy had finished the second time she said, flat, at a bedside, in the only voice, to a thirteen-year-old who had been raised by the drained and had therefore known this rule longer than anybody alive: "Right. Then that is what I am going to have to do, and I want you to know that I have decided it, and I want you to know that I did not decide it a week ago, and the reason I did not decide it a week ago is the reason I am going in there now and not before. Watch the knife. Watch the rail. And if I am not somebody who comes back, you will put the twelfth on the rail and you will *tell* people what it is for, because telling is the trade now."

And Tam Ovre said, with the enormous dignity of the absolutely correct: "You're being *sentimental*, lamp-girl, on the stair, in front of four thousand, which is *bad form*, and I'll do the knife."

**Fourth**, and last, and the reason the chapter is called what it is called, she went up to the inch by herself, in the hour before the sermon, and put both palms flat on the frost where the door-shape was, and did the one thing she had done four hundred and eleven times in nine domes and two generations and one winter, and had not been able to do since the bell-stroke of the blue hour at a flame-bracket nine levels above her head.

She asked the wall. And the wall, which had her hands in it, and had had them in it since a cellar in the Ninth, and had been waiting for them, six weeks, politely, the way a good dog waits — the wall said nothing at all. Not a blank. Not a refusal. Nothing, which is what a wall says to the one person in the world it cannot reach, because the reach is gone out of the hands, and the hands are all that is left of the woman who used to have the reach, and the frost sat there with a thousand years of morning behind it and a Lampbearer in front of it and neither of them able to speak the other's language anymore.

So she turned around, in front of the ring, and she said, out loud, level, in the flat voice, itemized, the way you report a loss at a bedside, and it is the sentence the whole road was for:

"I cannot read it. I have asked it three times and I cannot read it, and there is nobody in nine domes left who can do the trade I do, and I am the one who is standing in the place where the person who could do it is supposed to be, and I would like somebody to *tell* me what is in this inch, because I have spent twenty-four years being the one who tells."

And nobody in the crossroads moved, and nobody pitying her was anywhere in the four thousand, and Mirelle Dax came up behind her and put her scarred hand over the back of Isolde's and laid the whole of it flat on the frost again, both pairs at once, and pressed, and asked, and read out loud in the voice of a locked door doing for another person the thing she had taught her and could never do herself:

"There is a harvest in here. Mid-pull. A woman, forty-odd years, hands quick, ceiling cracked, and she is saying a word at the end of it and the word is not finished and it has been not-finished for seventeen years, and the shape is *patient*, Lampbearer, it is not trapped, it is *patient*, the way a held breath is patient when somebody finally comes in the room who is allowed to let it out." And then, quieter, in the front of four thousand taken and eight hundred Kindling and one boy with a knife across two palms, in the voice she never used for anything but this and had used for this exactly once in eight years of road, on a stair in the Ninth, with a jar: "And there is a girl of seven in it, braided, in the next room, and the woman has been hearing her breathe through the wall the entire time, and I want you to know that before you go in, because you are going to want to know whether she kept you *as a keep* or *as a promise*, and the answer, Lampbearer, is that the whole trade ran a thousand years on not knowing the difference and the answer is *the second one*."

"How do you know?" Isolde said.

"Because I have been told," said Mirelle, whose own hands had not been told a thing in their whole lives by anybody, and which were still on the back of hers, flat, warm, open enough to let go of, and which did not let go. "That is what it is to be *kept*, little door. It has always been that. Go and finish your mother's word."

And the sun, which had been *nearly*, went *warm*, and the handmark came off the glass, and the fingers the size of corridors *left*, and the frost thawed into the shape of a door, and the day began to come up *slowly, and from the wrong direction, and asking whether everyone is ready* — and the sermon came, and the leaning came, and the four thousand palms came flat, and *everybody else* was ready, and—

There is a minute in every rite that the Court never writes down, because it is not an *event*, and the minute at the inch of the thawed door went like this. Mirelle went and put her scarred hands on the hands of the hollowed woman of the Sixth — the mother who had paced since her sixteenth year and had come to a pillar and stood still like a tool returned to its hook — and lifted them, gently, the way you take a knife out of somebody who has been holding it a very long time, and laid them, one pair at a time, flat on the frost where the door-shape was. "There you are," she said, in a voice that was not the locked door and had not belonged to anybody at all for eight years of road. "You're on the *good* side of it tonight." And the drained woman of the Sixth did not know her. Did not know any of it. Had nothing in her hands to give and no shape left to keep, and she *leaned* — which was the whole of what a mother had been able to hand over since the census, and the whole of what a daughter had spent two generations learning to ask for without a *for* on it.

And here is the last thing the trade ever taught Isolde Vane, reported in the flat voice at a bedside in the amber of the New Light, two hundred and thirty-nine jar-shapes lighter in her coat, in the hour between the sermon and the step: the trade had trained her to *pull*, the road had trained her to *carry*, the wall had trained her to *ask*, and standing at the thawed inch of the finished door, with the locked door's hand closed on her wrist, *palm to wrist, the last inch of the hold*, she found that every single one of those verbs had been *one verb* the whole time, said different ways by different people on different nights, and the sentence came out of her the way Seren's voice came out of the fourth wedge — *as a practice, which is to say a handover*:

"*Hold*, then," she said — to Mirelle, to the ring, to the Woken with their crossed and uncrossed arms, to the taken who had been holding strangers' hands across the last pulls of the long road since before the Ledger had a first page — "*hold* the way you already do. Don't hold *for* me — a *for* is a *price*, and the day is not a price, and I have *done the sum of that* at three separate bedsides this winter and the ledger is *closed* — just hold, the way your hands hold when the walking *gets to the end of you*, and *let the inch be the whole rite*, because my mother's harvest goes *down*, and the *pull* goes *through us*, and the word she was saying when the waking took me—" and the flat voice, at the thawed door, at the last inch of the last night, went — not flat. *Personal*. Low. Spent. *Free*:

"— *I am going in there and finishing it with her.*"

And four thousand hands did not answer, because the answer is not a word — they simply *leaned*, and Isolde put both palms into the door, and Mirelle's hand closed on her wrist, and the voice in the fourth wedge said *my dawn-daughter, watch your hands*, and the voice on this side of the glass said *I am keeping you* — and the whole world's hands went flat, *trembling, ready*, and

---

## Chapter 28 · Inside the Glass

Inside the glass there was no dark, which was the first surprise, and no light, which was the second, and the reason for both was the third thing, the one no sermon had ever found words for because words are an *inside-the-world* technology: inside the glass, *time was still mid-pull*.

Isolde walked — if walking is the word for what hands do when the floor leans toward them the way every lamp in the world had always leaned toward her — and the place she walked through was not a room and not a country. It was *one second, opened like a book*. A thousand-year second, thick as a cathedral, and inside it, everywhere, in every direction the eye could take, were the *twelve*.

Not dead. Not in a jar. *Mid-harvest.*

She found her mother first because the lean found her mother first — the same way the Ledger wall had known her name before the clerk did, the same way the tube-starved lantern had turned its face — and Seren Vane stood in the middle of the second the way a woman stands at a bedside: bent, both hands on an invisible funnel, hair coming loose, mouth open around *half a word*. Around the pulled thing. The Vigil had told it true at the crossroads: *a harvest interrupted mid-pull*, and here it was, here *she* was, here was her mother's whole last evening still happening at a standstill like a knife stuck in a table —

— and Isolde stood in front of her mother's face for as long as a person can stand in front of something, which is longer than she had planned, because after the Seventh Dome's wall had taken it she had thought she knew what a clean bright room was, and this was worse and better at once: the face was *there*, unspent, kept in the glass the way a shape is kept in a wall, only *warm*, only *her*, only turned at last the way a face turns when it has heard the door—

"*Little door*," Isolde said, in the voice, the flat one, because the counting kind cannot stop even now, even here, *especially here*, "I have come to finish the rite. I have come to *let you go down*, and I need you to hear the arithmetic before I do it, because you taught me the arithmetic is the only honest part of a person—"

Here, then, is the arithmetic, delivered at a bedside in a held second, in the flat voice, level as a slate, because a truth handed over gently is handed over wrong: *Item — the harvest I am finishing is yours, and the thing in the funnel is mine, and I carried it six weeks and never once billed anybody for the walk. Item — I am not here to be saved by you, and I am not here to save you, and our whole family has wasted a thousand years on those two ideas, which are both Court ideas, which are both* ***prices***. *Item — the only thing a daughter can give a harvester is the sound of the knock she has been waiting for.* *Item — I am the knock.*

And the mid-pull woman could not answer — that was the law of the place; nothing in a held second can talk; a held second is a *hand* — but the pull *slackened*, one degree, the way a grip slackens at the sound of the knock it has been waiting for, and the glass all around them went one degree *less tight*, and Isolde understood, the way she had understood at the crossroads with two hundred and thirty-nine jars of her life going into the waiting hands of strangers, that understanding here was *doing*:

You do not finish a harvest by *cutting* it. A thousand harvesters had tried the cut in here, over the centuries — the Vigil had said as much; every walker who ever followed the counter-lean down had tried to *push* the second closed and push makes only wick. You finish a harvest the one way the rite never trained because the rite cannot train it, the way Corvin said on the stair at the ribs, the way old Maren said in the dying room with the sea in her hands, the way the drained man said to the pillar in the amber without knowing he had said it:

*You ask. You press your palms to the wall and you ask, trembling and ready, and you do not keep one.*

And she tried it. Once, there, before she had checked the room, because she was in a hurry — in the worst hurry of her life — and a harvester in a hurry is a harvester doing a *procedure*.

She put her palms flat and she asked: the four words, the naming, the counting, the whole of it done *correctly*, angles right, voice level, shoulders set, seventeen years of the best training in nine domes arriving at an inch of held time like a well-documented letter.

And the pull did not slacken. Not one degree. Nothing moved at all, except that eleven faces in the ring turned half an inch toward her, the way sleepers turn toward a sound they cannot place, and came back.

*Item — the first ask, made at the funnel of the harvest of Seren Vane, First Keeper, inside the one open second, by the finest harvester alive; it is on the record because she put it there herself, at a bedside, in the flat voice, two mornings later, in front of a boy who did not laugh: the ask came out* correct, *and correct is a* procedure, *and a procedure is a thing you do* to *a room, and there was nothing in that place that anything could be done to, because nothing in that place had a* floor.

She stood a long while with her hands on the rope her mother had been holding since the year Isolde was seven, and then she said the next thing out loud, in an empty cathedral of held time, to nobody, in a voice that had never once in her whole professional life been allowed to be heard. It cost her, runners, and this is the honest item, file it where the Kindling will read it at a bedside for two hundred years: it cost her more than the forty-one faces, more than the sledge, more than two hundred and thirty-nine jars going into strangers' hands at the crossroads — because what she lost in that second was the last thing she owned that was *useful*.

"I am doing it as an *officiant*. I am asking the way I would *take*. My hands are on the wall and my *face* is on the wall and my case is under my arm, and my case is empty, and *I* am the case, and there is not one thing in me that is not a *qualification*." And then, because the counting kind cannot stop, not even here, not even in front of her mother's hands, not even at the end of the whole trade: "That is why it did not slacken. A rite is a door. I have brought a *desk*."

She went down the ring of the eleven before she tried it again, because she was a Lampbearer and the trade trains you to check the room before the bed, and because walking is what you do with hands that have just been told they are no good.

Eleven stations, all the way round the thickness of that first night, at the angle of harvesters — bent, patient, both hands on nothing, faces turned to the work — and the work was *enormous*. She could see it now that she had stopped trying to read it. Every one of the eleven was pulling, still pulling, had been pulling for a thousand years, and what was coming down the funnel in front of each of them was not a life and not a name and not anything a ledger had a column for: it was *the day*. A field, going down a funnel, forty acres of it, with a fence in it and a bird over the fence that had been singing the same note since before the domes. A whole afternoon of weather, the kind nobody in nine domes had a word for because nobody in nine domes had stood in it. The smell of a street that lay under the Ninth's floor now. The color, somewhere in the third station, of a sky at the hour Isolde had only ever seen through the wrong end of a lamp.

They were burning it. That was what the twelve had done on the night the sun died: not a tithe, not a rent, not a coin — they had put *the memory of light*, which was nobody's because it was everybody's, into a lantern they had built to be *enough*, and the pulling had never finished, because a pull of a thing that belongs to everybody cannot be finished by twelve pairs of hands in one night, and the Court had spent a thousand years calling the arithmetic *growth*.

*Item — the mechanics, at last, in plain words, because nine domes have asked and the answer is not holy and is not complicated: a harvest that is* finished *goes into a lamp. A harvest that is* kept *goes into a wall, a jar, a pocket, a head. A harvest that is* never finished *goes nowhere, because there is no shelf in the trade for a pull with no end, and so it stays* happening *— in the one second that is still open, at the bottom of everything, warm as a pulse, patient as a woman at a bedside who has been handed the wrong paper and is waiting for somebody to bring the right one. Every unfinished harvest in nine domes is in here. There are eleven of them older than the Charter. There is one more, at the head of the ring, seventeen years behind the other eleven, with a cracked ceiling behind her and a small quick hand that wrote its own tag, and the Ledger calls her by the name of the seat she held in the lantern: hand of the twelve.*

And she laid a palm on each sleeve in turn and went to *read*, the way she had read since the cellar, and got nothing, and did not mind it, and understood in that minute exactly why: inside the glass there was nothing *to* read. Every shape in the place was still in the hands going down the funnel, mid-pull, and a mid-pull is not a keep, and a keep is the only thing a wall or a palm or a Court can hold. For the first time since the blue hour she stood in a room of other people's past with no advantage, no professional distance, no case, no margin, and no sentence anybody could print, and the *not-reading* was — runners, this is the honest item, file it — like taking a wet coat off in a warm house after seventeen years of weather.

Then she did the thing the rite has no motion for, and she did it eleven times, and it came out wrong every time, and she did it anyway.

"Thank you," she said, at the first station, to a sleeve, in the flat voice, level as a slate, like a woman reading an invoice aloud to a wall. "Thank you. For the field. I know it is not a *payment*. I know there is nothing you can do with it now. Thank you." At the second: "Thank you for the weather." At the third, in a voice that had stopped being level somewhere back around the second: "Thank you for the street. It is under my feet. I have walked on it four times a day for seventeen years and I did not *know*, and you did not get told, and nobody ever came to tell you, and I am sorry in the specific, which is the only kind of sorry with any *facts* in it."

The Unlit do not thank, they persist — a locked door said that to her in a cellar in the Ninth, in her first hour, and she had carried the sentence the whole road, and here she was at the end of the road inside the darkest place in the world saying the forbidden thing out loud to strangers a thousand years dead, and none of the eleven faces changed, and none of them needed to, and she understood by the eighth station what Mirelle had actually meant, which was not a prohibition but a *description*: the Unlit do not thank, because nobody has ever stood in front of them and *given them anything*, and a hand that has never been handed a thing does not know what to do with a *thanks*, and so it persists instead, which is the only motion left.

And at the twelfth station there were quick hands, clever hands, hair coming loose, a mouth open around half a word, and Isolde Vane did not put her palms on the funnel from outside it, the way you lay hands on a tool you have been told not to touch.

She stood in the seat behind her mother, and she put her own hands *over* her mother's, inside the hold, palm to knuckle, the way a seven-year-old stood at a rail once and was lifted up to it, and she took the weight of a thousand-year pull into her arms with eleven strangers in front of her and one nameless runner somewhere out in the black holding a jar she had sent there on purpose, and the weight was — runners, this is the item the Kindling will want most and the one she gave them last, because she had to think about it a whole winter before she would say it — the weight was *nothing*. Not light, exactly. *Ordinary*. The weight of a case on the way home from a ward, the weight of a family's whole year going down a funnel, the weight everybody in nine domes had been carrying since the first night with nobody ever once saying out loud what it weighed, and she said it, at the twelfth station, in the voice from the ward lists, unprofessional, low, going slightly uphill like a tag in a quick hand:

"Oh, Mother. *This* is what you were holding. You held this every day. You held this and you braided my hair the next room and you never once said it was *heavy*, and I have billed four hundred and eleven people for less than this and called it a *tithe*."

So Isolde Vane put her palms flat against the funnel her mother's hands had held for seventeen years, inside a second that was a thousand years old — *palm to palm, the last inch of the hold*, the same inch the locked door had closed on her wrist on the other side of the glass, and the thought of that, the sheer *outside-ness* of Mirelle's hand holding there through everything, was the trembling, and the ready was the same thing said the other way: *I am keeping you* — and she asked. Not the glass. Not the Vigil. The *rite*, which is older than all the people in it, the way a road is older than every traveler:

"Finish," she said. "*Down* it goes. Not into a lamp, not into a jar, not into my hands — *down*, through us, into the morning it was always too early for. Mother — *let go mid-word*. That is the only thing nobody ever did in this trade. Let go *before* you finish saying it, so the word is not yours and not mine and not the flame's, so the word goes *unspent* into the day and comes up *as* the day—

"— and I will say the rest of it *out there*, in the light, *for free*, where the Ledger can't read it."

And the pull *slackened* the second degree — and the twelve, all through the cathedral thickness of that one year-held second, felt it, the way a room full of sleepers feels the first bell of a morning they are not awake for yet: faces turned on a thousand invisible pillows, hands loosening on a thousand invisible funnels — and somewhere in the ring of them the *first hand*, the one whose place at the head of the eleven the Ledger had kept for nine hundred years, the one who had started the pull at the first night because it was *there* and someone was *cold*—

—and Seren Vane's hands came off the funnel, *mid-word*, at the *real*—

And the second opened, and went down.

Not fast. That was the thing the prophecy had gotten wrong in every version, and the thing Isolde reported afterward in the flat voice so the Kindling would not make it holy: *it went down the way a held breath goes down, all at once, everywhere, gently, because it had been held a thousand years and the body of the world was very, very tired*—

And inside the glass, in the new dark that was not dark, Isolde Vane stood with her mother's loose hands still warm around hers, and the last kept thing she had ever carried in her life — the jar over her heart, old Maren's sea and grass, forty years of *giving it freely because it is the only door left* — lifted off her chest in the leaning of the whole place going down, and she could have kept it. Here, at the very end of everything, no rite reaching, no flame pulling, no wall taking, she could have simply *held on*—

She thought of a cellar in the Ninth with forty-one faces and a slate. She thought of a woman in a coat saying *I am keeping you* at an inch of glass. She thought of a boy who had been born in the deep dark watching her do a thing on a stair and then making a knife out of it.

"*You're so good at giving things back*," she said, to her mother's hands, in the flat voice, at the very end of the whole trade, and she *opened her fingers*, and the sea went down, and the grass, and the gratitude, freely, unlabeled, unkept, the last wick-thread of the Last Lampbearer going into the dark *welcomed*—

And above her — *above*, in the only direction the word had ever really meant — the morning came up.

---

## Chapter 29 · The Wick Went Down Welcomed

The relighting has been described nine hundred times since, by people who were there and people who have read the wall-shapes, and the Court's own record — Crail's record, the honest one, begun that night at the little desk in the key-room — has the whole of it in one item, because there was no clerk awake enough for more:

*Item — at the hour the old calendar called the blue hour and the new one has not yet named, the glass of the world went bright from the inside. No cause assigned. The corps is advised the dome is not on fire. The dome is* ***day***.

But a dome is not where it happened. Here is the whole of it in the order the kindling can verify, palm to palm:

**First**, the wick. Two centuries of hoard, nine domes' *everything*, every wall-shape and jar and fourth-wedge keep, the whole labelled love of two generations and the unlabelled love of nine centuries of taken — all of it going *down* through the opened second, none of it burnt in anger, all of it *fed*, the way grain goes down a mill's throat, which is the only image Tam Ovre ever allowed for it, because the boy had watched the trade run his whole life from outside the warm hole and he said, at the end, in the smallest possible sentence: "*It was never a fire. It was a* ***mouth***. *Mouths have to eat something.*" And the eating was done. And the mouth—

**Second**, therefore, the mouth. The thin glass of the world did not shatter and did not open; the handmark came off it at the crossroads at noon, and what came *through* was not a flame and not a face and not a shepherd, though the sermon-voice kept its shape because four thousand people had been trained since birth to hear that voice and the day, being kind, *met them where they were*. What came through was *a morning*. Ninety million miles of *daylight* arriving at a small cold world the way morning arrives anywhere: slowly, and from the right direction this time, and not asking anyone whether it was *ready*, because every pair of hands in the crossroads had spent the night *being* the asking—

**Third**, the tubes. Nine domes, a thousand years of white wire, went dark at the noon turning — *all of them*, all at once, the way lamps go when the trimmer comes at the last of the oil with the good news that the oil was never the point — and in the Ninth, in the low rows, in the ward where the sick had always been placed under the brightest tube for *comfort*, the tube went dark, and then the glass *roof itself* went clear for the first time in the memory of anything living, and the light that came down onto the ward floor was *gold*, and old and new at once, and *free*, and a woman who had been dying of the cold all winter sat up in it and said, to no one, in the flat voice of somebody verifying a thing she had only read about: "*Warm.*"

**Fourth**, the walkers. This is the item the Ledger of the New Light could not bring itself to write in the *counts* language, and Crail, who had been a counting man his whole life, wrote it instead in the margin, in his own hand, above four hundred columns of arithmetic, in the only margin note the Corps ever let stand uncorrected:

*The procession stopped. Not at once. It is not a miracle if it is instant; the trade taught us that. It stopped the way a clock stops when somebody finally asks it to — first one, in the Sixth, at a service door, who turned off the road to look up at the gold and did not start walking again; then three, at the ribs of the Seventh, where the frost was thawing off the memorial lamps; then, by the evening bell, all of them. They sat down in the drifts wherever they were, like the ring at the crossroads, only* ***out***, *and the ones whose hands were held woke into their own coats, and the ones whose hands were not* — *the record cannot say this item and does not want to. The record says the ones whose hands were not were waited for. Kindness is not instant. Kindness was the whole point. — C.C., Lord Examiner pro tem, whose first act was this margin and whose last will be.*

**Fifth**, the deep camp. Ruen, number one hundred and thirty of the procession, who could not come home until somebody carried the shapes right, woke at the far end of the night at a table that was *there*, with her hands off it, in a camp of forty walkers who had begun to *remember each other's names out loud the way people do when the light is on and the rent is paid*, and the first name she said, before her own, before the camp's, before the year she was taken, was *Maren* — the sea, the grass, the first gratitude of the whole road, given back at the first word of the New Light's waking list — and old Maren's shape, which had gone *down* free through a daughter's fingers inside the glass, came *up* gold in every ward of the Ninth at once, the way tide comes in when the moon finally shows itself, and the two of them, which is to say *everyone*, learned the last mechanic of the trade that night with nobody's palms on anything:

*The shapes were never in the walls. The walls were where the shapes* ***waited***. *Ask a wall no more. It has carried enough.*

**Sixth** — and this item is in the record in the plainest hand Hask ever used, because some facts need to look level-eyed — the great lantern at the center of the First went out. Not starved. Not smashed. Went out the way Assur's lamp went out in the boiler hall, the way Corvin said a lamp should, *finished and fed and thanked*, and Vess Kel's Woken, the ones who had come to force the door of the world with a hammer, stood in the Lantern Chamber in the gold coming down through a hundred years of glass and watched the last Lampbearers of the First set their hands on the dark flame *not pulling*, and one of the young ones asked what happens now to us, and the oldest Lampbearer, who had trimmed that flame for forty years in the white and had never once been *thanked* by it, said the sentence that ended the profession:

"*Now we are only the people who were keeping lamps. Watch our hands.*"

And the seventh item is not in any record, and belongs here, in the walls of the kindling, where it is kept unlabeled:

At the crossroads, at the evening turning, when the gold had climbed all the way up the thin glass and the day was on the world proper for the first time in a thousand years — *proper*, no dome, no tube, no rent, no tithe, just *weather* — four thousand people stood in a ring around an inch of thawed door with their hands flat and their faces turned *inward*, because the morning had come up and the second was finished and the door was *still* mid-harvest from the outside, and the Vigil's sermon had said the hand that opens it must be the hand that was braided, and nobody in four thousand had said *and what closes it*. Nobody had to. The ring of the taken closed it first, one by one, palms off pillars, turning around into the *day*, leaning — the whole ring finally, only, *toward each other* — and the fourth circle stood up, all of them, in nine columns and two more, and *went home*. Walked out through the ribs and up the fourteen stairs and down the black straw, which was not straw anymore at all but a floor, a road, a *world*, into domes that were only *buildings* now, warm, gold, open, full of people making the sound of doors being thrown off a hundred years after they were built. And at the front of the Sixth's column, a woman with her mother's hands still held at her side like a rope finally paid its last link, saying, low, personal, *spent*, over and over in the rhythm of walking, to no one, to the wall-shapes, to the day, the one line Kindling runners would recite from that night forward without ever quite agreeing who said it first:

"*You're so good at giving things back. You're so good at giving things back. You're so good at giving things back.*"

**Eighth**, and it is in no ledger because the only person it is about cannot read and the only other person present would not have known how to write a child: the deep let the boy go.

He came up the fourteen stairs on the evening bell with his sledge empty and his eleven-roll over one shoulder and the twelfth knife in his fist, because that is how you come up fourteen stairs, and behind him, in the throat of the stair, the ring of the taken came up out of the chasm the way they always had — hundreds deep, patient, single-file, facing the wrong way for a thousand years and now, at last, facing the right one — and they did not stop when he stopped, and they did not close on him, and the last of them, the woman with the rotted seal of the Sixth on her collar who had taken his hand in the dark of the fall and held it the way you hold a rail, came level with him on the eleventh stair and put her palm flat against the back of his neck, one second, which is not a hold and is not a harvest and is, runners, as near as the taken have ever come to a goodbye, and then she walked past him and down toward the crossroads with everybody else, and the stair was empty, and Tam Ovre stood on it in a coat the color of nothing with his hand up behind his neck and understood the whole of his thirteenth year in one plain, terrible, ordinary sentence: *nobody is keeping me anymore, and I have to walk home by myself like a* ***person***.

He was not crying, and it took him until the fourth day to manage that, and I am telling you the order of the items honestly because this is a book about counting and the counting is the emotion.

What he found at the crossroads was the cat lamp. It stood in its niche by the thawed inch where the Kindling had kept it twenty years, lit, because the ring would not let it go out, and he had tried, and every year of the memorial the whole Ashlands had leaned toward that small blue flame and said *there she is* without ever once saying what comes next. And now the day was coming down through the thin gold glass in a whole column of it, and the flame was unnecessary, and unnecessary, out here, does not mean *worthless*; it means *finished*.

Tam stood in front of it a long time with his knives in his lap, and then he did the thing he had been the only soul alive permitted to do, and he did it wrong on purpose, the way you do a thing wrong on purpose when nobody taught you and the doing is the point: he trimmed it *down*, wick and all, gentle, patient, the way a tall woman had once done it in a warm hole under the drifts with a boy holding the stopper knife for her, and the little flame went lower and lower and did not fight him, and when it was a thread, and a spark, and nothing, the crossroads was very quiet, and the boy said, aloud, to nobody, in the small tired voice of a thirteen-year-old explaining a thing he has been hoping his whole life nobody would ever make him say: "Right. That's the one I'm *jar-ring*."

He had no jar. There was nothing left to jar; every jar in the trade had gone into the hands of strangers, and the dark does not keep what light spends anymore because the light is not spending it, and so he put his two hands flat on the cold niche where the lamp had been, and pressed, and asked, and got — the Kindling tell this part with the wall-shape and the wall-shape will show you his face doing it, runners, and there is not one harvester's motion in it, not one pull, just a boy with his palms on a stone — and the niche gave it up *willingly*, because it had been kept by a thousand listening walls for two generations with nobody's permission on it and nobody's price: a warm hole under the drifts, a sledge, a lamp no bigger than a plum, a woman with a service-tube seal in one fist and a stopper knife on the other knee, saying *the drained cannot take what was never given, and you, little scavenger, were never anyone's*, and Isolde Vane — who came up the last of the stair behind him and stood at the rail with nothing in her hands and nothing left in her fingertips — could not have told you what it was, and did not need to, because Mirelle Dax was already there, already down on her knees in the gold in the middle of four thousand people going home, already taking a thirteen-year-old's shaped-and-labelled *entire family* out of the stone into the air with her scarred hands, and handing it to him, *palm to palm*, giving, the third motion, free, no price, no counter-sign, in the voice of a locked door with nothing left on the other side of it to be locked against:

"There. It was always yours. I am not *reading* it to you, kid, I am *bringing* it to you, and if anybody in nine domes ever asks you who kept your mother, you say the Kindling, because that is the whole name of the trade we are going to have to invent, and it starts with you being *told* instead of taken."

And Hask came up behind them out of the tail of the column with the muster-slate under his arm, and made an entry that night in the Ledger of the Free that the Corps has never once been permitted to correct, because it is the first line in the history of nine domes in which a child is counted as a person instead of a tithe to be arranged:

*Item — OVRE, T., age thirteen, Kindling, unharvested, harvest-proof (see* laws of the taken, *which the New Light will have to write down sometime, because the Court never did and it took a boy to teach the world the eleven jobs). One (1) keeper of the deep ring's release. One (1) knife, twelfth, no mark, worn at one spot, property of himself. Entered freely, sixth column, at his own request, and the man who wrote it asked him twice whether he wanted his name in a book on purpose, because a free signature is a thing you do not paper over, and the boy answered the first time with an insult, and the second time with yes, and the third — because he called him back to say it again — with yes, and write the thirteen too, since nobody ever has.*

And at the tail of that hour, because out here a promise is a schedule and not a feeling, the boy stopped the front row of the ring where it went past him at the crossroads.

"Four," Tam Ovre said, to the man who had carried a dead woman's jar up fourteen stairs without knowing he had done it, with the whole ring waiting past him patient as weather. "I haven't got your better name. The books with the names in them are ash, and I will not *guess* you into somebody else's, and you will not have a lie off me. So you keep *Four*, and I am going to ask every soul that comes up out of the deep what their mother called you, every day, for the rest of my life, and when I find it I will come and *read* it to you until you know it yourself; and if I find it the day after I am old, you will still have spent the whole of it with somebody looking, and that is the only thing the Kindling have got instead of a church."

The man whose name was Four until somebody better turned up shook his head, which is yes; and the ring behind him shook its head, four hundred deep, which is the loudest amen this book has room for.

And at the back of the column, coming up the road *out*, through the thawed shape of a door that closed gently behind her the way a hand closes gently over a finished page — *palm to palm, the last inch of the hold* at last let go, both sides free — came Isolde Vane, twenty-four years old, the same hour the morning did, empty-handed, empty-pockets, empty *kept*, the last Lampbearer of the whole trade, walking into the gold of a world that had not one shape of her in it, blinking, *trembling*, and — *ready*.

---

## Chapter 30 · The Small Thing

She came back through the same door, because she had been told to, and because the road had ended in the only promise it had ever really kept: *come back, and I will be the keeping*.
