The crossroads took an hour to empty around her, and she let it, standing in the gold at the foot of the thin glass while the four circles went home in their columns, because — the flat voice, her own now, always her own — *a person who has given everything is not owed a scene; she is owed a minute*. And the minute she stood was the last minute the book of the trade had, and in it Isolde Vane did the audit of herself that Corvin had taught her to do at a landing with a dead man's coat on her shoulders, and found, itemized, exactly what a Lampbearer costs:

*Item — the mother's face: given* (seventh dome, one held breath and a half, on purpose, at a wall).

*Item — one (1) leaf, picked up off a cellar floor for nothing: given* (blue hour, first dark, pressed flat to a flame-bracket at the door of the sun; the only item she owned that no one had ever been paid for, and therefore the only one her hands could let go of).

*Item — the road, the monk's pear, the scriptor's ink-stain, two hundred and thirty-nine (239) jar-shapes of a carried life: given* (crossroads, all palms, whole chest, at the fortieth without any lesson, one jar excepted, and the exception seen by every soul present and written down anyway).

*Item — one (1) coat that would not open, at the greatest transaction in the history of the world, in front of nine domes: filed under* not yet *, which is the only column in this ledger that has ever moved on its own.*

*Item — Maren's gratitude, last kept, unlabeled at the end: given* (inside the glass, finger by finger, on purpose, at a mother's loose hands).

*Item — the reading, the gift, the ninety generations, the whole profession in the fingertips: spent* (blue hour, first dark, at a flame-bracket, unsaid for two days and unpitied since, because a kindling who is *asked* at a pillar does not need a Lampbearer to read it to her, and the counting kind will not be made to say *that is the part I mourned*).

*Item — the seventh year, the smallest self, the one shape of her own that ever came back: read at a stranger's wall in the same stroke, given straight back, unkept, untold all winter, and told at the rail before the day was an hour old — see* ***margin, third***.

*Item — the voice in the fourth wedge: the rite cannot reach hands; kept, unsought, unspendable, and* ***not*** *a shape — it is a* ***practice***, *which is the only thing this trade ever failed to put a price on, because you cannot drain a* ***verb***.

*Item — one (1) census column, Court hand, uncounter-signed, unforged, reading simply* asked *(the late Assessor, being the last officer of the trade, entering it in the year's book at a key-room desk in the company of a dying man, and never afterward explaining it, which is the single line the Court ever wrote about Isolde Vane that came out true)*.

And the audit closed level-eyed, because the counting kind cannot stop and because it was, finally, *balanced*: she had spent nine domes' everything and her own everything into the same dark, and the dark had answered with a *morning*, which was not a return on the investment, which was not anything *for* anything, which was — the word she had let go mid-pull inside the glass so the day could come up as the word itself — *real*.

So she stood in the gold with nothing kept, and nothing in the whole world *keeping* her, and Mirelle Dax's wall-shape of her *eight years deep* in the Sixth, safe where a census cannot reach, and she thought — not in words, words had all been spent; in the lean — *this is the price. This is exactly the price and I said yes to it out loud at a bedside in the amber and the yes stands. I will be kept or I will not be. The day does not owe me my mother's face. The day was the day. That was the whole trade, that it could* ***just be the day***—

"— you have the look," said a voice, "of a person who opened something and is going to *keep* it."

Mirelle came back. Of course she came back; the Kindling do not leave a door mid-harvest, hers least of all. She came up the empty crossroads alone, sleeves down, her hands *empty*, her neck's scar gold-lit, having sent her mother's column home with Vess Kel's Woken — *an argument does not end when it is won; it ends when it is spent; Vess Kel had spent hers on the Lantern Chamber stair, weeping, trimming a dead flame like a woman learning to pray in the wrong religion* — and her mother, DRAINED-NAMED-NO-ONE, in the Sixth, at a service door, *asking* her hollowed hands one name at a time from the neighbors, with eight years of a daughter's wall-shapes to read back when she was ready, which is to say *by lamplight, which is to say slowly, which is to say like kindness*.

"I have the look," Isolde agreed, in the flat voice, and then, because the flat voice had never once been a *lie* voice either: "I have nothing, locked door. Not the face. Not the sea. If there is a wall in the Sixth with my hands in it, that is the whole of me on this side of the glass — and I cannot *check*, runners, I have been standing in the thinnest place in the world for an hour with my palms on the glass of it and I cannot tell you whether there is one shape of me in there, because the thing that used to go *looking* went *through* with the hum at the blue hour and did not come back, and I have not said that sentence out loud since the night I lost it, and I am saying it now, in the gold, at the last minute of the last night of the whole trade, so that you will know the arithmetic is *finished* and not *polite*: I spent the gift with the year. I gave the face. I gave the sea. I have *the voice*, and a voice is a *verb*, and a verb needs—"

And Mirelle Dax, eight years of a mother in a wall, one spent daughter of the arithmetic of mercy, did the thing that the *whole of the trade* had never managed to invent despite owning every mechanic required for it, having learned it, as she learned everything, from *her*:

She reached into her coat and handed Isolde a jar.

It was unlabeled. It had no keep, no claim, no census mark, no Executor's line under it, and it was the smallest glass Hask had ever pressed for personal use, the kind an Unlit child carries a *single* kept shape in, a marble's worth — and inside it, curled at the bottom like a sleeping cat, was a shape everyone in nine domes had *always* been able to read and *no one* had ever been able to buy, because it had never entered a tithe-house: it was the cellar shape, the deep one, the Kindling's oldest unlabeled keep — *a Lampbearer's hands in a low row of the Ninth, twenty years ago, trimming an Unlit fire for nothing, telling a scarred sixteen-year-old* nobody goes out around me, not one shape of you, *and meaning it, and being* ***meaning it*** *the way only a practice can, the verb before there was a name for it* — and Mirelle said, in the voice, level, ledger-flat, itemized, final:

"Entry one of the Ledger of the Free: *one shape, given, no counter-sign, no wedge, no debt.* Don't read it yet — you *can't*, and I have known since the stair that you can't, and I have never once in my whole life said the word *pity* to your face and I am not going to start now, so here is the honest version, Lampbearer: *nobody in this dome can read you the way a Lampbearer reads*, and the walls of the Sixth have your hands in them and your own hands will not answer you now, and the kindling reading will give you a shape and not a *weight*, and you will have to live on the *weight*: *you cannot check yourself anymore*, and there is not one person left in nine domes with the profession that could tell you whether what they see in you is *true*. So you will have to have it *told*. That is what a *kept* person is; that has always been what one is; the whole trade ran nine hundred years on people who would not *admit* they needed it, and you are the first one in the history of the domes who gets to find out with the *good* ones around her. *I* will read you the walls. *Hask* will read you the slate. The *boy* will read you the knife, when he has had enough years to. And *this* one you do not read, you *hold*, and when you are ready I will say it out loud, and *I will say it properly, because that is what the day is for* — " and the locked door opened, on its true hinge, all the way, at last, without being asked— "— *so this one is from me, and it is small on purpose, because you taught the whole world at a bedside in the amber that the big ones were the* ***lie***. *The small ones were the trade's real currency all along. You just finally got rich enough to spend the price off.*"

And Isolde Vane, the Last Lampbearer, empty-handed in the gold, took the small thing.

And there was one more item at the rail that hour — the smallest in a chapter full of small ones, and a promise made in a boiler hall one whole road ago, now collectable at the end of the trade by exactly one person, who intended to collect it in public. Hask had brought the first page of the new list up from the sledges in a two-handled box — the book that will become the Ledger of the Free is, at this minute, one line long, written on the back of a census sheet with a knife-point dipped in soot, and will take the Sixth's column one whole winter to grow to nine pages — and the ring still going past the rail stopped and listened while the quartermaster of the New Light read the head of it aloud in the flat exhausted voice, because it is the first page in the history of nine domes that lists only what was *given*.

And when he got to the second line Isolde put out her hand, and he gave her the page, and she read it herself, aloud, in the flat voice turned personal, to whatever was left of four thousand people going home in the gold:

"*Dax, M. — Sixth column — given, not taken — free of the rite, free of the rent, free of the whole arithmetic, this night and after.*"

And she handed the page back, and said, to nobody in particular and to everybody in the ring, "I promised a woman in a boiler hall that if I ever stood at a rail and gave a name for somebody, I would give *hers* first, and I had no idea at the time that I would be able to keep it, because I had no idea what a rail was for, and it turns out a rail is for exactly that, and I would like the quartermaster to note that it was made by a Lampbearer with four free readings on her hands and no idea at all what she was promising, and it has cost nothing whatever, which is the only kind of promise that has ever survived a census." And the locked door, at the other end of the rail, with her mother's loose hands still held at her side like a rope paid out to the end of its own length, did not say anything at all, and did not look up, and put her scarred palm flat on the frost along the rail in the manner of the Kindling answering a name that has been called across a very long road.

And Mirelle Dax told the story of it at the rail, because somebody had to, and because the counting kind is worst at telling anyone and there is no rule in nine domes that says the same is true of the locked door. She told it to Hask, and Tam, and the boy with the eleven knives, and the returned walkers who had come back for their jars, in the flat ledger voice, at the crossroads rail where a knife was a bridge once — the *seventh year*, the *stranger's wall*, the *one held breath*, the *gave it back and did not say a word* — and she told it, runners, in the open, at the last hour of the trade, in front of the one person in the world it was about, which is the only way any of us have ever been loved properly and the only way the Kindling ever intended to be: *read aloud, at a bedside, with you in the room, trembling, ready, and not one shape of you kept*.

And Isolde Vane, who had lost her mother's face and her own seventh year and the gift in her fingertips in the space of one winter, sat at the rail of the crossroads in the gold with a small unlabeled jar in her two hands, and heard it, and *did not correct a single item*, and said — in the voice, her own, always her own, level, and, for the first time in the whole book, *not flat*:

"*That is accurate.*"

And then Hask, whom nobody had asked anything at all, walked up out of the column's tail with a slate under his arm and the *keeps* box in both hands, and did the last piece of quartermaster's work in the history of the Kindling: he squatted in the gold, opened the box, took out one folded margin page, and read it aloud in the flat exhausted voice, exactly as filed, to Isolde Vane, in front of the thin glass.

*Item — one (1) street-name, Vane, Isolde, age seven, lost to the carrying; filed as column property; to be read back to the owner at the blue hour, or whenever she asks, or at her funeral, whichever comes first.*

"I cannot give it you," Hask said, when he had finished, because Hask had never in eleven years of slates said a word he did not have a column for. "It went *down* with the rest, it is *morning* now, I have looked at every wall in six domes and the reading is gone out of them or gone *into* them and I cannot tell which and I do not have the gift. But the *label* held. That is the item, Lampbearer, that is the whole of my report: you wrote it down, and because you wrote it down it went into the glass *with an owner on it*, and the arithmetic of mercy has run nine hundred years and *never once* mislaid a thing that had a name on the tag — so you will not *remember* your street, and I am not going to *pretend* at you in the gold, but somewhere in the New Light there is a bell, or a tin shop, or a particular way the cold comes off a roof at the hour the day wakes, and it is *yours*, and it is *labelled*, and it will find you the way the lean finds the glass, which is *patiently, and with the room already prepared*."

And Isolde Vane sat in the gold of the first real morning with a margin page in her hands and no memory of the street it named, and put the page back in the box, and stood up, and some weeks afterward, in a low row of the Sixth, woke at the hour the day comes up and heard a sound she could not place and did not try to place, and asked nobody what it was, because she had finally learned what a label is for.

That is all the book has left to report, except three margins, kept here because the kindling read palms and not pages and the palms deserve the plain words:

*The first margin*: the domes were not opened that year and not the next; the day came up gentle, and slow, and from the wrong direction for a hundred winters, because a thousand years of cold does not turn in a season and the morning has never claimed otherwise — but the *tubes* stayed dark, and the *rent* stopped, and the memory of the trade is now kept the honest two ways: the walls keep their shapes for whoever asks, and the Ledger of the Free lists only what was *given*, which runs to nine pages by Hask's first count and took Mirelle's own column one whole winter to write down. Crail's Corps audits it every census. The countersigns are real now. Under Corvin Aelfric's memorial — *not a lamp; the Kindling refused the lamp; a plain stone at the ribs of the Seventh with the eleven rooms and the four hundred and twelve cut into it, and one line in his own flat hand copied off the muster-slate* — *It was never the fondness. It was the only—*, Brother Assur's old order sits, and does not pray, and trims nothing, and is *thankful*.

*The second margin*: Tam Ovre has eleven knives and one cat, and the twelfth — *Seren's* twelfth, the share of a boy's story that a mother's hands made before a daughter's hands finished — is laid flat across the rail of the crossroads where a knife was a bridge once, unlabeled, unkept, open to anyone who passes with empty enough hands to read it. The boy himself goes where the tide cannot take him, which is everywhere now, which has always meant *everywhere*, which is the whole of the New Light's geography in one line for the children who will not need to grow up inside it.

*The third margin*: Isolde Vane does not get the face back, and does not get the seventh year back either, though it came to her in a stranger's palm at the blue hour and she gave it away in the same breath. That is the true thing, and the trade's one honest inheritance: what is spent is spent, and the morning never promised *returns*, only *day* — but what the morning *does* invent, and this is the whole invention of the New Light in one line, is that a thing you cannot keep may still be *told*: at a rail, in the gold, in a flat ledger voice, in front of you, by the person who has the years and the voice and no shame about either. But in the low rows of the Ninth, in a warm ward under a *gold* window, a woman who sits up in it some winters when her cold is kind says to whoever is closest, in the flat voice of somebody verifying a thing she has only read about, *watch your hands, little door* — and the Kindling do not call this a shape, or a keep, or a haunting; they call it what Isolde herself called it at the crossroads at the end of everything, blinking, trembling, ready, holding one small free jar up into the morning like a lantern that had finally been *let down*:

"*A practice. Which is to say —* ***mine***. *Which is to say —* ***given***. *Which is to say the trade is over, runners. Watch the hands.*"

---

# EPILOGUE · The Morning

There is a book about a woman who harvested the dying.

It is kept in no library; the paper would be a jar, and the jar would be a wall, and the wall would have to be *asked*, so — *asked*: put your palms flat and read it in any keep in nine domes, the whole road in it, sea-gratitude and leaf and knife, and the shape at the center of it that the Kindling label only as *Lampbearer*, and the shape at the center of *that* which they label only as *daughter*, and the last line, which the walls keep the way hands keep a verb:

The sun died a thousand years ago. It came back the only way a thing comes back when it has been *eaten* — by being *given*, one small free shape at a time, by people who had finally been taught, at the end of the whole dark arithmetic, what it had cost and what it was worth and which of those two numbers *matters*:

*Not one shape lost. Not one shape spent. Not one shape kept that could be let go.*

*The morning is not a reward. The morning is what is left when the trade is done.*

*Watch your hands.*

**THE END**


---
---

# GLOSSARY OF THE TRADE

*Kept in the plain words, because the Kindling read palms and the Court read pages, and this book is meant to survive both.*

**ASK** — To open a wall, jar, or lamp with flat palms, trembling and ready, and receive what the shape gives without holding anything back. The opposite of pull. The only verb the New Light uses.

**THE ARITHMETIC OF MERCY** — The Kindling name for the sum every ask comes to: *who pays, how much, and is it in the record*. It has four columns and one of them is always a person. Mirelle Dax taught it to recruits by refusing to tell them what it was for; Isolde Vane learned it on a road; Assessor Crail, at the end, was the only officer of the trade who ever wrote his own number into it and left the column honest.

**BRAIDING** — The rite's name for binding a harvest into a keeper's hands. Isolde was *braided* at seven; the hand that was braided is the only hand that can open the door.

**CENSUS** — The counting of a dome's people and keeps. Held at the blue hour. Under the trade, it took; under the New Light, it lists.

**COUNTER-LEAN** — The pull of a drained thing back toward the thin glass. Long mistaken for the Vigil returning what it had taken. It was the Vigil *practicing*.

**DRAINED** — Emptyed by rite beyond the point of waking. The walkers. The procession. Under the New Light: *the taken*, and then, at last, *the hands*.

**FOURTH WEDGE** — The sealed section of a Lampbearer's keeping-case, reserved for a shape too dangerous to label. Isolde's held her mother's voice, which is in the hands, which is where the rite cannot reach.

**FOURTH CIRCLE** — In the plan: the taken themselves, the hands that cannot wake but can hold. The discovery of the book's last movement: they were never the fuel. They had been the hold, all along.

**HARVEST** — The rite of taking a dying person's memories, palm to funnel to lamp. *Harvest reversed*: asking at the wall, carrying everything, keeping nothing, spending what you carry.

**THE LEDGER** — The wall of the First Dome that keeps every claim, countersign, and *Executor* line of the trade. The Lantern Ledger recorded: *Seren Vane, First Keeper.* The Ledger of the *Free*, begun on the night of the blue hour, lists only what was given, with no counter-sign.

**LEAN, THE** — The direction all kept things point while the trade runs: toward the Vigil. Walls, jars, lamps, flames, hands. When the shapes were freed, the ring of the taken finally leaned *toward each other*.

**MEMORY** — Under the trade: currency, fuel, and sin at once, because the same substance paid rent, fed the lamps, and was confessed as sin — and the trade kept its books on all three ledgers simultaneously.

**MIRELLE'S LAW** — "*Nothing goes out around me.*" Said out loud in a boiler hall in the Ninth, in the same breath in which she gave her own name back to herself; the law of a woman whose mother sold her shape when she was sixteen and who has spent twenty years making certain that nothing leaves her without being *asked for*; kept for eight years of road; verified by the whole book.

**NOT YET** — The one word on the tag of Seren Vane's harvest: a sixty-year-old Kindling keep, resealed nine times, packed by its own keeper's quick hand and sent out into the dark in a runner's coat before they came for her; the unfinished pull standing inside a second a thousand years old, warm as a pulse; and the only honest label the trade ever printed on grief, which is that a thing is not *gone*, it is *not yet*.

**THE PROCESSION** — The long road of the walkers between the domes, pacing toward the bottom of the world. Stopped on the morning after the blue hour, first one, then three, then all, "*the way a clock stops when somebody finally asks it to*."

**PULL** — The old verb: bedside, funnel, pull, lamp, flame, spending. Push makes wick. Pull makes shapes. Only *ask* makes morning.

**READING** — The Lampbearer's gift: palm to wall, and the shape comes up into the reader as the dead woman's sea, the pear, the ordinations. Costs a person nothing when it is done *to* a dome and *everything* when it is done *through* one; Isolde Vane spent hers in the bell-stroke of the blue hour, and it is the reason this book ends with other people reading *her*.

**SHAPE** — What a wall, a jar, or a pair of hands keeps of a memory: sea-water, grass, ordinations, pears, gratitudes. Read palm to surface. Not the memory itself — the memory's *wait*, in a place.

**SOULFLAME** — A lamp fed with harvested human memory. Starving ones lean. Finished, fed, and thanked ones go out the only way a lamp should.

**TARIFF** — What a memory was worth under the trade, told to the seller afterward, in a room the seller would never be in: a day, three days of oil; a face, a winter of roof; a wedding, a season, and the hall takes the dance; a trade, two months; a song, one; the shape of you, a year in full, and it is the cheapest thing we own. Old Maren made a Lampbearer read it out aloud at a dying bed, and the reading started a road. The New Light keeps one column, headed **given**, and the clerks have found they cannot keep it empty.

**TITHE-HOUSE** — Where a dome paid the trade: the year at census, everything at the Grand Tithe. Its empty rooms are now free keeps.

**THE TRADE** — The compact older than the Court: memory to the lamps, lamps to the Vigil, the Vigil to the day that was eaten a thousand years ago. Over on the morning after the blue hour, at a crossroads under the ribs of the world, by four thousand hands doing the arithmetic of mercy.

**THE VIGIL** — What came in at the first night as a shepherd, a sermon, and a thousand borrowed voices: the eaten sun-day, not evil, *farmed* — a nearly-wake, kind for a thousand years and *nearly*, whose whole demand at the end was one finished rite and a held breath going down.

**WICK** — Hoarded memory held in anger, fit to burn. The Court counted only the year as tithe and could not see the *everything*; the Kindling emptied the *everything* into the glass themselves, welcomed, and the trade's last arithmetic came out with not one shape burnt in anger.

---

# A LAST LAMPBEARER ALMANAC

*The roster of the people whose hands are in these walls, filed in the decent language, for whoever reads the palms after us.*

**ISOLDE VANNE** — no. Correction, in the flat voice, as she herself filed it: **ISOLDE VANE**. Lampbearer of the Ninth, exiled, braided, carrier, ask-er, door. The Last Lampbearer of the whole trade and the first person in a thousand years to be *kept by a promise instead of a ledger*. She does not get her mother's face back, and she does not get her *reading* back — the gift went through her with the nine-dome hum at the blue hour, uncomplained of for two days, and nobody in nine domes has the art to tell her what she used to be. She gets one small free jar, and she calls it *mine*, which is the same as calling it *given*, and she has to take Mirelle Dax's *word*, and a kindling's slow reading, for what is in it and for what she used to be, which is the last, smallest, and hardest thing the trade ever asked of her.

**SEREN VANE** — First Keeper of the Lantern, hand of the twelve, who proved the flame could be returned; mother; the mid-word. Her harvest went *down* on the morning after the crossroads, finished by her daughter *mid-pull, on purpose*, and the word she was saying came up as the day. Her voice lives in a pair of hands, where the rite cannot reach.

**CORVIN AELFRIC** — Lord Examiner, late. Twenty-two years of written *none*; eleven rooms, four hundred and twelve souls; one forgery of honor, declined; one promise made on the black straw (*keep your hands off my brass*), and it is the only promise in this book that both parties kept; one junction, opened with his own two hands and spent. Buried at the ribs of the Seventh under a plain stone, no lamp, one line: *It was never the fondness. It was the only—*. The Kindling consider the dash a complete sentence.

**MIRELLE DAX** — Leader of the Kindling; the locked door; the scarred cellar-woman of the Ninth, aged sixteen, on the night she burned a ledger-room and took nothing on the way back. Her mother, DRAINED-NAMED-NO-ONE, woke by degrees on the morning after, asking names one at a time at a service door in the Sixth, which is to say *by lamplight, which is to say slowly, which is to say like kindness*. Mirelle authored entry one of the Ledger of the Free and has never once been caught trimming a fire so her hands stop shaking.

**TAM OVRE** — Thirteen. Born in the deep dark; harvest-proof; eleven knives and one cat; maker, at the last, of a bridge out of the twelfth. His sentence of the century: "*It was never a fire. It was a mouth. Mouths have to eat something.*" He goes where the tide cannot take him, which now means *everywhere*.

**BROTHER ASSUR** — Monk of the Order of the Book; the fake death that opened the whole road; giver of the glass-leaf letter, burned by the Kindling in a floor-lamp in the under-ice city, which is where he died a walker, at a table, having declined a bed for eleven years and finally taken the long one. His old order sits now at Corvin's stone, does not pray, trims nothing, and is thankful.

**HASK** — Quartermaster of the New Light; the counting kind, unarmored; held the muster-slate through the blue hour, the paperwork of honor, and the relighting, all in the flat exhausted voice, and invented the *personal* inventory. His list of what the walkers need — *names, years, roofs* — is the first page of every free keep in nine domes.

**VESS KEL** — Captain of the Woken; hammered for a forced waking, stood at the glass instead, and spent her argument on the Lantern Chamber stair, weeping, trimming a dead flame like a woman learning to pray in the wrong religion. The Woken now hold the outer ring of every rite in the New Light, which is to say: they *stand*. It was always what they wanted. It was never what they were offered.

**ASSESSOR CRAIL** — Lord Examiner pro tem; promoted man; calendar-cutter; the first signature of the Ledger of the New Light, in the real voice, and never once forged afterward. He stood at the thin glass with everybody else, trembled, and was *not* ready, and asked the record to note the difference between those two words for the use of whoever comes after. The record notes it.

**OLD MAREN** — Died in a ward of the Ninth on the first night of this book, mid-harvest, with a sea and a grass field in her hands; the gratitude went freely, forty years early, into a jar over a daughter's heart; went *down* on the last night of this book, unlabeled, welcomed, the last wick-thread of the Last Lampbearer. The sea came back gold. The grass came back as morning. It was the only miracle anyone actually needed.

**HARB** — The old clerk on the desk of the Ninth's tithe-house; the first officer of the trade in nine domes to say *when* instead of *if*; kept his pension light and his ledger lighter and got what he had only read about in the end, which is a *gold window*. The Kindling rate him the first of the eleven rooms' worth of *sleepers who were never woken because they never sleep*, in the sense that he was awake the whole time and simply could not afford, at sixty, to say so in writing. He said it in writing now. It runs to nine pages in the Ledger of the Free and he countersigns every one.

**RUEN** — Walker, number one hundred and thirty; the girl of about sixteen who came up out of the day with her hands folded in the posture of a guest at a table that was no longer there, because the dream had kept the first thing she had ever been *given* — until the morning after, when the table was *there* and her hands were off it, and the first name she said was *Maren*. She spoke the first name before her own, which is the last mechanic of the trade, and it goes: *the shapes were never in the walls; the walls were where the shapes waited.*

**BLUE** — A child of the Ninth's low rows, met in a boiler hall at the beginning of the road, where she corrected her mother twice in five minutes and was called nothing at all. She brought a pot of soup to the crossroads on the last night, gave it to the Lampbearer for no reason whatever — *it isn't for you, it's because you're here, and it's hot* — and became, at the end of the same hour, the first entry Isolde Vane ever wrote in her own hand that was a gift instead of a loss: **Blue**, with a big B, which is the home name and is not on any schedule.

**FOUR** — The runner who took Seren Vane's packed keep out of the Seventh seventeen years ago and came back up fourteen stairs as a walker, not knowing. Unnamed by nine censuses, labelled by a thirteen-year-old with a stub of charcoal at the crossroads: *Four, of the crossroads; trade, carrying; keep, the front row.* The better name is being looked for still, every day, by somebody who intends to keep asking, which the Kindling consider a promise and the domes would have filed as *not applicable*.

**THE TAKEN / THE WALKERS / THE DRAINED** — Names in order of use, worst first. The front row of the ring at the glass; the fourth circle; the hands that had been finishing each other's harvests on the long road for a thousand years with their palms, unrecorded, unasked, *practicing the hold for the use of a door none of them could see*. Read them off the nearest pillar. They will not need you to, but go anyway.
